--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface (Vorwort) where the author, Gustav Theodor Fechner, establishes a metaphorical framework for his philosophical mission. He compares his sequence of published works to the repeated attempts to wake a sleeping roommate.</meta>

# Preface.

As a student, I had a <term>roommate</term><note>The original German *Stubenbursche* refers to a fellow student sharing a room, a common arrangement in 19th-century German universities where one student might also perform minor services for another.</note> who could not easily find his way out of bed. Once, when it was important to me, I achieved it in the following way. Every five minutes, I called out the same word to him: "Get up!" The first time, it had no success at all; the second time he said, "Leave me in peace!" The third time: "Nothing you do will help!" The fourth time he was silent, but he was seething; the fifth time he began to storm and curse; the sixth time he cried out, "It is unbearable!" The seventh time he really could not bear it any longer, sprang out of bed to fall upon me; yet his anger soon vanished, as he was now glad himself to be out of bed, and he did not lie down again.

So have I spoken for a first time to a public that cannot find its way out of the bed of old views, with the *Little Book of Life After Death*: "Get up!"; I spoke a second time with *Nanna*: "Get up!"; I spoke a third time with the *Zend-Avesta*: "Get up!" I spoke a fourth time with the *Moon-book*: "Get up!" I speak now for a fifth time: "Get up!" and, if I live, I shall call out "Get up!" a sixth and seventh time, and it will always be the same "Get up!"

Now, I do not mean to suggest that I could force the "getting up" by calling ever so frequently, if the time for getting up were not truly here; but the getting up must come soon, even if I do not call at all; at most, I can do something through my call to accelerate it, and I do what I can in this regard. To the call that is meant to awaken a sleeping world belongs a strong breath; I am but a single breath in this respiration.

<summary>In this preface, Gustav Theodor Fechner uses a humorous anecdote about a sleepy roommate to illustrate his persistent efforts to "awaken" the public from traditional materialism through his philosophical writings.</summary>
<keywords>Gustav Theodor Fechner, Preface, panpsychism, philosophy of mind, Little Book of Life After Death, Nanna, Zend-Avesta, Moon-book, awakening, German idealism</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s preface. Having established his "Get up!" metaphor for awakening the public to a new spiritual-scientific worldview, he now addresses the dominant mid-19th-century trends of industrialization and materialism, arguing that these "soulless" developments will actually create the "hunger" for the spiritual philosophy he is proposing.</meta>

People believe that the way machines are increasingly replacing life, and railways and telegraphs are covering the earth, indicates a direction entirely opposite to the spirit of the times. And indeed, if things continue this way, only one of two outcomes is possible: either all life on earth will be submerged in machines, or all machines will eventually be absorbed into a life of the earth <note>Fechner refers here to his signature "Day-view," the belief that the Earth is a living, conscious organism, and that technology is merely an extension of its organic life.</note>. Since the former is impossible, only the latter can occur; thus, the current tendency of the era is precisely what best accommodates and assists the tendency represented by this work. One must first become hungry before reaching for bread.

People believe that the one-sidedness of materialism gaining so much ground indicates an entirely opposite tendency of the age. Yet the fact that people now find the only means of salvation from sinking in the coarsest timber from the wrecked ship of philosophy proves that a new ship and a new voyage are necessary.

Is the wind the ship? Is its blowing the voyage? If we are to believe today's <term>idealists</term><note>Idealists (like Hegel or Schelling) argued that reality is fundamentally mental or spiritual. Fechner felt their theories had become too abstract and "wind-like," lacking a solid connection to physical reality.</note>, then yes. But where has the faith in them gone? Ask the wind itself.

This is what allows <term>materialism</term><note>The belief that only physical matter exists. Fechner admits materialism is "grounded" in facts, but argues it is "spiritually empty."</note> to gain so much ground today: it is the only thing in philosophy that still has any "ground" beneath it. Yet the ground is all it has. For where has all faith gone along with it? Even the questions of faith have vanished with it.

Here, however, by answering the old questions of faith, a new territory for them is opened.

Simply and without metaphor, this work provides briefly much of what I have previously said at length in the same spirit; it treats at length much that I previously said briefly; and it offers much that is new, which I have not said before. Principles and arguments are more precise than before, and general views are more general than before. The first chapter provides information on the nature of the task, while the final chapters conclude with the most general perspectives.

<sig>1</sig>

<vocab>machines, railways, telegraphs, materialism, philosophy, idealists, questions of faith, principles, viewpoints</vocab>

<summary>Fechner argues that the rise of industrial technology and materialism is a necessary "hunger" that will lead humanity back to a spiritualized worldview. He positions his book as a refined, precise evolution of his previous philosophical attempts to reconcile science and faith.</summary>
<keywords>industrialization, materialism vs. idealism, philosophy of nature, soul of the earth, spiritual hunger, Gustav Theodor Fechner</keywords>

<meta>The previous page provided a Table of Contents for a work focused on ethics and honor, listing "The Introduction in General" as the first chapter starting on page 1. This page matches that numbering and title, beginning the philosophical investigation by framing the "question of the soul" as the fundamental entry point for the broader study.</meta>

## The Question of the Soul in General.

To question someone about their soul means to ask the most pressing question that can be put to them: what they have within their soul. This is preceded by the question that every person can ask of themselves—how things stand with their own soul—and more generally, the question of how things stand with the world of souls to which their own belongs. Certainly, the most urgent and important questions that a soul can ever ask are those it must ask about itself and the world of souls. Here, then, we have <term>soul-questions</term> <note>German: *Seelenfragen*. This refers to existential inquiries regarding the nature of the human spirit and its place in the cosmos.</note> in the preeminent sense.

If only we also had Someone whom we could ask about our own soul, based on His own. But who could it be? Indisputably only One, for whom the question would simply be about something He has in His own soul—One in whom all souls live, and move, and have their being. <note>This is a paraphrase of the biblical passage Acts 17:28, often used in 19th-century German philosophy to describe a pantheistic or panentheistic view of God.</note> But now the question arises again: does such a Being truly exist, and how do we reach Him? And whom, finally, should we ask about this? The philosophers? The theologians? We approach them; alas, what bickering! Where is the answer about which there is no bickering?

One sees here, roughly, the field, the meaning, and the difficulty of the questions at hand.

<summary>The author introduces the "question of the soul" as the most vital human inquiry, arguing that a true answer could only come from a universal Being in whom all souls exist, though he notes the constant disagreement among philosophers and theologians on the subject.</summary>
<keywords>soul-question, world of souls, divinity, philosophy, theology, existential inquiry</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction from page 1, narrowing the broad "question of the soul" down to a specific inquiry regarding the presence of consciousness or "soul" within the natural world.</meta>

...could and in part will deal with. However, let us proceed in order, first listing and categorizing everything in the customary way, so that we may finally arrive at the specific small portion that will be the subject here—and, in doing so, arrive at some things to which people are not quite so accustomed.

More specifically, various things can be understood by the <term>question of the soul</term> <note>German: *Seelenfrage*. This term encompasses the study of consciousness, the spirit, and the metaphysical essence of life.</note>: the question of the nature of the soul, the question of the soul’s fate, the question of the soul’s relationship to the body, and the question of the extension of the soul-realm through nature, the <term>physical world</term> <note>German: *Körperwelt*, literally "body-world," referring to the material universe.</note>. Fundamentally, these are all merely sub-questions of a more general question, and they cannot be treated entirely independently of one another. Yet one may be dealt with before the others; and that will be the case here with the last of these. Regardless, then, of how one might otherwise choose to interpret the "question of the soul"—I simply reserve for myself the freedom that I allow to everyone else—here it will be understood as the question of the extension of the soul-realm through nature or the physical world. This shall be made the primary, if not the exclusive, object of our consideration.

Even in this restricted meaning, however, the question of the soul is still broad enough; it has its own various sub-questions, many of which one admittedly never asks, because one already has the answer before the question is even posed—or at least believes one has it.

Are humans ensouled?
Are animals ensouled?
Are plants ensouled?
Are stones ensouled?
Are celestial bodies ensouled?
Is the world ensouled?

<summary>The author defines the "question of the soul" as the study of how far consciousness extends into the material world, setting the stage to ask if plants, stones, and planets possess souls.</summary>
<keywords>soul-question, philosophy of mind, panpsychism, nature of the soul, physical world, ensoulment, celestial bodies</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific inquiry into the "question of the soul" established on page 1. Having previously defined the scope as the "extension of the soul-realm through nature," the author now narrows the focus to the human body and the physical requirements for consciousness.</meta>

How far does the soul reach within the human body itself?

And of these questions, the last one alone presents many sub-questions:

->Is the entire human being <term>ensouled</term>? <note>German: *beseelt*. This refers to the state of being permeated by or possessing a soul or consciousness.</note><-
->Is only the nervous system ensouled?<-
->Is only the brain ensouled?<-
->Is only a part of the brain ensouled?<-
->Is only a point in the brain ensouled?<-
->Does a torso without a head still have a soul?<-
->Does a head without a torso still have a soul?<-
->Are the various functions of the soul distributed among<-
->different parts of the body, and how so?<-

Questions which must necessarily be preceded by the question: what does it even mean for a body to be ensouled at all? What is the "seat of the soul" in a body or body part?

To this are added some more general questions.

->Can an organic whole have ensouled parts,<-
->without being ensouled as a whole?<-

->Can an ensouled whole be produced by anything<-
->other than an ensouled whole?<-

->Can the entirety of the consequences of an ensouled whole<-
->be anything other than another ensouled whole?<-

These questions, too, are usually not asked; yet it would be good if they were, as one would more easily find the answer to many specific questions, or would find different answers than those currently found.

And furthermore, one can pose several counter-questions:

->Does God have a body?<-
->Do angels have bodies?<-
->Do souls in the <term>hereafter</term> <note>German: *jenseitigen Seelen*, literally "souls on the other side," referring to the afterlife.</note> have bodies?<-
->Is the higher spiritual nature in humans bound to<-
-><term>corporeality</term> <note>German: *Leiblichkeit*. The condition of having a physical, material body.</note> just as the lower nature is?<-

<summary>The author lists a series of provocative questions regarding the localization of the soul within the body and the relationship between physical structures (like the brain or nervous system) and spiritual existence. These inquiries expand from the human anatomy to broader metaphysical questions about the bodies of divine beings and the nature of the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>soul, ensouled, nervous system, brain, corporeality, anatomy of consciousness, seat of the soul, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's refinement of the "question of the soul." While the previous page listed specific anatomical questions regarding where the soul resides in the human body, this page pivots toward a broader philosophical and cosmological scope, specifically whether the entire physical world possesses a soul.</meta>

Questions which must again be preceded by the question:
is there a God? Are there angels? Are there souls in the <term>afterlife</term> <note>German: *jenseitige Seelen*, referring to souls existing beyond the physical world or after death.</note>? What is meant by the higher spiritual realm?

One sees that the questions are many, and they are important questions leading into breadth and depth, which are subordinate to the <term>question of the soul</term> <note>German: *Seelenfrage*.</note>, even in the narrowest sense in which we grasp it here. To treat them completely and thoroughly in context would require a difficult work; I do not wish to provide that here. I am therefore restricting myself once more, by making only the question of the expansion of the <term>soul-realm</term> <note>German: *Seelenreiches*.</note> beyond us, and not how and how far the soul reaches into our bodies, the object of my consideration; and I shall only keep in view the question of the <term>ensoulment</term> <note>German: *Beseelung*. The quality of being permeated by a soul or consciousness.</note> of the physical world, not the question of the embodiment of the spiritual world, thus leaving the last main question with its sub-questions and counter-questions entirely aside.

Having stated what part of the overall question I will tackle, something remains to be said about *how* I will tackle it.

A double path is open: from the top down, or from the bottom up. At the very top stands the most general question, the question of the ensoulment of the entire world. Does a frog without a head have a soul? "Oh yes," says <note>Eduard Pflüger (1829–1910) was a prominent German physiologist who argued that the spinal cord possessed a "sensory soul," citing experiments where decapitated frogs still performed purposeful movements.</note> Pflüger, for it still behaves almost like a frog with a head. Does the world with a thousand heads have a soul? "Oh no," thinks the whole world; for it behaves neither like a frog nor a human with a head. There are other reasons against it; but also some in favor, which partly flow from the answers to those questions that people are not accustomed to asking, yet which it would be good if they were asked. Enough; it is a question, and remains the supreme question. The lowest question, on the other hand—

<summary>The author clarifies that his inquiry will focus on whether the physical world as a whole is "ensouled," rather than investigating the mechanics of the human body or the afterlife. He introduces the "top-down vs. bottom-up" methodology, referencing contemporary physiological experiments on headless frogs to illustrate the complexity of defining consciousness.</summary>
<keywords>ensoulment, soul-realm, physical world, spiritual world, Eduard Pflüger, methodology, cosmology, panpsychism</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "ensoulment" of the world. While the previous page ended with the "highest" question (the soul of the entire world), this page transitions to the "lowest" level of the inquiry: the souls of animals, plants, and even minerals.</meta>

On the other hand, there is the question of the lowest level of individual **ensoulment** <note>German: *Beseelung*. The state of being endowed with a soul.</note> in the world. Is it the animals, is it the plants, or is it even the stones? Starting from the highest question, one can descend to the lowest; starting from the last, one can ascend to the first. <term>Nature-philosophy</term> <note>German: *Naturphilosophie*. A 19th-century philosophical movement that sought to understand the organic unity of nature and spirit.</note> is accustomed to going downstairs; we want to go upstairs. This is tiring; therefore, it will again be necessary to shorten the path as much as possible.

Yet we cannot begin directly from the bottom; for we do not stand directly at the bottom; everyone must start from where they stand. Fundamentally, every person is directly certain only of their own soul. *I think, therefore I am* <note>original Latin: *Cogito, ergo sum*.</note>; there is no denying or doubting that. This unquestionable fact is the starting point of the whole question. Yet, beyond this, no one doubts the soul of their brother—even if some might doubt whether they should consider the Black man <note>German: *Neger*. The author is reflecting 19th-century debates regarding the "hierarchy" of humanity, which often used racist pseudoscience to question the spiritual equality of different races.</note> as such; and, apart from **Descartes** <note>Latinized in text as *Cartesius*.</note> and a few modern thinkers, everyone considers the singing nightingale in the tree and the roaring lion in the desert to be something more than acoustic machines <note>Descartes famously argued that animals were "automata" or machines without souls or feelings.</note>. In order to keep our task as brief as possible, it shall not first be proven that humans and animals possess souls; rather, that shall be taken as granted, and we will proceed from there. Just as we shall proceed from here, we could have proceeded from our own ensoulment to that point. But we gladly spare ourselves and others that part of the journey already traveled.

So much for the *what* and the *how*; now a few more words on the foundations of this treatment.

Philosophy is quite ready to offer us such foundations, just as theology is ready to spare us from needing them. We reach for the former and begin—since no philosophical foundations are currently regarded as universally familiar and valid—

<summary>The author describes a bottom-up approach to studying the soul, moving from the certainty of human and animal consciousness toward the more controversial question of plant and mineral souls. He rejects the Cartesian view of animals as soulless machines, taking their "ensoulment" as a starting point for further inquiry.</summary>

<keywords>ensoulment, animal souls, Descartes, Nature-philosophy, Cogito ergo sum, methodology, 19th-century philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 5, which discussed the starting point for investigating the soul. The author is moving from the subjective certainty of one's own soul to the broader philosophical concepts required to understand the soul's place in the universe.</meta>

...to presuppose, in the following chapter with the necessary preliminary discussions about the **identity and non-identity of Being and Thinking** <note>A central problem in German Idealism: whether the objective world (Being) and the subjective mind (Thinking) are fundamentally the same or different.</note>, the relationship of the **Ideal and the Real**, the relations and stages through which the **Absolute** <note>In this context, the Absolute refers to the ultimate, unconditioned reality or the divine source of all things.</note> descends into reality, the **concept of creation** <note>German: *Schöpfungsbegriff*.</note>, the relationship of **God and World**, **Nature and Spirit**, **Force and Matter**, **Life**, **Soul**, **Organism**, **Necessity**, **Freedom**, **Finiteness**, and **Infinity**—all of which, along with many other things, belongs essentially to the philosophical preparation for our question. In doing so, it will be necessary to briefly review the various philosophical views on these matters historically and critically, in order to establish our own position clearly and securely amidst the conflict of viewpoints.

Admittedly, this is no easy task, and it becomes increasingly difficult with every new attempt at a solution. Happy **Sisyphus**! <note>In Greek mythology, Sisyphus was condemned to roll a boulder up a hill for eternity, only for it to roll back down just as it reached the top.</note> Your stone always rolls back down; but it remains the same old stone familiar to you. The stone that the "Wise Men" <note>The author is referring to philosophers.</note> roll also only reaches the peak to roll down again, but after every fall it has become twice as heavy, and the old handles have broken off. Right now it lies at the bottom and refuses to budge, no matter how many arms and breaths have already been spent to begin its new ascent. Up! Let us try once more!

Yet with sadness I see that all who have followed me thus far are fleeing at this announcement, this call to arms; I see that in my next chapter I shall have to roll the **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>German: *Stein der Philoſophen*. Usually a legendary substance in alchemy, here the author uses it metaphorically to describe the "heavy" and difficult problems of metaphysics.</note> with the philosophers alone—and, what is more, I will have to overcome the resistance of everyone else. Therefore, I would rather begin differently. Let the philosophers roll their stone, and roll it against one another, uphill and downhill; let us instead simply climb the mountain itself, the comfort—

<summary>The author lists the complex metaphysical concepts necessary for his inquiry but realizes such dense academic jargon might drive readers away. He decides to abandon formal philosophical "stone-rolling" in favor of a more direct, accessible approach to the subject.</summary>
<keywords>Metaphysics, German Idealism, Sisyphus, Being and Thinking, God and World, Nature-philosophy, Absolute, methodology</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the metaphor from page 6, where the author compared the pursuit of philosophical truth to Sisyphus rolling a heavy stone. He is now transitioning to his specific method: moving away from abstract, "shaky" philosophical definitions toward a foundation built on experience and observation.</meta>

...seeking the highest steps and holding onto the firmest ledges <note>The author continues a mountaineering metaphor, suggesting that abstract philosophy is like a slippery slope, while experience provides "ledges" to hold onto.</note>. That is to say, instead of dealing with wavering concepts that only hold up as long as we hold onto them, let us attempt the path of firm, experiential viewpoints that always hold fast.

One asks from the outset: is this path possible? The whole question is not one of experience. Nevertheless, we want to attempt it, and instead of using philosophical preliminary discussions as a foundation, we shall seek to underpin philosophical views themselves with it. And though we do not begin with a general worldview, we shall certainly end with one.

I am well aware that people today argue about the **question of the soul** in a different sense than I conceive of it here. But why should I carry one more drop into the fruitlessly raging sea? And what does it matter, in the end, whether the world is said to be "dead" in an idealistic or materialistic sense, or whether it possesses a spirit made of words or none at all? Idealism is generous enough with the former; but the true **vital question** is not whether the world has a spirit that only *we* know about, but one that also *knows about us* <note>This is a pivotal distinction: the author is looking for a living, conscious connection between the individual and the universe, rather than a cold, mechanical, or purely theoretical "spirit."</note>. It is this question, with its subordinate questions, that will be dealt with here, in short.

The following is an overview of the upcoming reflections:
Some conceptual and fundamental preliminary discussions. The counter-arguments against a generalization of the **animation of nature** <term>Naturbeseelung: the philosophical concept that all of nature, not just humans, possesses a soul or inner life</term> beyond humans and animals. In particular, the question of the **plant soul** as the soul of the lowest stage that can be considered. The principle of an experiential grounding for faith in the **highest and last things** <note>Meaning the ultimate concerns of existence, such as God or the afterlife.</note>. The question of the soul...

<summary>The author rejects abstract philosophical debates in favor of an experience-based approach to the soul, defining his central goal as proving the existence of a universal spirit that is conscious of humanity. He provides an outline for the book, including his intent to argue for the "plant soul" and the animation of nature.</summary>

<keywords>experiential viewpoints, question of the soul, idealism vs. materialism, vital question, animation of nature, plant soul, universal spirit, methodology</keywords>

...of the stars and the world. Difficulties regarding freedom and individual persistence <note>The German word *Fortbestande* refers to the continued existence or immortality of the individual soul.</note>. Fundamental view, world view, concluding view.

<unclear>falls asleep to the spirit.</unclear>

<unclear>II.</unclear>
<unclear>Conceptual matters concerning Soul, Spirit, Body, Living Body, Nature.</unclear>

### Conceptual matters concerning Soul, Spirit, Physical Body, Living Body, and Nature.

Soul, spirit, physical body, living body, and nature—all of these are so interconnected that one cannot thoroughly inquire about one without also inquiring about the others. But before all else, the first question is what is actually being asked here—that is, what we are to understand by all these terms.

What are we to understand by them? By this, I do not mean what they are in their *essence* <note>original: *Wesen*</note>; I have already declined to answer that by rejecting philosophical preliminary discussions. One will not find oneself at a loss if one demands an answer, but rather at a loss as to which answer one should follow. According to one person, it is "the Absolute" <note>A term in 19th-century German Idealism referring to the ultimate reality or God.</note>; according to another, it is a fluid concept; according to a third, it is a plurality of rigid beings that underlies everything—and thus also the soul, the living body, and nature—in the final analysis. 

Here, it will be a matter of indifference which of these answers one chooses to follow; for we shall consider soul, spirit, physical body <term>Körper: the physical or anatomical body</term>, living body <term>Leib: the lived, sensing body as experienced from within</term>, and nature here merely from the side of their appearance <note>In philosophy, "appearance" or "phenomenon" refers to things as we perceive them through experience, rather than their hidden "true" nature.</note>. — "How superficial!" I hear someone say. On the contrary, it is very superficial to [treat] the appearance...

<summary>The author begins defining the core concepts of his study, arguing that we should focus on how the soul and body "appear" to us in experience rather than debating their abstract philosophical "essence."</summary>

<keywords>soul, spirit, physical body, living body, nature, essence, appearance, German Idealism, phenomenology</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions of "soul," "body," and "living body" from the previous discussion on philosophical terminology. It picks up mid-sentence from page 8, where the author was cautioning against viewing these concepts as superficial.</meta>

...[opi-]nion <note>The German word fragment "nung" likely completes "Meinung" (opinion) from the previous page.</note> for something merely superficial. However, let us not argue about this already in the second chapter, but rather only in one of the final chapters.

Now, however, it is necessary to point out within the world of appearance itself that which I intend to refer to by those words, which have gradually become more and more incomprehensible through the ever-increasing profundity <note>original: *Tiefsinn*</note> of philosophical explanation. It must be pointed out. Whatever anyone may then wish to seek behind the appearance <note>In philosophy, the "appearance" or phenomenon is the world as it presents itself to our senses, as opposed to the "thing-in-itself" or its hidden nature.</note> is left to each individual, provided that they seek it behind what is pointed out here, and behind nothing else, if we are to understand one another. In this task of pointing things out, I will, of course, also speak of <term>essence</term> and of the <term>essence</term>, and call the <term>soul</term> itself an <term>essence</term> <note>original: *Wesen*; this can mean a living being, a creature, or the inner nature/substance of a thing.</note>. Yet it will only ever be insofar as something truly apparent can be shown of it or can be abstracted from what appears.

By <term>soul</term> <note>original: *Seele*</note>, then, I understand that unified essence which appears to no one except itself—in us as elsewhere, wherever such a thing occurs—bright to itself, but dark to every external eye; at the very least linking sensory perceptions within itself, upon which, as the level of the soul rises, the consciousness of higher and higher relationships is built up.

By <term>physical body</term> <note>original: *Körper*</note> in contrast to the <term>soul</term>, I understand the material system graspable only in external appearance, which the <term>physicist</term> and <term>physiologist</term> so name.

I point to the <term>soul</term> with a finger turned inward, and to the <term>physical body</term> with a finger turned outward; the <term>soul</term> with the finger of the <term>soul</term>, and the <term>physical body</term> with the finger of the <term>body</term>.

By <term>living body</term> <note>original: *Leib*; German philosophy often distinguishes between the body as a physical object (*Körper*) and the body as a living, felt experience (*Leib*).</note>, I understand a <term>physical body</term> that stands in a corresponding relationship to a <term>soul</term>, just as our own <term>body</term> stands to our <term>soul</term>.

He introduces the critical distinction between the physical body as an object of science and the "living body" as the vessel of the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's terminological definitions, moving from the definition of the soul to the definition of "Nature" and the "Physical World." It transitions into a discussion of whether non-human entities, like plants or the universe itself, possess an inner life equivalent to the human soul.</meta>

By **nature** or the **physical world**—often also simply called "the world"—[I mean] the entire system of the corporeal that exists, which is graspable only through external appearance, along with all its movements, relationships, and determinations.

If it is now asked: **does a plant have a soul?** then this means: in addition to what can be seen of it externally and what is called its body—that which the botanist calls a "plant" and investigates with external senses and tools—does there also appear something internally in the form of sensation, feeling, and the like? Is there something of which the botanist sees nothing and which they cannot abstract from all that is seen, something which is graspable only in <term>self-appearance</term> <note>original: *Selbsterscheinung*; this refers to the way a being experiences its own existence from the "inside," as opposed to how it is observed by others from the "outside."</note> and is linked into a unity similar to all the determinations of our own soul, which appears to itself? In other words: is there within the plant an **inner brightness** to correspond to the **external brightness** in which its body appears to us? Or is it dark through its entire depth, and the external appearance the entirety of what exists of it, or at least, the entirety of what appears of it?

Does **nature** or the **world** have a soul? This means: does there belong to the totality of what is graspable with the external senses—what is visible and tangible—to the total system of <term>celestial bodies</term> <note>original: *Weltkörper*; literally "world-bodies," referring to planets, stars, and the physical structures of the universe.</note> rolling around one another externally, turning green and blooming, carrying creatures and their history, just such a unified being that appears only to itself? Does such a being belong to the world just as one belongs to the totality of what is visible and tangible in a human being—to the total system of their surging blood, bones, veins, and nerves? [It would be] a being that is just as little recognizable with telescopes, earth drills, measuring rods, chemical reagents, and all the mathematics in the world, as the corresponding being in us is recognizable with microscopes, scalpels, chemical analyses, and mathematics.

<summary>The author defines nature as the physical system visible to the senses and poses the central question of whether plants or the entire universe possess an invisible "soul" or inner life. He argues that such an inner life, or "self-appearance," cannot be detected by scientific instruments, just as a human's inner thoughts cannot be seen with a microscope.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's terminological definitions, specifically transitioning from the relationship between the physical world and God to the distinction between "Spirit" and "Soul." In short: is there a God belonging to the world—not outside it, not behind it, not in a distant Beyond above it, but in a relationship to the world corresponding to the one our soul has to our body? Regardless of how one might wish to conceive this relationship itself more precisely and deeply.

I am only posing the questions now, not to decide them yet, but merely to clarify their meaning.

Now it only remains to state what I understand by **spirit** as opposed to **soul**, in order to complete the circle of concepts in which we will constantly move in what follows.

As is known, nearly everyone conceives the relationship between spirit and soul differently, and some in such a way that it cannot be grasped at all. Perhaps a few more words on this than are strictly necessary here might nonetheless be useful.

An image! Imagine a circle and divide it crosswise into a right and left half, and then again into an upper and lower half.

One usage is to call the entire circle indifferently "spirit" or "soul"; the second is to call the right side "spirit" and the left "soul"; the third is to call the upper side "spirit" and the lower "soul." I speak only of the primary usages, for who can think of all the possible cross-wise and diagonal divisions?

In the first sense, spirit and soul are placed as synonymous wholes in opposition to the body or physical frame as a whole, and it is considered the same to say: "The soul goes to heaven," or "The spirit goes to heaven." It is the entire circle.

In the second sense, spirit is presented as the active, the creative, the procreative (hence the names *natural talent* <note>original: *ingenium*; refers to innate character or mental capacity</note> and *guiding spirit* <note>original: *genius*; refers to the divine or creative power within a person</note>) and the soul as the desiring,

The wind has no rest; the sea rests as smooth as a mirror, reflecting the moon and stars—until the wind arrives and lifts it into waves.

The spirit is King, the soul is Queen at his side; the King rules the land, but the Queen rules the King.

In the third sense, the spirit occupies the upper position and the soul the lower.

The soul, in this third sense, is the more sensory element, more closely related to the physical, still entirely or partially submerged within it and bound by it—perhaps even still half-body itself. The spirit is that which works and weaves above it in higher, freer relationships and strivings...

The previous contrast <note>referring to the "second sense" of masculine/feminine complementarity described on the previous page</note> is more a matter of living usage, whereas the current one is more a matter of philosophical usage; and as these two ways of using the terms intersect, they overlap halfway—but only halfway.

To be sure, even the philosopher seems likely to call such an eye "soulful" despite his own definition of the soul; but consistency makes such inconsistencies necessary.

Everyone now places the dividing walls through the circle a little differently: the vertical one a bit more to the right or left, the horizontal one a bit higher or lower. Some place the wall at an angle, draw additional partitions here and there, and tear down the walls drawn by others, thinking they have finally solved it.

To one person, the soul is nothing more than life-force; to another, it is a simple, rigid being concerned with its own self-preservation; to another, it is the abstract unity of every thing; to another, it is the center point within a periphery. For some, the "spirit" coincides with law, with force, with every universality—even if it is only abstractable from the physical, only conceivable, but not itself thinking. <meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on the shifting definitions of "spirit" and "soul," moving from various philosophical interpretations to the author's own pragmatic decision to use the terms synonymously for the purpose of this text.</meta>

...a spirit—though one must by no means think of anything possessing properties other than those of a spirit.

And so, in the end, everything is stirred together; and every prong on the great whisk <note>original: *Quirle*; a traditional kitchen tool used for stirring or whisking liquids</note> believes it has contributed something to the clarification.

And which of all these is ultimately the only true and correct meaning? I believe that each one is equally correct. After all, every word means exactly what each person intends; though, to be sure, one person should not intend something different than another, and no one should mistake the feathers for the flight of the bird. Furthermore, because common usage <term>Sprachgebrauch: the way language is typically used by the public</term> fluctuates, it is necessary to end this vacillation where stability is required by "tightening a screw"—one that can be loosened again at another time. Thus, with the following, I am now setting the screw for the use of "spirit" and "soul" in this work, without preventing anyone else from setting it differently for their own purposes.

The first and broadest usage is the one I shall follow here: I understand "spirit" and "soul" synonymously to mean the same essence as opposed to the physical body <note>original: *Körper* or *Leibe*; German distinguishes between the body as a physical object and the living, experienced body</note>—that which appears to itself—because this definition is best suited for our purpose.

For how else should I name the entire unity of the self-appearing being, including all the lower and higher contents appearing within it, in contrast to the externally appearing body, if I were to divide the names "soul" and "spirit" among its various aspects, regions, or parts?

It is only the relationship of that whole to the body, and from the most general perspective <term>Gesichtspuncte: a specific point of view or analytical standpoint</term> at that, which concerns us here, not the discussion of its internal relations; though where there is nonetheless an active and a receptive element, a lower and a higher, to be distinguished <note>original: *unter-*; a catchword likely continuing as *unterscheiden* (to distinguish) on the following page</note> within the spirit, or the soul...

<meta>This page concludes the author's terminological distinctions between spirit and soul. He establishes a pragmatic framework for the rest of the book, choosing to treat these terms as synonymous "internal" experiences to focus on his primary inquiry: whether non-human entities like plants and the Earth possess a soul.</meta>

...to distinguish them, there will be no shortage of words for the matter.
Let us be easy with words, but rigorous with the matter itself. <note>original: "In verbis simus faciles, in re difficiles."</note>

Thus, to finally summarize what has come before:
**Spirit** and **soul** are synonymous terms for a unified essence that is only graspable through self-appearance <note>Fechner’s term for internal, subjective experience—how a being feels or perceives itself from the inside</note>, and can only be characterized through the phenomena and determinations of that self-appearance. **Physical body** and **living body** refer to a system that is only graspable through external appearance and the outer senses, and is characterized by the relationships and determinations of that external appearance. **Nature** is the entire system of physical things, of which our own small body is but a part. The **soul-question** is the question of the extent to which **spirit**, **soul**, **spirits**, or **souls** are linked to the system as a whole and to its subordinate parts.

The nature of the fundamental relationship between physical body and spirit, or living body and soul, remains entirely set aside in these explanations, just as it does in this question. The former are verbal pointers toward factual reality, intended to help find that reality; the latter is a question of factual reality itself, regardless of how one might later interpret those facts.

Within these explanations, one can still be a materialist, idealist, dualist, monist, or <term>monadist: a follower of the philosophy that the universe is made of fundamental, indivisible units called monads</term>. One can ask, regardless of their basic view on the relationship between body and soul: does the plant have a soul, does the earth have a soul in the same or an analogous sense as a human being has a soul? *That* is our question. One could then ask further: is the soul a tiny point, a flow, a <term>monas: a monad; a single, irreducible metaphysical unit</term>, or a result? *That* is not our question. One can make the former question dependent on the latter, and will thereby fall into all the obscurities and disputes of the latter; one can also attempt to treat it independently—and such an independent treatment is the intention of this work.

<summary>The author defines soul and spirit as internal "self-appearance" and body as external "appearance," setting aside metaphysical debates to focus on whether plants and the Earth possess souls.</summary>

<meta>Following the definitions established on the previous page, where the author distinguished between the internal experience (soul) and external appearance (body), he now begins his formal inquiry into which entities in the universe can be said to possess a soul.</meta>

### III.
## Approach to the Soul-Question.

From the outset, it must be admitted that the entire **soul-question** is and remains a **matter of faith** <note>original: "Glaubensfrage." In this context, the author means a question that cannot be proven by the strict rules of natural science, but requires a leap of conviction based on evidence.</note>; and regardless of how we begin or how we end, we shall be unable to find or provide exact proofs for anything. Exact proof rests on experience and mathematics; but direct experience is possible only of one’s own soul, and mathematics lacks any starting point to prove the existence of another.

In this regard, it is no different with the soul of my brother, my father, or my mother, than it is with God, with those in the afterlife, or with any souls whatsoever. People assume that the **existence** of those familial souls is self-evident; and indeed it is, yet it is just as little capable of exact proof as the existence of these others. Consequently, the existence of the latter could be just as well-founded as the former, even while being just as little demonstrably exact.

My brother is very similar to me and expresses himself similarly; therefore, I believe most firmly that he is **ensouled** <note>original: "beseelt," meaning "possessing a soul" or "animated by spirit."</note>. But where does the similarity to me required for the existence of a soul begin, and where does it end? Is **analogy** a strict proof? Have I not had to hear again and again that it is not? My father begat me as an ensouled being, and my mother gave birth to me; I believe, therefore, most firmly that they are also ensouled. But the world has generated all ensouled creatures, and the Earth has given them birth; my ancestors, from where could they finally ori—

<vocab>Soul-question, Matter of faith, Mathematics, Soul, Existence, Analogy</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that the existence of any soul other than one's own cannot be mathematically proven and is ultimately a matter of faith based on analogy. He suggests that if we accept the souls of other people based on their similarity to us, we must question where the limit of that similarity lies.</summary>
<keywords>soul-question, faith vs proof, subjectivity, analogy, existence of others, ensoulment, Gustav Theodor Fechner</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 17, where he questioned why we assume human parents produce ensouled offspring while the world (which produces humans) is viewed as soulless. He now addresses the limits of "exact science" in proving or disproving the existence of souls in the physical world.</meta>

...originate? And does one consider it exactly proven by this that the world, the Earth, is **ensouled**?

Just as little as a proof can be offered *for* it, no such proof can be offered *against* the existence of any soul. Could not a grain of sand, a dot over the 'i', indeed even a simple atom—or perhaps a simple essence behind the atom—be ensouled? Everyone is free to have their own fancy in this regard, and such ideas have indeed been held; exact science cannot have anything binding against the soul in a tiny point, precisely because it also has no binding "for" regarding the existence of a God in the world or a soul in my brother.

In a word, **exact science** <note>In the 19th-century context, "exact science" refers to fields like physics and mathematics that rely on empirical measurement and proof.</note> has thus far found no point of attack across the entire field of this question.

It certainly cannot avoid coming into contact with it; but science is like a duck out of water whenever it attempts to cross over into that domain. The controversy surrounding **Pflüger’s spinal cord soul** <note>Eduard Pflüger (1829–1910) was a physiologist who famously argued that the spinal cord possessed its own sensory consciousness, separate from the brain, based on his studies of reflex actions in decapitated frogs.</note> is still fresh; has it found a firm ground, a principle, or an end? What has exact science known since then of the grounds of the soul, other than **ganglion cells** <note>original: "Ganglienkugeln," a 19th-century term for the cell bodies of neurons.</note>, phosphorus, or electricity? And does all of that have any more to do with the soul than the tallow of a candle has to do with the radiance of the light?

It is indeed possible that from a doctrine whose elements I am presenting in another work, an exact approach to the **soul-question** may one day be achieved in the future. This doctrine seeks the empirical, lawful relationships between one’s own soul and the **physical world**, tracking them from the exterior to the interior, and seeks to fix them through comprehension and connection under **mathematical expressions** to make them productive for further conclusions. Until now, the scope—

<meta>catchword: scope—</meta>

<summary>The author argues that exact science can neither prove nor disprove the existence of souls in physical objects or other beings, comparing the biological components of the brain to candle wax that is distinct from the light it produces.</summary>
<keywords>exact science, soul-question, Pflüger, spinal cord soul, ganglion cells, phosphorus, mathematical expressions, physical world</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 18, where he established that "exact science" (empirical fields like physics) lacks the tools to prove or disprove the existence of souls. He now transitions into why we must seek answers beyond strict scientific proof.</meta>

...the scope of the laws and expressions gained thus far cannot yet be fully surveyed. But I do not base my argument here on distant prospects. The doctrine of this writing <note>The author is likely contrasting his current philosophical work with his earlier, strictly empirical scientific publications.</note> begins where the doctrine that has only just begun in that other work will one day have to end.

Why then, one might ask, not set the entire **soul question** aside, if for now nothing certain can be reached regarding it? But belief in the existence of the souls of our fellow human beings is necessary to us; belief in the existence of **animal souls** is natural; and a worldview that includes a general answer to the soul question is a higher need. Thus, while **exact science** can reject the question as long as it cannot provide an exact answer, we cannot reject it altogether; and if no exact proof is to be found, we must look for **substitutes** for exact proof.

And in truth, there must surely be very effective substitutes for such proof, given that we are as certain, or almost as certain, of the existence of other human and animal souls as we are of our own. But if these substitutes are capable of awakening such strong **confidence of belief** regarding the souls closest to us—to the point that we do not even miss strict proof here, and even the most "exact" thinkers do not even ask for it—why not seek and use the same or related substitutes beyond that scope?

In fact, this is the **fundamental point of view** from which I proceed in what follows: to claim no certainty of a different character in the entire soul question than that which we have for the existence of the souls closest to us, but also to utilize the means available for that purpose.

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 19, where the author argued that since we already accept the existence of other human and animal souls without "exact" scientific proof, we should apply similar methods of reasoning to broader questions of the soul.</meta>

The existence of those souls themselves—in which we are compelled to believe by the most immediate reasons—will serve us as an example, foundation, and support for the further construction of the **realm of souls**; only that, in the same measure as we move further and higher beyond the human, and as the difficulty, scope, and height of the task increase, the circle and perspective of our means must be expanded and elevated accordingly.

On one hand, there are practical motives; but I will leave these aside here. On the other, there are points of view that are not themselves direct **experience**, but rather a rational synthesis of what experience offers.

Indeed, although no **other soul** <note>original: "fremde Seele"; referring to the internal life of another being which remains hidden from direct observation</note> can be directly seen or grasped, much can be seen and grasped that stands in relation to its existence; by grasping these things together, we can grasp the other soul along with them.

Just as a **center point** <note>original: "Mittelpunct"</note> that cannot be directly seen or reached can still be recognized through the connection of the parts of the visible **periphery** that relate to it. Of course, one must direct the inquiry toward it, and not seek the sole path of science in using a **microscope** to break down the pieces of the visible circle into even smaller visible parts. That too is necessary, to be sure; but it is not the only thing that is necessary.

The **inference** <note>original: "Schluß"; a logical conclusion or deduction</note> from the visible to the invisible cannot be the same as the inference from the visible to the visible; rather, it takes its place alongside it as a coordinated partner.

The inference from the visible to the visible—the inference of <term>natural science</term> <note>original: "Naturwissenschaft"; the study of the physical world</note>—closes the circle from the outside, in that it proceeds from existence, the connection, and

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 20 regarding the methodology of "natural philosophy." The author is expanding on the geometric metaphor of the circle to explain how we can logically deduce the existence of an invisible "soul" or "center" by observing the visible "periphery" of physical reality.</meta>

...the manner of connection of the given parts of the visible circle reveals the parts that are not given, based on the very condition that they must complete the circle. The inference from the visible to the invisible—the inference of **natural philosophy** <term>Naturphilosophie: A 19th-century German philosophical movement that viewed nature as an organic, living whole rather than a mere machine</term>—completes the circle internally. It does this by deducing the invisible point in the center of the circle from the connection and harmony of all parts of the visible circle.

Can one method of inference sound just like the other? The *materialists* <note>original: "Materialisten"; those who believe only physical matter and its movements exist</note> want to settle everything using only the first method of inference <note>likely referring to the purely empirical "visible to visible" method mentioned on the previous page</note>, and thus they lose what belongs to the second. They only look around the circle and say it has no center point; for indeed, it is not to be found within the visible *periphery* <note>original: "Peripherie"; the outer boundary or circumference of the circle</note>. The *idealists* <note>original: "Idealisten"; those who believe reality is fundamentally mental or spiritual</note> want to reverse the second method, trying to find the *periphery* from the center point. However, they do not even know how to find the center point correctly, and instead wander about searching for it in a *transcendent* <term>transcendent: Existing beyond or independent of the physical universe</term> "beyond." Furthermore, the *periphery* of a center point is not as strictly determined by that center as it is the other way around; thus, all their inferences run from the indefinite into the indefinite.

The task is to seek out the visible *periphery* for the idea of the center point—a periphery that simultaneously proves its existence and determines its location. Then it is easy to draw *radii* <note>original: "Radien"; plural of radius, the lines connecting the center to the edge</note>, and all the dispute over whether the center point or the *periphery* is a *result* of the other becomes vanity.

But just as the goal is a center, so too—though in a different sense—is the starting point of the inference from the visible to the invisible a center point within a circle. All the *radii* of the inference radiate from this point, the only fixed point that exists in this field, the only foundation of experience we have in this domain. All the levers of *argumentation* lean upon it as their fulcrum <note>original: "Stützpunct"; literally a point of support or pivot point</note>.

<vocab>Naturphilosophie, Materialisten, Idealisten, Mittelpunct, Peripherie, transcendent, Radien, Resultat, Argumentation, Stützpunct</vocab>

The author positions this balanced approach as the true method of natural philosophy, using experience as a fixed fulcrum for all reasoning.</summary>
<keywords>natural philosophy, materialism, idealism, geometry metaphor, center and periphery, logical inference, soul, empirical evidence, epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the "circle" metaphor introduced on page 20. While the previous page critiqued materialists and idealists for their one-sided views of the "periphery" (matter) and the "center" (soul), this page establishes the human body as the primary evidence for the connection between the two.</meta>

It is a simple fact: our own bodies are endowed with souls <note>original: "beseelt"; literally "ensouled" or animated by a spirit</note>.

This is something we know; indeed, everything else we know or are capable of knowing about this entire subject, we know only because of this fundamental truth.

We now ask: what further conclusions can be drawn from this regarding the animation of other physical systems—those that are similar to our own in various ways, that complement ours, that differ by degrees, or that stand in some relationship of connection, origin, or purpose to our own?

Each of these questions can be examined and pursued from an empirical <term>empirical: Based on observation or experience rather than theory or pure logic</term> perspective. By considering how all these questions are interconnected, we "complete the circle" <note>This refers to the geometric metaphor from the previous page, where the visible physical world forms a periphery that points toward an invisible spiritual center</note>.

From the standpoint of logical proof, the result remains merely a matter of probability; however, from the standpoint of faith, it can become a deep conviction. Thus, from the first perspective, we can only claim to establish a prevailing probability for all that follows; meanwhile, we strive to build a foundation for conviction from the perspective of faith.

## IV.
### General counter-arguments against the extension of the realm of the soul beyond the world of humans and animals.

Humans and animals are ensouled <note>original: "beseelt"; meaning animated by a soul or spirit</note>, and nothing else—at least nothing else on our earth. Such is the common opinion; such is the opinion of the exact scientists <note>original: "die Exacten"; referring to practitioners of the "exact sciences" like physics and mathematics</note>, the opinion of the philosophers, the opinion of the theologians, the opinion of the idealists, the opinion of the materialists, and the opinion of the monadologists <term>monadologists: Followers of Leibniz’s theory that the universe is made of fundamental, soul-like units called "monads"</term>. It is a compact unity of thought, and it is only sad that it refers to a negation, and that this negation of unity results in a lack of agreement in all other matters. 

This is sad, but natural; for it seems natural to me that spirits can only become "of one mind" <note>The author uses a play on the word "Geist," which means both "spirit" and "mind." To be "of one mind" is "Eines Geistes" in German</note> in the view that there is **One Spirit** of the world, and all spirits are of **One Spirit**. Instead, nowadays, everyone unites around a gap—a void—too large for them to reach across and join hands.

But what is the reason for restricting the realm of the spirit so severely, for turning life into a mere parasitic kingdom of the dead? Let us speak here only of the most general reasons.

1) The primary reason is that people have believed they must raise God so far above the world that the world no longer dares to claim itself as His body. Now, the world remains behind beneath Him, empty of spirit; all its spirit rests only in our own spiritual observation, or in the world's laws and forces—in that which even a statue or 

<summary>The author critiques the modern consensus that only humans and animals possess souls, arguing that this "gap" in our worldview prevents a true spiritual unity. He begins to list the reasons for this restricted view, starting with the theological desire to separate God from the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>soul, spirit, ensoulment, materialism, idealism, monadology, philosophy of nature, theology, unity of spirit</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page concludes the list of five common arguments against the world-soul initiated on the previous page, and then begins the author's systematic rebuttal, specifically addressing the concepts of divine omnipresence and the definition of natural "rules."</meta>

...which the machine also has. The idealists still call it spirit <note>original: "Geist"; in German philosophy, this can mean mind, spirit, or intellect depending on context</note>; however, we are not dealing with that kind of spirit here.

2) Since the world as a whole is once and for all considered soul-less, its members must naturally also be considered as such. An exception is made for humans and animals; one must, after all; but one naturally seeks to restrict exceptions as much as possible. In order to justify them, one makes a second exception, positioning the human and animal bodies themselves as exceptions to the rest of nature; and after one has removed all spirit from nature, it is, of course, quite natural that she no longer possesses any.

3) The soul is invisible; and so one nowhere sees the slightest trace of a soul in nature and natural bodies beyond humans and animals, can point to nothing, grasp nothing; thus, it is simplest if one denies its existence.

4) Apart from animals, all natural bodies are so unlike humans that analogy does not permit one to believe in a soul beyond humans and animals. — No nervous system, no soul.

5) Since the contrary opinion has once become general and fixed, education and habit contribute to maintaining it.

Regarding the first point, however, one speaks of an omnipresent God in addition to a transcendent <note>original: "überweltlichen"; literally "above-the-world" or extramundane</note> one, and why should He penetrate the world more like water in a sponge than like spirit in a body?

Regarding the second point, one must first be certain of the rule in order to be able to make exceptions; now the question arises first: what is the rule?

**Vocabulary**

*   **Machine:** Used here to describe a purely mechanical view of the universe.
*   **Idealists:** Philosophers who prioritize the mental or spiritual over the material.
*   **Spirit/Mind:** (*Geist*) The animating or intellectual principle.
*   **Soul-less:** (*seelenlos*) Lacking an internal life or consciousness.
*   **Nature:** The physical world and its laws.
*   **Soul:** (*Seele*) The individual's seat of consciousness and life.
*   **Natural bodies:** Physical objects found in nature (stones, plants, etc.).
*   **Analogy:** The logical method of assuming that if things are similar in some ways, they are similar in others.
*   **Nervous system:** Often cited by materialists as the mandatory physical requirement for having a soul.
*   **Education:** How societal norms and scientific biases are passed down.
*   **God:** Here discussed in the context of how the divine relates to the physical world.
*   **Rule:** The foundational principle from which exceptions are judged.

### V.
## The Plant Soul. Counter-arguments.

My doctrine <term>Lehre: a systematic body of teachings or a specific philosophical theory</term> of the plant soul has been contradicted so often that I may well be permitted to speak in its favor once again.

Winds either uproot the tree, or they cause it to take firmer root and turn a fresher green. As it is with the winds and the plant, so it was with the objections and the belief in the soul of the plant.

It <note>the belief</note> stands upon six main roots, which no wind up... <meta>catchword: ent=; likely "entwurzelt" (uproots)</meta>

...will root; it wears a crown that no wind will strip of its leaves.

The counter-arguments against the plant soul <term>Pflanzenseele: the concept that plants possess a form of consciousness or "soul," a central pillar of Fechner's panpsychism</term> certainly have it easy, because even the lightest among them receives a heavy weight from the last of the established general reasons: upbringing and habit. Or rather, they require no weight at all, because the scale of public opinion already hangs in their favor. And so they step forward lightly enough, yet with the confidence that they would not actually need to appear at all.

To be sure, the plant soul is only a small, weak link in the great soul-structure <note>original: "Seelenbau"; refers to the author's vision of the universe as a nested hierarchy of souls</note> of the world. And how can one see the ornamentation in a house when the whole house is dark? Here, the first of the general counter-arguments comes into play alongside the last. The others place themselves submissively at the service of the former. Who has ever seen anything of a plant soul? And the plant body does not remotely look or behave like a human or animal.

I do not wish to repeat everything I argued against this in *Nanna* <note>Referring to his 1848 book "Nanna, or on the Soul-Life of Plants," where he first detailed these theories</note>, as I will leave it mainly to the positive reasons to strike down the counter-arguments at the same time. I only want to address two objections once more—not because they are the most important, and thus their refutation most vital, nor because they are the easiest, and thus their refutation the most effortless (for, to be honest, I can find no difference in this regard)—but simply for the reason that they are the most recent with which a philosophical opponent has renewed the attack in a widely read work (*Body and Soul* <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary critique of Fechner's work, such as those by materialist philosophers of the mid-19th century</note>). To be sure, the opponent also appeals to experience, but does so in a philosophical manner by making experience subservient to the concept. His first counter-argument is that long-standing one from physiology, which briefly amounts to this: animals require nerves for **sensation**, therefore plants will also require **nerves for sensation**; but they **have none**. I was, admittedly, somewhat chagrined to find that everything with which I believed I had already settled this objection earlier has had no other result than its repetition on the part of a philosopher. But why not? Taken up by a philosopher, whose trade is, after all, the drawing of conclusions, the objection could gain entirely new strength—though, admittedly, it might also merely justify its own renewal. One must only excuse me if, in the face of his repetition, I now also repeat that which seems to me still to possess the old strength against it, if only to meet his new turns of argument with a few new turns of my own.

From the outset, I asserted against the objection that the conclusion upon which it rests cannot be valid, because otherwise one would have to conclude just as follows: Violins require strings to make a sound; therefore, flutes will also require strings to make a sound; but they have none, and therefore cannot make a sound; candles and wicks—

<meta>This page continues the author’s rebuttal against a philosophical opponent who argues that plants cannot have "souls" or sensations because they lack a nervous system. The author uses common technological and biological examples to demonstrate that the same function can be achieved through different structures.</meta>

lamps require wicks to burn, so gas lamps <note>original: "Gaslampen"; at the time of writing in the mid-19th century, gas lighting was a modern innovation compared to traditional oil lamps or candles.</note> will also require wicks to burn; but they have none, and therefore cannot burn. Yet flutes sound without strings, and gas lamps burn without wicks.

This has been called "playful analogies," and my philosophical opponent also speaks of the "frivolous play of analogy" in my reflections. But I wish one had rather seriously justified the logical conclusion <note>The "conclusion" refers to the opponent's assumption that because animals need nerves to feel, all living things must need nerves to feel.</note> which these analogies, in fact, playfully refute. For the plant, I think, no other principle of logic can be valid than for the flute and the lamp. To be sure, one is so accustomed in philosophy to seeing a sharp distinction made between the **organic** and the **inorganic** <term>Organischem und Unorganischem: the distinction between living matter (biology) and non-living matter (physics/chemistry)</term> that it can hardly be surprising to see a different logic applied to them. But the logic of the objection holds just as little in the organic realm as it does in the inorganic.

Mammals and birds breathe with lungs, and if one destroys these, they can no more breathe than animals can feel if one destroys their nerves. But is there therefore no breathing without lungs? On the contrary, there is breathing with gills, with the skin, and with the gut. If, however, fish and worms can breathe without lungs, while mammals and birds can only breathe with lungs, why should plants not also feel without nerves, while animals can only feel with nerves?

The fallacy <note>original: "Fehlschluß"</note> underlying the objection is this: Because **A** can only perform something with the help of **a**, then **B** also needs **a** to perform the same thing. — No; precisely because it is **B**, and not **A**, it needs **b**, and not **a** for that purpose. The animal feels with nerves; creatures that are constructed quite differently than animals can do something quite

...need something other than nerves for sensation. Why then must there be nerves for sensation everywhere, if there need not be lungs for breathing everywhere? What good are such "whys," the opponent retorts, which at most allow space for a hollow possibility, when no positive experience speaks for reality. One cannot attempt to prove reality with possibilities. "That through a similar argumentation (as Fechner adopts for the refutation of opposing arguments) we can also present the capacity for sensation in a stone, a planet, and any arbitrary thing as possible, is readily apparent." <note>This quote is likely from a contemporary critic who found Fechner's "panpsychism"—the idea that all things have a soul—to be logically reckless.</note>

And indeed, that we truly can do so proves how poor the counterarguments are, since not even the soul of a stone can be refuted by them. The refutation of objections is, however, in truth not intended to prove **reality** through possibilities, but rather to counter the objection of **impossibility**; positive arguments exist for positive proof; and I do indeed have positive arguments.

Yet there is, in fact, sensation without nerves. The plant-animals <term>Pflanzenthiere: a historical term for "zoophytes" like corals, sponges, or polyps, which were seen as occupying the border between the plant and animal kingdoms</term> have no nerves and need none for sensation. Why then should plants need them for that purpose; and instead of having all other similarities with plant-animals, why should they not also share this: that they need no nerves for it? Why should nerves suddenly return to them, after they have already been set aside in the approach to them <note>Fechner refers to the "scale of nature"; as one moves from complex animals down toward plants, certain organs like lungs or nerves disappear even while life functions continue.</note>, without sensation having been set aside? These "whys" are not so easily dismissed.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s logical rebuttal against critics who claim plants cannot have souls because they lack a nervous system. He uses the existence of "plant-animals" (zoophytes) to show that life and sensation can exist without the specific biological structures found in higher animals.</meta>

...can perform **a**, but **B** can perform the same thing without the help of **a**, then **C**—which is very similar to **B**—would only be able to perform the same thing with the help of **a**, or not at all. <note>Fechner is using a mock-logical formula here to show how his opponents' reasoning fails: if a creature (B) can feel without nerves (a), it is illogical to claim a similar creature (C/plants) must have nerves to feel.</note>
With such "fine" **empirical deductions** one fights against the **plant-soul**; and, however I may ponder it, it seems to me that the entire objection regarding the **nerves** ultimately boils down to this.

To be sure, the previous conclusion does not seem to have fully satisfied my philosophical opponent. But has he perhaps subsequently uncovered his error? On the contrary, we shall see how he makes it even worse.

The animals in question are generally very inconvenient for the opponents of the plant-soul; for they are sufficient on their own to invalidate almost all other objections against the plant-soul along with the objection regarding nerves. This is because these lower animals—with the exception of one single trait that is represented differently in plants—lack everything one might demand for the existence of a soul (based on an analogy with higher animals) just as much as plants do. If plants also agreed with these **plant-animals** <term>Pflanzenthiere: organisms like corals or sponges that appear plant-like but are classified as animals</term> in this one missing trait, they would actually be entirely animals—which they neither want to be nor should be.

For the point is not that plants should **tautologically** <note>original: *tautologisch*; meaning to repeat the same thing in a redundant way</note> repeat the **animal-soulfulness**, as people seem to demand in order to consider them ensouled at all—as if no other souls were possible besides human and animal souls. Rather, the point is that they join the **soul-kingdom** of animals as a complementary, different soul-kingdom. To this end, it is essentially required that they differ from animals in at least one trait that is significant to their particular way of being ensouled.

Let us look closer: the plant-animals, among which

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s logical rebuttal against critics who claim plants cannot have souls because they lack a nervous system. He uses the existence of "plant-animals" (zoophytes) to show that life and sensation can exist without the specific biological structures found in higher animals.</meta>

...we briefly summarize here as **polyps**, **infusoria** <note>original: *Infusorien*; a historical term for microscopic aquatic organisms found in infusions of organic matter</note>, and similar animals, largely possess neither nerves, nor central organs, nor a closed circulatory system; some do not even have a mouth or stomach. In contrast, plants possess almost everything these creatures have, with the exception of free **locomotion** <term>locomotion: the ability of an organism to move from one place to another</term>, though many **plant-animals** <note>original: *Pflanzenthiere*; organisms like corals or sea anemones that appear plant-like but are animals</note> also remain fixed in place and can only move their limbs freely. One might say a plant is nothing but a frozen **arm-polyp** <note>likely referring to a hydra or similar tentacled polyp</note>. But is it really frozen? On the contrary: while the polyp stretches its arms in all directions for prey and moves toward the light, the plant stretches out branches, leaves, and blossoms in all directions. It renews, lengthens, and multiplies these with inexhaustible vital force, rises against gravity, erupts on all sides in a creation of form and color, and pulls, turns, and twists itself toward the light, and sends its fragrance to other flowers. Could a plant carved from stone or wood do the same? The way in which this visibly signifies a supplement to animal and human life in the invisible realm will be considered later; but for now, it is enough to point out as much as is necessary to provide a counter-proof.

I say that these creatures, the **plant-animals**, are very inconvenient for the opponents of the **plant-soul**; but since the plant-soul is even more inconvenient to them, they must know how to eliminate the inconvenience, even if it is in the most inconvenient way. Let us see further how they go about it.

To get rid of the **plant-soul**, one might as well sacrifice a few animal souls. The polyp has no nerves; therefore, it probably has no soul either and feels nothing.

<summary>Fechner argues that plants exhibit active, purposeful growth and movement toward light, much like "plant-animals" (zoophytes). He mocks critics who would rather deny souls to simple animals than admit that plants might also possess sensation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument from the previous page, where he countered critics who dismiss the inner life of plants and "lower" animals. He shifts here to a direct quote from his earlier work to prove that biological simplicity does not equal a lack of consciousness.</meta>

“Since I foresaw this objection, I sought to meet it in advance in *Nanna* <note>original: "Ranna"; an OCR error for Fechner's 1848 book *Nanna, or the Soul-Life of Plants*</note> (p. 246 f.) as follows:

—What gives us a right to find merely doubtful traces of **soul** <term>soul: in this context, the capacity for sensation, feeling, or inner experience</term> in the **polyps**, **infusoria** <note>a historical term for microscopic organisms found in water</note> and other so-called ‘imperfect’—but fundamentally only simpler—animals?
