The great receptivity of these lower animals to various stimuli, the clear **power of discernment** <term>discernment: the ability to distinguish between different types of food or environments</term> which they possess for it, the liveliness and decisiveness of their movements, the specific direction which they give them for specific purposes, the character of **voluntary action** <term>voluntary action: movements that appear to be chosen rather than mechanical reflexes</term> perceptible therein, the decisive resistance with which they meet interventions in their natural life conditions, the struggle into which they fall even among themselves—all of this speaks entirely against an undecided, dull life of the senses that is still half-submerged in unconscious natural life.”

“Let us look more closely at the life phenomena of polyps, and it might be shown that the apparent lack of clarity in their soul in fact depends solely on the actual lack of clarity in our observation of them.”

“If an extended **freshwater polyp (Hydra)** is touched, or the water in which it is located is shaken, it suddenly contracts into a small lump; surely a sign of lively sensitivity. It follows the light, and if one places a glass with several polyps in it, after a while one finds them all hanging on the side facing the light. The polyp therefore has several kinds of **sensory perceptions**. It is tremendously gluttony, snatches greedily with its tentacles for prey, and two polyps often fight over the same prize. It chooses and distinguishes very specifically between its diet, in that it only consumes animal fare and rejects plant fare; even among animal fare, it makes distinctions, notably not seizing polyps of its own kind even if one lets it starve and allows these to fall onto its outstretched arms, whereas it seizes small animals that it likes to eat at the first touch. Here, a clear power of discernment is shown. (Trembley). <note>Abraham Trembley (1710–1784) was a naturalist famous for being the first to study the Hydra and its ability to regenerate</note>”

“What else does one have in the polyp, after all, than a

...is a **being with a well-developed sensory perception**, even if perhaps nothing more. One only has to look at the simplest and coarsest of men. Do they have less violent and decisive desires than the most civilized and educated? I think the facts cited speak for themselves, and the preceding interpretation of them is simply rational; my philosophical opponent also knows all this quite well, both from my writing and independent of it. But were he to grant **sensation** <term>sensation: (Empfindung) the internal conscious experience of a stimulus</term> to the polyp despite its **lack of nerves**, he would then be unable to deny it to plants because of their own lack of nerves. And since it is a fixed point for him that the plant has no sensation, it is just as "simply rational" to him that he regards all those signs not so much as evidence for the sensation of the polyp, but rather lets the lack of nerves count as evidence against it—even though he himself says that we do not know how nerves actually function to produce sensation.

It may be said: the polyp gives those signs of sensation; but the plant simply does not. Indeed, it does not give *these* specific signs, and the question remains whether it might not give others instead; I refer again to the positive arguments; but one...

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of a specific logical fallacy used by his opponent. On the previous page, the opponent argued that because we observe sensation tied to nerves in higher animals, we cannot assume sensation exists in plants which lack nerves. Here, the author uses a "reductio ad absurdum" (reducing the argument to absurdity) by applying that same logic to legs and movement.</meta>

...needs for sensation, does not need the falsehood of the general proposition; that one creature needs the same means for sensation as another; how can he at the same time prove that a creature needs the same means for it as he himself does?

But since, according to the opponent, we lose every **empirical** <term>empirical: (empirisch) based on observation or experience rather than theory</term> ground if we do not adhere to this **mode of reasoning** <term>mode of reasoning: (Schlußweise) the logical method used to reach a conclusion</term>, let us then adhere to it everywhere—even though it is certainly an invalid generalization, insofar as it was intended merely for the refutation of the **plant soul** <term>plant soul: (Pflanzenseele) the controversial 19th-century idea that plants possess an inner life or consciousness</term> and will otherwise certainly make no claim to validity.

according to which there can be no question of fish also being able to move through space, and the flight of birds could only occur by means of their legs.

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of his opponent's logic regarding the necessity of nerves for sensation. On the previous page, the author used the example of movement—noting that while dogs need legs to move, snakes do not—to illustrate that the same function can be achieved through different physical means. Here, he applies this logic more broadly to biological functions in plants.</meta>

Is it then any less palpable for the latter <note>Referring to the opponent's argument mentioned on the previous page.</note> after this?

The fallacy has now assumed this form: if A requires an *a* for a given performance, and B requires a *b* for the same performance, one must not assume that C requires a *c* for that same performance, but rather that it can only carry out this performance either with the help of *a* or *b*, or not at all. Otherwise, one loses all **empirical ground** <term>empirical ground: (empirischer Boden) a basis for knowledge derived from sensory experience and observation rather than pure theory</term>.

Yet further: in *Nanna* <note>Fechner’s 1848 book, *Nanna, or on the Soul-Life of Plants*, where he first laid out these arguments.</note> I pointed out that we have all the less reason to demand nerves for sensation in plants—as we do in animals—because we see so many other functions carried out in plants without nerves that are only possible in animals with the help of nerves. Thus, the processes of sensation simply form no exception to this rule. **Respiration**, **metabolism**, and **nutrition**—all of which occur in the animal only under the influence of the nerves—do not require them in the plant. The **organic connection** <term>organic connection: (organischer Zusammenhang) the structural and functional continuity that allows a living being to act as a whole</term>, to which the **unity of the soul** <term>unity of the soul: (Seeleneinheit) the philosophical idea that a being’s consciousness and experiences function as a single, integrated whole</term> is so essentially related, is mediated in the animal through the network of nerves, yet exists in the plant without any nerves at all. 

The ordered play of an **imponderable agent** <term>imponderable agent: (unwägbares Agens) a 19th-century term for invisible forces, such as electricity or magnetism, that were thought to have no measurable weight</term>, which one is accustomed to seeking in the nervous system of animals and viewing as the immediate foundation for the play of the soul (without, admittedly, knowing anything clear or decisive about it), cannot be lacking in the plant, given the powerful and ordered chemical processes within it that are unthinkable without electrical activity. If, then, the plant possesses everything that the animal only has through nerves—including everything one might associate with the work of nerves on behalf of the soul—what can justify the continued demand for nerves to explain sensation in the plant?

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s logical defense of his theory that plants possess a soul. Following the previous page’s discussion on how different animals achieve the same function (like movement) through different organs (like legs versus a snake's body), he now addresses the formal logical error made by his critics who insist that nerves are the only possible organ for sensation.</meta>

Only this conclusion: if A requires an *a* for factors *u, v, w, x, y, z*, but B does not require an *a* for *u, v, w, x, y*, one still assumes that it [B] requires an *a* for factor *z*, simply because A requires an *a* for it; otherwise, one loses all **empirical ground** <term>empirical ground: (empirischer Boden) a foundation for knowledge based on sensory observation and experience rather than pure abstract theory</term>.

A fundamental confusion underlies all these **fallacies** <term>fallacies: (Fehlschlüsse) errors in reasoning that lead to an invalid conclusion</term>: If A, according to the widest possible experience, has always been accompanied by *a*, we will indeed have reason, according to the method of **analogy** <term>analogy: (Analogie) a comparison between two things to highlight a similar feature</term>, to presuppose that wherever A occurs in the future, it will continue to be accompanied by *a*. Thus, wherever organic legs occur in the future, they will carry with them the capacity to move through space; where lungs occur, the capacity to breathe; and where nerves occur, the capacity to feel. 

However, just because A always occurs with *a*—the possession of legs with the capacity for **locomotion** <term>locomotion: (Locomotion) the ability to move from one place to another</term>—it does not follow in the least that *a* conversely occurs only with A—that the capacity for locomotion occurs only with the possession of legs, and so on. Rather, the previous examples prove through experience what is recognized in **logic** <term>logic: (Logik) the systematic study of valid inference</term>: the complete inadmissibility of such a reversal.

Even if I must fear renewing the accusation of "playful analogies," I nonetheless wish to set some others, in my own way, against the analogies of the philosopher <note>Fechner is likely referring to a specific contemporary critic or a materialist philosopher who argued that mental life is impossible without a nervous system.</note>.

Nature varies the form of the means for analogous functions in the most manifold ways, according to an arrangement that extends across the entire organic kingdom and all well-recognized functions of organic creatures, so that in distantly related creatures, they [the organs] no longer look similar at all. Respiration and locomotion provided examples; but the same applies to all organic functions, as far as we have insight into the means for them, and can thus be—

<summary>Fechner argues that just because humans and animals use nerves for sensation, it is a logical fallacy to assume sensation cannot exist without them. He highlights that Nature frequently uses different physical structures to achieve identical biological functions across different species.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s logical argument. He moves from a general discussion of logical fallacies to a specific comparison between biological organs and physical instruments. He argues that just as different instruments can produce the same type of vibration (sound or light), nature might use different "instruments" (not just nerves) to produce the vibrations necessary for sensation.</meta>

...also assume by analogy from those instances where we do have them for those where we do not; these are the functions upon which **sensation** <term>sensation: (Empfindung) the raw experience of the senses, which the author argues can exist in plants even without a central nervous system</term> depends. From this, we may suspect that nature has also varied the means for these functions within limits that are not too narrow, and will not have stopped merely at nerves. And another analogy comes to the aid of this one.

As little as we know about the essence of these functions, we can nonetheless—given the state of all circumstances and so many positive indications (such as the fact that the sensations of sound and light are stimulated by vibrations)—not doubt that it is some kind of ordered **vibrational processes** <term>vibrational processes: (Schwingungsvorgänge) rhythmic movements or oscillations; the author suggests that if consciousness is tied to physical motion, it is specifically these "ordered" vibrations</term> that underlie sensation in us. 

Now, however, ordered vibrational movements in general, whether they concern **ponderable or imponderable matter** <term>ponderable or imponderable: (Wägbares oder Unwägbares) a 19th-century distinction between physical matter with weight and "imponderable" substances like light, heat, or electricity, which were then thought to lack mass</term>, can be generated through the most varied **instrumentation** <term>instrumentation: (Instrumentation) here meaning the specific physical arrangement or apparatus used to create an effect</term>. Sound vibrations are produced not only by strings but also by flutes, bells, rods, cymbals, drums, the **siren** <note>original: "die Sirene"; here referring to a scientific instrument used to measure sound frequency by passing air through perforated disks</note>, and so on; in organic beings, they are produced through vibrating vocal cords, whirring wings, and in other ways. Light vibrations are produced not only by candles but also by lamps, torches, **pitch pans** <note>original: "Pechpfannen"; large metal baskets or pans used to burn pitch or tar for outdoor lighting</note>, glowing metals, electricity, and so on. And so we can further suspect that the same will be the case for the ordered vibrational processes—however they may be constituted—of ponderable or imponderable matter which underlie sensation; for, as far as our experience reaches, this is a general property of vibrational processes.

Both viewpoints support and complement each other. For, if the first viewpoint—that nature loves to vary the means for the same purposes—could still leave doubt as to whether such a variation regarding the generation of sensation-bearing processes...

<summary>Fechner argues that sensation relies on "ordered vibrations," and since sound and light vibrations can be produced by many different instruments, sensation might likewise be produced by structures other than nerves.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s logical argument regarding the existence of sensation in plants. He is responding to the objection that nerves might be the only "instrument" capable of supporting consciousness. He argues that since sensation depends on "vibrational processes," and physical vibrations can be produced by many different types of instruments, nature likely has more than one way to produce the physical basis of sensation.</meta>

Finally, the plant kingdom—along with the transition to it through the nerveless **zoophytes** <term>zoophytes: (Pflanzenthiere) literally "plant-animals"; a historical term for organisms like corals or sponges that seem to bridge the gap between the two kingdoms</term>—stands there as if perfectly made to represent in reality what we were led to suspect by the harmony of both viewpoints.

Now, opponents might say: "If nature is supposedly so eager to vary the means for the same functions, and if there are supposedly so many ways to produce sensation-bearing **vibrations** <term>vibrations: (Schwingungen) the rhythmic physical movements Fechner believes underlie all conscious experience</term>, why then has she stopped at only two kinds, namely animals and plants? Is there really a great difference in substituting 'two' for 'one'?" One sees that there are, in any case, limits here.

Undeniably so, and we wish to acknowledge them; however, since we have no means of determining them **prior to experience** <note>original: "a priori"; a philosophical term referring to knowledge that comes from theoretical deduction rather than observation</note>, we must look again to experience to see if it offers a hint for determining such limits. And lo and behold, we can find them in the same circle of experiences upon which we just relied, thereby further strengthening the previous **argument by analogy** <term>argument by analogy: (Analogieschluß) a logical move where one concludes that if two things are similar in some ways, they are likely similar in others</term>. In fact, even in the fields of **sound generation** and **light generation**, we find that among so many possible ways of production, there are only two of primary—indeed, on a large scale, of exclusive—application for their **instrumentation**. Each of these, however, is carried out through the greatest diversity of variations, and thus two main classes of instruments are determined for both sound and light, of which one class...

<summary>Fechner argues that just because nature has limits (like having only two kingdoms, plants and animals), it doesn't mean sensation is limited to nerves. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument by analogy. He is comparing the physical structures that produce sensation to musical instruments or lamps. Just as sound can be produced by visible strings or less visible air columns, he suggests that life force or sensation can be carried by visible nerves in animals or less visible structures in plants.</meta>

...shows the material carrier of the **vibrations** <term>vibrations: (Schwingungen) the rhythmic physical movements Fechner believes underlie all conscious experience</term> more clearly than the other; string instruments and wind instruments in the realm of sound, or **wick lamps** <note>original: "Dochtleuchten"; traditional lamps where a physical wick carries the flame</note> and gas lamps in the realm of light. And since animals, through the string-like or wick-like form of their nerves, show the most striking analogy to one of the main classes of these instruments, we cannot help but look for an analogy to the second main class, which has a less distinct carrier of vibrations. We can find this in the plant kingdom, and only in the plant kingdom. Why then should we not accept what we find here, especially after there was so much reason to search for it, and now even more so, since positive reasons will give us cause to demand its existence. For I do not mean that such considerations are strictly binding; however, conclusions that are not strictly binding are still preferable to those that are entirely false; and what does not constitute a proof on its own can still help lead to one.

But the opponent’s objection is not yet finished; let us allow it to complete itself, so that we might let it perish completely and, as far as it lies within our power, make it regret returning.

Perhaps the **polyp** <term>polyp: a simple aquatic animal (like a coral or sea anemone) that Fechner's contemporaries used as a test case for the presence of nerves</term> might have nerves that have simply been overlooked. — This cannot be denied; and even if it were the case, the necessity of nerves for a plant to have **sensation** <term>sensation: (Empfindung) the capacity for conscious feeling or perception</term> would not be proven any more clearly by it. But aside from the fact that it is somewhat problematic to base an empirical argument on what a polyp *might* have, it is also not very likely—given the scrupulous care with which nerves have been sought in polyps and related animals—that such things will still be found here; and I have (*_Zend-Avesta, Vol. I._ <note>original: "Zend-Avesta, Th. I."; referring to Fechner's own major work, *Zend-Avesta: Or on the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter* (1851). The "Th." stands for *Theil*, meaning Volume or Part.</note>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal to an opponent who argues that simple animals like polyps might actually possess nerves, or at least the potential for them, thereby maintaining a biological distinction between animals (with souls) and plants (without souls).</meta>

(p. 214) ...has emphasized, on the basis of thorough investigations, how the **nervelessness** of **polyps**, **infusoria** <term>infusoria: a historical term for microscopic organisms found in water; Fechner uses them as examples of life without a visible nervous system</term>, and generally nerveless animals, is intrinsically linked to the absence of muscles and the fact that the body consists of a peculiar, gap-filled, mesh-like, contractile tissue (**sarcode**) <term>sarcode: an early biological term for the jelly-like substance of simple animals, later known as protoplasm</term>. Based on this, one can hardly expect to find nerves in animals constructed in such a way. The opponent ignores this, asserting instead the following perspective: "The substance of the animal (the polyp) can contain the **nerve elements** in a germ-like manner, analogous to the fluid of an egg in higher animals, which also only deposit a real **nervous system** during further development. Thus, the nervelessness of those animals should not be taken in an absolute sense."

But how wonderful! The opponent himself hands us the weapons. Unquestionably, in a philosophical sense, one can see a **germ** <note>original: "Keim"; here meaning a latent potentiality or embryonic starting point</note> of anything that is *not* there within what *is* there, in order to prove something that isn't there or to refute something that is. In ordinary life, one certainly requires the **capability of development** <note>original: "Entwickelungsfähigkeit"</note> for something to be called a "germ"; indeed, I would believe that both concepts are correlates. But in **philosophy**, and for the purpose of refuting the **plant soul**, one can quite easily excuse the "germ" of nerves in the polyp from this requirement—or one can view the development of nerves in higher animals as a development of the same from the germs in the polyp, just so that the polyp might somehow have nerves and thereby distinguish itself from the plant, which has none. For against the plant soul, any weapon is good. Only now we shall also say: "the substance of the plant can contain the nerve elements in a germ-like manner, just as," etc.

<summary>Fechner critiques the argument that simple animals have "potential" nerves, pointing out that if one uses "germs" or latent potential to define animals, the same logic must be applied to plants.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s critique of a "philosophical opponent" (likely referring to a specific thinker like Schleiden or a general materialist view). Fechner is dismantling the argument that a nervous system is a prerequisite for having a soul or sensation. He focuses on the "polyp" (a simple aquatic organism) as a test case for whether nerves are truly necessary for life and feeling.</meta>

...then their **lack of nerves** would not be taken in an absolute sense.

In short, after the opponent first showed that—in order to remain on **empirical ground** <note>empirical ground: the basis of knowledge derived from observation and experience rather than pure theory</note>—we must not attribute a **soul** to the **plant** without nerves, and after leading us around this empirical ground in his own way, he finally happily lifts us above it with the concept of **germ-like nerves** <note>original: "keimartigen Nerven"; referring to the opponent’s idea that even if nerves aren't visible, they exist as a "germ" or potential within the organism</note> and gives us the freedom to think whatever we want. For our part, however, we would rather remain on that ground, and in the future walk upon it somewhat differently than the example of the philosophical opponent would lead us—an opponent whom I truly believe to be seduced by his own philosophy.

The summary of the preceding is this: the lack of nerves in plants cannot be used from an empirical standpoint against the **capacity for sensation** in plants, so long as one cannot empirically demonstrate nerves in the **polyp** <term>polyp: a simple, jelly-like aquatic animal, such as a hydra; in Fechner's time, these were key examples of organisms that behaved like animals but appeared to lack a nervous system</term> while still being unable to deny it sensation. However, attributing nerves to the polyp simply because it has sensation, or denying it sensation because it lacks nerves, is not a matter of experience. Even assuming that nerves were to be found in the polyp—which is possible, though not probable—this would still provide no proof that they are also necessary for sensation beyond the polyp, in plants. Rather, the invalidity of such a **conclusion by analogy** <note>analogy: the logic that because two things are similar in one way (lacking nerves), they must be similar in another (lacking sensation)</note> can be demonstrated tangibly, and can be countered with a more valid, much more general conclusion by analogy.

Let us now turn to the second, more inherently philosophical objection of the philosophical opponent.

<summary>Fechner argues that his opponents are logically inconsistent by demanding visible nerves as proof of a soul in plants while inventing "invisible" nerves for simple animals like polyps. He concludes that the absence of a nervous system does not empirically prove a lack of sensation.</summary>

"Are then," he asks, "the plants individual beings, closed within themselves in the same way that animals are? — This question is the **fundamental question** for the decision regarding their **capacity for sensation**. <note>capacity for sensation: the ability to feel or perceive (German: Empfindungsfähigkeit)</note> That plants, despite the organic elements of which they consist, are not such individuals is shown in their entire internal structure and organization just as much as in all their life processes; it emerges above all in their indeterminate continued growth, in which the plant body—albeit according to a constant schema—drives new limbs out of itself without a definite measure, while dying off on the other side. It thus does not regenerate as a whole, so that it is perpetually engaged in a state of arising and passing away, without this 'becoming' being unified in one and the same **subject**." <note>subject: here meaning a single, unified center of experience or a permanent "self"</note> And in an earlier writing: "Despite its internal independence, the plant is nevertheless still grown together with the earth. "I reply: 1) That, insofar as one can speak of a 'closure' <note>original: "Abschluss"; referring to the state of being a self-contained, finished individual</note> of organisms at all—though given their **exchange of matter and interaction** <term>exchange of matter and interaction: Fechner uses "Stoff- und Wirkungswechsel," a 19th-century term for metabolism and the constant energetic exchange between an organism and its environment</term> with the outside world, there can be no talk of a material or functional closure—the objection is factually invalid, insofar as every plant presents itself to the rest of nature with just as individually closed a form, character, and conduct of its life process, distinguishes itself from nature, and confronts other creatures just as much as any animal does; so that if this were truly the fundamental—

2) That even if the plant lacks a final "stopping point" in its growth, its individual boundary against the outside world is by no means dissolved. The sharpness with which I have sought to highlight these consequences is, in fact, directed not against a person, but against a cause. My opponent does not step outside the philosophical school to which he belongs with his way of reasoning, but rather represents it only too well. <term>Hegelian philosophy</term> <note>The school of G.W.F. ### VI.

## The Plant Soul. Positive Arguments in Its Favor.

And are there no better arguments against the plant soul? There are plenty of others, which I believe I have already sufficiently illuminated in my earlier writing <note>Fechner likely refers here to his 1848 book *Nanna, or the Soul-Life of Plants*.</note>. But they all collapse of their own accord when the positive arguments prevail. Let us turn, therefore, to the positive arguments.

What, however, is actually to be proven when I seek to prove the existence of a plant soul? First and foremost, nothing is "proven" in an absolute sense; it is merely made more plausible than its opposite. What I mean by the <term>plant soul</term> <note>German: *Pflanzenseele*. For Fechner, this meant that plants possess a form of subjective experience, sensation, and "inner life," even if they lack the self-conscious "spirit" of humans.</note> I have already stated on page 10, and let it be considered stated once more—especially since, despite the very specific explanations I provided in my earlier writing on the subject, I have been accused by more than one party of speaking of an "indeterminate thing" when I speak of the plant soul. It is, of course, an indeterminate thing as long as one has not defined it for oneself.

Then again, it has been said that I could have made it easier for myself to prove the plant soul if I had chosen to define it differently—as if the goal were to prove a mere word, rather than the very thing I understand the plant soul to be.

For the sake of a clearer explanation, I will...

<summary>Fechner dismisses the remaining negative arguments against his theory and begins his positive defense of the "plant soul," emphasizing that his goal is to establish its plausibility rather than provide an absolute proof.</summary>
<keywords>plant soul, Gustav Theodor Fechner, panpsychism, positive arguments, philosophical methodology, Beseelung</keywords>

...positive grounds for the existence of a plant soul under six arguments, which I designate as those of:
    1) similarity or <term>analogy</term> <note>Fechner relies heavily on the idea that if plants behave similarly to animals in some ways, they may share similar internal experiences.</note>,
    2) supplementation,
    3) gradation,
    4) connection,
    5) causality,
    6) teleology <note>Teleology is the study of purpose or design in nature; Fechner argues that the purposeful structure of plants implies an underlying soul-life.</note>,
of which the second and third, united under a common argument of diversity, can be contrasted with the first; meanwhile, the last one, from a certain point of view, can be included within or subordinated to the first, rather than being coordinated with it.

Please note that none of these arguments stands on its own; rather, they must be viewed in connection with one another and alongside the refutation of opposing arguments. One might well break a single stick from the bundle; but let one try to break the cohesion of the whole. <note>Fechner invokes the classic "bundle of sticks" (or *fasces*) metaphor to argue that his evidence is cumulative and strongest when taken together.</note> In contrast, critics have hitherto found it easier to pull out individual sticks or simply to discard the bundle entirely.

In this, the physical will be designated by uppercase letters, and the corresponding mental or <term>psychic</term> <note>German: *Psychische*. For Fechner, this refers to the inner, subjective side of existence—sensation and feeling—which he believes exists in parallel with physical processes.</note> aspects by the corresponding lowercase Latin letters; so that if A and B represent two physical systems or physical processes...

The significance of all the arguments presented here essentially extends through the entire question of the soul; however, their application to the plant soul is the simplest example of their use.

## 1) The Argument of Similarity or Analogy.

This argument, both unconsciously and consciously, guides us most in our belief in the souls of our fellow human beings and animals. We infer the existence of their souls—of which we can perceive nothing directly—from the similarity of their bodies and their physical expressions to our own.

But now, plants are actually as dissimilar to us as possible, rather than similar; therefore, this should instead speak against them having a soul. So it seems at first, and so it is mostly understood.

But is a worm not also dissimilar to us in many important respects? Yet we believe in its soul, while we do not believe in the soul of a statue, which in certain ways is much more similar to us than the worm is. <note>Fechner is pointing out a logical trap: a statue has a face, arms, and legs like a human, yet we know it is inanimate; a worm has almost no outward human features, yet we acknowledge it is alive and sensing.</note> Ultimately, we must admit that not every similarity to ourselves and animals can prove the existence of a soul, just as no dissimilarity can prove its absence; rather, it is a matter of searching for the essential signs of the soul within the physical and finding a <term>principle</term> <note>In this context, a "principle" (Princip) is a fundamental rule or criteria used to determine if a physical form is capable of supporting a soul.</note> for it.

To find such a principle—this is a beautiful task for philosophy, as the establishment of principles in this field is among its primary duties. Here lies...

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s argument regarding the "Argument of Similarity." Having previously noted that outward appearance (like having a face) isn't a reliable metric for having a soul, he now pivots to a "fundamental question": what are the actual physical signs that indicate an internal life?</meta>

...the fundamental question. Now look around in all of today's philosophy to see if one finds an answer—a clear, useful, neat, applicable answer to the question. Since I find none within it, I seek it myself and provide the following: I am giving it only halfway at first, so one should not immediately find fault with just this half.

Based on experience and reason, I say, we must regard as the most essential signs of the <term>soul's existence</term> <note>Fechner uses "Seelendasein" to describe the presence or manifestation of a soul within a physical body.</note> within the physical those instances where the physical repeats and mirrors the most essential conditions of the soul itself—insofar as the physical is capable of repeating and mirroring the soul’s conditions at all. For, whatever fundamental view one may hold regarding the relationship between soul and body, both must exist together <term>here on earth</term> <note>Fechner uses the word "hienieden" (here below), a common 19th-century term for earthly life as opposed to the celestial or afterlife.</note>, acting with and through one another; thus, they must harmonize in their conditions and be adapted to one another. In fact, however, despite all the differences between soul and body, there are highly important determinations and relationships that are comparable in both—or even common to both—and which are found harmoniously in all undoubtedly ensouled creatures, humans and animals alike.

The body of a soul is, like the soul itself, a whole bound together in form and content; it is individually self-contained, standing relatively independent against similar but not identical beings. Under external stimulation and co-determination, it determines and unfolds itself from within, giving birth to an inexhaustible <term>multiplicity</term> <note>Fechner refers to "Mannichfaltigkeit," a key philosophical concept used to describe a vast diversity of parts or states that are nevertheless held together in a single unity.</note> of effects—governed by laws from one side, yet unpredictably new from another—out of its own abundance and <term>creative power</term> <note>The original German "Schöpferkraft" suggests an inherent vitality or agency within the living organism.</note>. It encompasses relationships of coordination, superiority, and subordination, and amidst continuous...

<summary>Fechner argues that for a soul to exist in a body, the physical structure must "mirror" the characteristics of a soul, such as unity, internal agency, and complex organization. He begins to define the body as a self-contained entity that unfolds from within while interacting with the outside world.</summary>

Not only does the human body harmonize in all these points with the essential conditions of its soul, but despite all the differences between animals—and between animals and humans—all animals share these similarities between the body and the soul. Meanwhile, these qualities are absent in stone, glass, waves, or air, which is precisely why one cannot be prompted to attribute a soul to them.

The essential relationship between these signs and the <term>soul’s existence</term> <note>German: *Seelendasein*. Fechner is looking for physical indicators that "prove" a soul is present in a material form.</note> is fully proven by the fact that our bodies and those of animals possess these signs only as long as the soul dwells within them; conversely, the body loses them when it loses the soul. Furthermore, as these signs vanish, the soul vanishes from the body. This relationship of <term>interdependence</term> <note>German: *Bedingtheit*. This suggests a reciprocal necessity where the soul needs the body to manifest, and the body needs the soul to maintain its specific organized form.</note>, combined with the previously mentioned relationship of similarity, can be expressed briefly by saying that these physical signs not only mirror, but also carry the most general and essential properties of the soul.

In the combination of these signs (though certainly not in the individual ones) lies an indication of the soul’s existence that is of much more general significance than the existence of a nervous system, a central organ, a closed circulatory system, or specially constructed sense organs. These anatomical features are merely specific forms in which those general and essential signs of the soul's existence are expressed in the body. However, they do not exclude the possibility of other forms, and the idea that they are universally necessary for the soul's existence is already refuted by the fact that they are not even found universally within the animal kingdom. Consequently, they can only...

referred to specific forms or stages of the soul’s existence, rather than being viewed as essential signs of the soul's existence in general, and should not be required everywhere. For it stands to reason that the general nature of those signs must account for the general existence of the soul, while their specific expression accounts only for the specific expression of that soul.

Our argument, in short, concludes that a soul exists in a natural body not simply from its resemblance to our own or an animal’s body—nor based on arbitrarily chosen signs—but from its similarity in those conditions and relationships by which a body is similar to its own soul; those qualities which it possesses and loses alongside the soul. It claims the general signs for the general existence of the soul, and the specific forms of these signs for the specific forms of the soul’s existence. 

It is therefore not merely the <term>analogy</term> <note>Fechner uses "analogy" here to mean a structural or functional correspondence between two different things.</note> of one body with another, but also the <term>analogy of the body</term> with the soul that grounds our <term>inference by analogy</term> <note>German: *Analogieschluß*. Now, however, the <term>plant body</term> stands equal to the human and animal body in all those conditions and relationships that can be related to the general <term>existence of a soul</term> <note>German: *Seelendasein*. Fechner argues that if the fundamental "rules" of life are present, the soul must be too.</note>; it differs from them only in those conditions that relate to specific forms or levels of the soul's existence. Thus, there is reason to believe that a soul belongs to the plant just as much as to the human and the animal—it is simply a different form or level of soul.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s systematic defense of the "plant-soul." ...which speak in favor of it having such a thing. Let us now take a look.

The awakening and being-awake of the <term>soul</term> <note>German: *Seele*. In Fechner’s philosophy, this refers to the internal, subjective experience of any living being.</note> has two sides: first, the internal process of becoming conscious and the inner workings of consciousness; second, the opening of the soul toward the outside and its interaction with the external world. Of the first side, there are no externally visible signs in the physical body, except subsequently through the signs of the second side; the second side, however, can be mirrored in an opening of the body's organs toward the external world and in their free interaction with it. In humans and animals, the soul is not only mirrored in this interaction but is also bound to it and sustained by it.

As the most essential signs of an awakening in creatures—those which display the aforementioned general signs of a predisposition for a waking <term>soul-life</term> <note>German: *Seelenleben*.</note>—we can accordingly regard the following: not that they open animal-like eyes and eyelids (for those are specific, specialized forms of awakening), but rather that they emerge from a state in which they were secluded from interaction with external <term>sensory stimuli</term> <note>German: *Sinnesreizen*.</note> and other creatures. In that secluded state, they were either just beginning to form the organs for such interaction or were renewing exhausted strength; they leave this state by breaking through some kind of shell or opening some kind of closure to enter into a relationship with the sensory stimuli of life and into general vital interaction. The plant, however, shows these most essential signs of awakening not just once and in one way, but even twice and in a twofold manner: once and for all in the breaking of the <term>seed coat</term> <note>German: *Saamenhülle*.</note> and its germination from the earth, and secondly in the repeated opening of the buds.

Twice; but is that not too much? So it will likely

...not mean an actual awakening. — One must be prepared for every objection.

But we also see animals awakening twice, only in a different form: once from the embryonic state, and later repeatedly from sleep; the only difference being that the animal always opens the same eyes, while the plant always opens new "eyes"—susceptible to the <term>stimulus of light</term> <note>German: *Lichtreiz*. Fechner views the opening of leaves or blossoms as a functional equivalent to the opening of eyes.</note>.

This is a formal difference, which is subordinate to the more general difference between the plant and the animal: that the plant generally adds the later developmental forms of its structure and life to the continued existence of the earlier ones, extensively expanding and increasing them; whereas the animal <term>sublates</term> <note>German: *aufhebt*. This difference, however, does not concern a feature essential to the existence of a <term>soul-life</term> <note>German: *Seelenleben*.</note>, although it may be suited to serve as the expression and foundation of a different form of it. I will return to this later.

The cited signs of awakening in the plant would, of course, mean little if they occurred abstractly on their own, instead of in connection with and as a consequence of the general signs of a <term>soul-predisposition</term> <note>German: *Seelenanlage*. The inherent capacity or structural potential for consciousness.</note>. We can open many capsules, or unfold many closed things, without a soul awakening as a result. But now, the previously discussed general signs of a soul-predisposition culminate specifically in this, during the course of the development of the germ—the plant. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on how physical forms represent the internal life of the soul. He is contrasting "hollow" symbols—abstract or artificial structures—with "full" symbols, which are living organisms that not only represent but also embody the soul's processes.</meta>

...my signs of **soul-disposition** <term>soul-disposition</term> <note>German: *Seelenanlage*. This refers to the inherent potential or underlying structure required for a soul to exist and develop.</note> in the **house**, the **artificial bud**, have not preceded and accompanied it in the same manner as they do with the **awakening plant**. This constitutes the difference between a hollow, empty **symbol** and a full one of the **awakening soul**—that is, a symbol which simultaneously carries the **awakening soul** it reflects.

This viewpoint will become important for us later. For every individual **property** and **relationship** of the **invisible soul**, sensory **symbols** can be found which mirror them in the **visible** world. **Nature**, **art**, **mathematics**—indeed, what does not offer such things? But are they full or empty **symbols**? The question will always be whether they, along with the specific property in question, simultaneously mirror those other properties belonging to the general **characteristics** of **soul-existence** and **soul-life**. 

The mathematical **symbol** of a **circle** is very well suited to mirror many important relationships of the soul for us: the enclosure of its own **content**, the exclusion of the **content** of other souls, the unified connection of the total **content**, the **dual direction** from **within** to **without** and from **without** to **within** (the **dual direction** in which the **radii** can be followed), and so forth; but nothing stirs within the circle, nothing changes; it effects nothing, develops nothing, has no **periodicity** <note>German: *Periodicität*. In Fechner's thought, the rhythmic cycles of life (like waking and sleeping) are essential markers of a living soul.</note>; it is a hollow symbol. 

With the human, animal, and **plant body**, all this is different; they participate in everything essential that characterizes the life of a soul, each only in its own way, and they participate in their awakening as part of this; they are full symbols. Furthermore, we would be unable to point to any **body** on our earth to which the characteristics of a full **symbol** would apply; and this certainty with which the **plant**, according to a thoroughly **rational principle**, [provides] the full **sym-** <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("Symbol") at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>Fechner distinguishes between "hollow" symbols (like mathematical circles) and "full" symbols (living bodies). While abstract shapes can represent soul-concepts, only living organisms possess the movement and periodicity necessary to truly embody an awakening soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of how different philosophical schools—Materialism and Idealism—attempt to identify the presence of a soul. He is arguing that plants, like animals, transition from a dormant state (the seed/embryo) into a "waking life" characterized by a purposeful interaction with the environment, making them "full symbols" of the soul rather than mere biological machines.</meta>

...symbols <note>The text here completes the thought from the previous page regarding mathematical symbols like the circle.</note>, while simultaneously excluding all other earthly bodies in such a definite way—that means something.

Do the **Materialists** or **Idealists** have a more valid principle for distinguishing "full" symbols from "empty" ones? Do they even have one at all? And is it not necessary to have one, if one wishes to take even a single step in the **question of the soul** <term>question of the soul</term> <note>German: *Seelenfrage*. A central theme in 19th-century German philosophy concerning whether plants, animals, or the Earth itself possess consciousness.</note> or to judge any perspective on it? Among the **Materialists**, amidst all their talk of **force** and **matter**, I can find no other principle than to see an empty symbol wherever there is no brain, **nervous system**, or something similar. And the **Idealists** have so little of a principle—even one that satisfies only themselves—that, as we have seen, when they must finally move beyond the vagueness of their concepts, they even borrow the materialists' principle from their own enemies; and this is, as has been shown, the simple principle of a **fallacy** <note>Fechner argues that Idealists, despite claiming the primacy of spirit, end up relying on physical brain-matter as the only proof of a soul, just like the Materialists they oppose.</note>.

The signs of the **plant's** transition into a **waking life** then continue into the essential signs of waking life itself, by which the plant distinguishes itself from the sleeper and the **embryo** no less than the waking animal does.

1) The entire **vital arrangement** <term>vital arrangement</term> <note>German: *Lebenseinrichtung*. This refers to the physiological and structural makeup of a living organism.</note> and posture of the growing and flowering plant is purposefully calculated for the reception of the same stimuli that maintain the waking state in animals by exciting sensation; it also enters into just as lively an interaction with them. In contrast, the entire arrangement and posture of the sleeper and the embryo is just as purposefully calculated for closure against the stimuli that could awaken sensation.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that plants exhibit the characteristics of "waking life" rather than a permanent state of sleep or embryonic dormancy. He is specifically comparing the plant's active engagement with its environment to the behaviors of animals, contrasting both against the isolation of a sleeper or an embryo.</meta>

...other waking creatures of the earth find, but also enters into a **living interaction** with them, whereas the sleeper and the **embryo** are as much as possible closed off from all interaction with other waking creatures. Notably, the **flower** and the **insect** emerge as equal factors in a living **reciprocal relationship** <note>original: "Wechselverhältnisses". Fechner highlights the symbiosis between plants and insects as proof of a shared "waking" consciousness and mutual purpose.</note>, for which there is no analogy in the relationship between a waking and a sleeping being.

3) The **plant** strives no less than the **animal**—even like the latter by bypassing and overcoming obstacles—for the most favorable external conditions of life and stimulation, adjusted according to the change of circumstances through an outward-directed impulse; and it flees the unfavorable ones. There is only a formal difference between the two: the animal, which is not rooted, runs in all directions and reaches with limbs formed once and for all toward whatever it needs to obtain; the plant, which is rooted once and for all, extends itself in all directions with an unrestricted, free **driving force** <term>driving force</term> <note>German: *Triebkraft*. This refers to the inherent vital impulse that directs plant growth toward resources like light and water.</note> and pushes out new limbs in every direction where there is something for it to gain. The animal moves through **locomotion**; the plant withdraws from unfavorable spots by the "withering away" or retreating of its shoots. In contrast, the embryo—entirely enclosed in a shell and folded as tightly as possible—shows nothing analogous to either, but only a uniform development within this shell proceeding to a fixed limit. Likewise, the sleeper presents the opposite conditions to those mentioned above.

4) The plant shares with the animal the **sexual process**, in which the animal moves furthest from sleep and the embryonic state, manifesting the strongest sensations and **psychic drives** <term>psychic drives</term> <note>German: *psychische Triebe*. Fechner argues that reproductive processes are the peak of conscious "soul-life" in both plants and animals.</note>.

<summary>Fechner argues that plants are "awake" because they actively interact with insects and adaptively grow toward resources, unlike embryos or sleepers who are closed off from the world. 5) After breaking through its **seed coats**, the plant shows—in its roots with their branches on one hand, and the stem with its branches, leaves, and blossoms on the other—a contrast of organs parallel to that of the animal. Some organs are sunk into the dark depths, closed off from external life-stimuli, and serve only for nutrition; others are immersed in the realm of light and air, intended for interaction with **sensory stimuli** <term>sensory stimuli</term> <note>German: *Sinnesreize*. Fechner argues that plants react to their environment in a way that suggests a form of consciousness or feeling similar to animal sensation.</note>. In animals, these latter organs are likewise destined to draw **sensation** <term>sensation</term> <note>German: *Empfindung*. In Fechner's psychophysics, this refers to the subjective experience resulting from physical stimulus.</note> from this interaction, whereas in the embryo and the sleeper, all organs are as uniformly as possible sealed off from sensory stimuli.

Yet, while the plant appears so entirely analogous to the waking animal after the breakthrough of the seed coats and the opening of the buds, it appeared just as analogous to the sleeper and the embryo beforehand.

If one surveys the totality of these circumstances, it indeed seems difficult to say upon what foundation rests the ubiquitous comparison—so often used to deny **sensation** in plants—of the entire life of a plant with a state of sleep or embryonic life. For the plant offers not only the most characteristic signs of having left such a state, but also displays signs afterward that are entirely opposed to it, just as it offered analogous ones before.

This is not the case; rather, the plant and the animal run parallel to each other in both states, while they complement each other in the way they lead their waking lives—a perspective <term>perspective</term> <note>German: *Gesichtspunct*. Fechner is critiquing the view that plant movements or reactions are just "faking" or imitating "real" animal behavior.</note> or empty foreshadowing of what takes place in the other kingdom. But if one asks oneself impartially what makes these signs more hollow and empty than the corresponding ones in animals and humans, one finds nothing but the hollowness and emptiness of the assumption itself.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal to critics who claim that attributing a soul to plants is a purely arbitrary or unscientific endeavor. He defends his method of using empirical observation and analogy against both pure empiricists and abstract philosophers.</meta>

...says,*) that it will always remain arbitrary which phenomena I choose to count as essential to soulfulness <term>soulfulness</term> <note>German: *Beseelung*. In Fechner’s work, this refers to the state of possessing an internal, subjective experience or consciousness.</note>, that the essential signs of the same cannot be discovered through empirical abstraction <term>empirical abstraction</term> <note>The process of deriving general principles or "signs" from physical observations.</note>, and that the analogy <term>analogy</term> <note>Fechner’s primary method: arguing that if plants share physical structures or behaviors with humans/animals, they likely share internal experiences as well.</note> of humans and animals to other beings derived only from empirical abstraction could therefore lead to nothing substantial. I only admit this insofar as there is no reason <term>reason</term> <note>German: *Vernunft*. Here, Fechner implies that data collection without logical, rational framework is directionless.</note> found in empirical abstraction. But I admit even less that reason *without* empirical abstraction is able to achieve anything in this question—not even with all the preconceived notions about "the essence of being besouled," the "moving energy of the organic unity," and so forth, which have been missed from a philosophical standpoint in my treatment of the question.**) I consider the aforementioned signs—abstracted from experience according to a rational point of view, and which completely disregard such indefinite, fluctuating concepts—to be the most certain ones available thus far. One must therefore hold to them until such a time as one perhaps knows how to establish even more exact points of view.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For indeed, I do not represent them as being exact myself. The grouping of these signs cannot be established with such strict certainty, their scope cannot be so sharply defined, and specifically, the conclusion drawn from them cannot be proven through direct experience in the way that exact science would demand; yet, in the connection <note>German: *Nexus*.</note> of those signs, there lies more—even while acknowledging that they do not possess all these qualities, or at least not to the full degree—than in everything that an a priori philosophy on the one hand and empirical physiology on the other has offered for this purpose.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his methodology, specifically addressing the criticism that his belief in plant souls relies too heavily on analogies. He argues that his critics use the exact same logic of analogy to deny plant souls, but do so less rigorously than he does.</meta>

...let one not forget that this argument is only the first of six.

In almost every review of my doctrine on the **question of the soul** <term>question of the soul</term> <note>German: *Seelenfrage*. This refers to the philosophical and scientific debate over which beings (humans, animals, or plants) possess an internal, subjective consciousness.</note>, I have had to hear the reproach that I base my arguments solely or almost solely on **analogies** <term>analogies</term> <note>Fechner’s primary method: the idea that if two things are physically similar in certain ways, they likely share internal experiences as well.</note>; whereas the following arguments do indeed keep analogy in reserve and even draw upon analogies for support—because without them nothing can be achieved in the question of the soul at all, indeed no ground can be gained—but they by no means establish the main point of view through them; in the two following arguments, the main point is instead established through the perspective of the *difference* between plants and animals.

And on what else do the opponents themselves base their claims, if not on analogies, when they consider their fellow human beings and animals to be **besouled**, and on what else when they consider plants *not* to be besouled? Are these not analogies: "animals need nerves for **sensation** <term>sensation</term> <note>German: *Empfindung*. The basic capacity to feel or perceive, which Fechner argues is present in plants even without a nervous system.</note>, therefore all creatures do"? Should a **polyp** <note>A simple aquatic organism, like a sea anemone. In 19th-century biology, polyps were often used as "borderline" examples to test the limits of what constitutes an animal versus a plant.</note> be able to feel without nerves, then all creatures feeling without nerves must be constructed like polyps. The plant is grown together with the mother like an **embryo** <note>Fechner is critiquing a common contemporary view that plants are "stuck" in a primitive, fetal stage of development and therefore lack independent consciousness.</note>, and otherwise resembles the embryo (although it resembles the opposite of it much more), and will therefore also resemble it in its lack of sensation. For better or worse, the opponents must themselves resort to analogies in order to refute my analogies. The question of the soul simply has no other foundation. 

Now, however, it seems to me that my use of analogy in this matter is somewhat more cautious, circumspect, and refined than that of my opponents; it possesses more of a guiding principle and holds more firmly to the general **point of view** <term>point of view</term> <note>German: *Gesichtspunct*. The conceptual framework used to interpret scientific data.</note> that matters. With all due modesty, that is how it seems to me.

<summary>Fechner defends his use of analogy as a necessary scientific tool, arguing that his critics also rely on analogies to deny plant consciousness, but do so with less logical rigor.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of the arguments used to deny consciousness to plants. He argues that his opponents rely on the very same method of analogy they criticize him for, but they do so with far less evidence. He begins to summarize his core argument: that the shared structural and functional patterns between plants, animals, and humans point toward a shared internal experience.</meta>

The opponents undoubtedly reply: for us, analogy serves merely to clarify and support a general view which we have formed from more general grounds regarding the **soul-lessness** <term>soul-lessness</term> <note>German: *Seelenlosigkeit*. The philosophical position that plants lack any internal, subjective experience or consciousness.</note> of the plant. — I have no objection to this, if only it actually served that purpose. Fechner’s central concept that plants possess a form of "soul" or consciousness, albeit different from that of animals.</note>—then this sufficiently characterizes the validity of the general view itself, which those analogies are intended to clarify and support.

Let us finally summarize the argument: The plant shares with the human and the animal a unified individual **organizational and life-plan** <note>Fechner refers to the *Organisationsplan* and *Lebensplan*—the idea that a living being is not just a collection of parts, but is built and functions according to a holistic blueprint.</note>, which we have reason to regard as the expression and carrier of the unified individual **soul-plan** <term>soul-plan</term> and **soul-life** <term>soul-life</term> <note>German: *Seelenleben*. This refers to the inner, subjective world of an organism, including sensations and feelings.</note> in humans and animals themselves. This is because the conditions of the body only mirror the most essential conditions of the soul, and they exist and perish along with the soul. However, considering what we find in the **embryo** and the sleeping human, these physical structures alone might still leave room for doubt as to whether they establish a truly waking soul-life, or rather merely a precondition, a contributing factor, or a predisposition for one. But the plant now further shares with humans and animals those signs with which the awakening of soul-life is associated in them; finally, it also shares with them those signs with which the very wakefulness of soul-life itself is associated, in that the variations existing in this regard from the animal to the plant are only the...

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s defense of plant consciousness. The opening sentence completes a thought from the previous page, arguing that differences in physical structure between plants and animals are merely matters of external form and do not negate the presence of an internal "soul-life."</meta>

...concern the form, and not the essential expression of the **soul-life** <term>soul-life</term> <note>German: *Seelenleben*. Fechner’s term for the internal, subjective experience of a being, encompassing both consciousness and unconscious mental processes.</note>.

### **Formula for the Argument from Analogy.*)**

If the visible **A** is admittedly accompanied by an invisible **a**, then we are entitled to conclude that the visible **B** is also accompanied by an invisible **b**, provided that **B** resembles **A** in the totality of essential points in which the visible **A** resembles the invisible **a** itself—points which **A** possesses and loses simultaneously with **a**. The plant-like **B**, however, resembles the animal-like **A** not only in the entire context of essential points reflecting the existence of **a** in general, but specifically in those points that mirror and support the awakening and wakefulness of **a**. From this, we must conclude that the plant-like **B** is no less entitled to an awakening and wakeful **b**. In short: insofar as **A**, **a**, and **B** are given in their existence according to the stated relationship, the existence of **b** belonging to **B** follows from this by logical **inference** <note>German: *Schluß*. Here, Fechner is using formal logic to argue that if plants share the physical markers of "wakefulness" with animals, they must also share the internal experience of it.</note>.

Since we will frequently make use of schematic-symbolic representations for clarification in the following sections, I consider it useful to interject some general remarks regarding them.

I distinguish between **schema** <term>schema</term> and **symbol** <term>symbol</term> insofar as a **schema** is an abbreviated or illustrative representation of given primary relationships in the realm of the physical, intended to facilitate their comprehension (for example, representing the posture of a human by a vertical line and that of an animal by a horizontal line), without regard for whether something spiritual is mirrored by it...

---

*) Regarding this and future formulas, recall what was stated on page 47 concerning the meaning of the uppercase and lowercase letters. <note>In Fechner's system, uppercase letters (A, B) typically represent the physical, visible world, while lowercase letters (a, b) represent the corresponding internal, psychical states.</note>

<summary>Fechner presents a formal logical "Formula for the Argument from Analogy" to prove plant consciousness and begins defining his terminology for "schematic" versus "symbolic" representations.</summary>
<keywords>analogy, logic, soul-life, wakefulness, schema, symbol, physical vs. psychical, formal argument</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s discussion on the methodology of his argument. He is defining the technical distinction between a "schema" (a structural outline) and a "symbol" (a sensory representation), which he uses to bridge the gap between physical observation and spiritual inference.</meta>

**symbol**, on the other hand, is a spatial, physical, or generally sensory representation, insofar as it reflects something **spiritual** <term>spiritual</term> <note>German: *Geistiges*. In Fechner’s work, this refers to the mental, internal, or soulful aspects of existence, as opposed to the "physical" or "material."</note> (e.g., the circle in the sense mentioned above, p. 56), without regard to whether it also carries it. However, the schema of given primary relationships of an organic body may, under certain circumstances, be suitable to serve simultaneously as a symbol for the corresponding primary relationships of its **soul** <note>German: *Seele*. Fechner argues that the structure of a body (schema) can mirror the structure of its consciousness (symbol).</note>, provided that it reflects them at the same time; and a symbol may carry spirit or not carry it, according to which I call it a **full** or an **empty** symbol. It lies in the nature of things that schemata can only be **empty symbols**, which do not carry the spiritual content they reflect, but are often useful for facilitating the understanding of **full symbols** by illustratively highlighting the primary relationships of their spiritual content.

One can call all use of schemata and symbols "child’s play," and one will certainly make a game of it if the serious point of view underlying it has not been grasped, and if one does not keep it clearly and seriously in mind. But the utility of a well-targeted schematic representation of physical relationships is already recognized in **natural science** <term>natural science</term> <note>German: *Naturwissenschaft*. Fechner, a physicist by training, often seeks to apply the rigorous methods of the hard sciences to the study of the soul.</note>. As far as the symbolic representation of spiritual relationships is concerned, it naturally finds no place in natural science because the latter, by its very definition, does not deal with the representation of spiritual relationships; but, when applied methodically, it is merely a transfer of the so-called **graphical method**—long used in natural science with benefit for clarity and overview for the representation of time and force relationships—to that of spiritual relationships, a transfer whose possibility depends on the perspective of the previous argument, according to which the invisible spiritual realm

<summary>Fechner defines his "schematic-symbolic" method, distinguishing between structural outlines (schemata) and sensory representations (symbols) of the soul. He justifies this approach by comparing it to the graphical methods used in physics to represent invisible forces.</summary>

<keywords>symbol, schema, organic body, soul, natural science, graphical method, spiritual relationships, psychophysics</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s methodological justification for using symbols and analogies to describe the "invisible" or spiritual world. He transitions from the Argument of Similarity (Analogy) to his second major pillar, the Argument of Supplementation.</meta>

...shares its most essential relations with the visible, which then can serve for the representation and **symbolization** of the same. In any case, one can never manage without images and comparisons when representing **spiritual relations** <term>spiritual relations</term> <note>original: *geistige Verhältnisse*. This refers to the structural connections and patterns within the realm of consciousness or the "inner" world.</note>, though usually this happens without a method; all expressions for designating spiritual relations are even symbolic by their very nature; one cannot escape this even if one wanted to. The only difference is that a coherent representation of spiritual relations through coherent symbols is not necessary; rather, it is and can be replaced in direct representation by scattered symbols. It also remains true—just as it holds for the graphic representations of the **exact sciences**—that one cannot prove anything with a coherent symbolic representation that is not provable without it. And thus, with all the **schematics** and **symbolism** of which we shall make use in the following, nothing more is intended to be proven than what direct observations prove, for whose illustrative explanation they are intended, and for which they can nonetheless often perform good service.

Furthermore, it will be clear to any unbiased person how much the very circumstance that we are necessarily referred to the **analogy** of relations in the invisible spiritual realm with relations in the visible realm—when representing them—must contribute to strengthening the weight of our **argument from analogy**, insofar as it points us in the **knowledge** of the spiritual to the very same thing to which we are already referred in its representation.

### 2. Argument of Supplementation.

While the argument from **similarity** <note>original: *Aehnlichkeit*. This is the core of Fechner's "Argument from Analogy," where he compares the known (humans/animals) to the unknown (plants/stars).</note> rests on the similarity of plants with humans and animals...

<summary>Fechner argues that all language regarding the mind is inherently symbolic and that his "schematics" are intended as illustrative tools rather than independent proofs. He then introduces the Argument of Supplementation as a follow-up to the Argument from Analogy.</summary>

<keywords>symbolism, spiritual relations, schematics, argument from analogy, argument of supplementation, Gustav Theodor Fechner, philosophy of nature</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from Gustav Fechner’s first methodological pillar, the "Argument from Analogy" (similarities), to his second, the "Argument from Supplementation" (complementary differences). He is building a case that if animals and plants seem to be opposites in their physical form, they likely serve complementary roles in the spiritual or "soul" world.</meta>

rests upon such points in which the essential external expression of the **existence of the soul** <term>existence of the soul</term> <note>original: *Seelendasein*. Fechner uses this term to describe the manifest reality of a soul’s presence in the physical world.</note> may generally be sought, the **argument of supplementation** rests upon such points of contrasting difference between the two. In these, according to the same principle, the external expression of a mutual supplementation of their soul-existence can be sought—points which, while on one hand they establish the requirement for a **plant soul**, simultaneously allow for a conclusion regarding its nature as something that supplements the **animal soul**.

Clearly, the following relationships of contrast exist between animal and plant:

1) In humans and animals, **spontaneity** <term>spontaneity</term> <note>In 19th-century philosophy, "spontaneity" refers to the capacity of a being to act of its own accord or initiate movement, rather than just reacting to external forces.</note> relatively predominates; in plants, **receptivity** <term>receptivity</term> <note>The capacity to receive impressions or substances from the environment.</note> predominates. This is insofar as humans and animals act into the outside world, changing and mastering its conditions. In contrast, through their surfaces—which are covered, hairy, feathered, scaled, calcified, and relatively small in extent compared to their internal volume—they generally allow external substances and stimuli less frequent and more restricted access. The plant, however, is destined more for **passivity** <note>original: *Leiden*. While this can mean "suffering," in this philosophical context it refers to the state of being acted upon by external forces, the opposite of "acting."</note> and receiving than for acting. In its quiet standing and its nakedness, with a surface area that is very large in proportion to its volume—with its many-branched roots, its flat plate-like leaves, and its open, cup-shaped blossoms—it shows itself all the more suited for the uptake of substances and stimuli from the outside world, and in its life processes, it follows their influences directly.

2) The **organism** unfolds itself, works itself out, and develops itself in humans and animals relatively more in an **inward direction**, whereas in the plant it is more in an **outward direction**. One could say that in the animal, everything possible has been done to close it off from the outside world, except for those access points which were necessary for...

<summary>Fechner introduces the "Argument of Supplementation," arguing that plants and animals are spiritual counterparts. He contrasts the active, "closed" nature of animals with the passive, "open" nature of plants to suggest they fulfill different but complementary roles in the world-soul.</summary>

In this context, the author refers to the internal, mental, or soulful life of a being, rather than the modern paranormal definition.</note> contrast can be found primarily in the external appearance of animal and plant. On one side stands a soul characterized predominantly by spontaneity <term>spontaneity</term><note>original: *Spontaneität*. In 19th-century philosophy, this refers to the capacity of a being to act from its own internal power or will, rather than just reacting to external stimuli.</note>; on the other, one characterized predominantly by receptivity <term>receptivity</term><note>original: *Receptivität*. On one hand, we have a soul that is relatively more focused on the unfolding of an inner life and the intensive further development of this life; on the other, one whose life is intended to be absorbed more purely into relationships with the outside world and the expansion of the circle of these relationships. On one hand, we see a soul with a circle of far-reaching relationships to the outside world, and on the other, one with a narrower but more completely exhaustible circle of such relationships.

Since we now find that the animal really possesses and mirrors that side of the psychic contrast which it expresses in its physical structure and life—being a full symbol of it—we cannot doubt that the plant also really possesses that which it mirrors on the opposite side of the same contrast in its physical structure and life. The plant is not merely an empty symbol or an empty mirror of these qualities, as some have tried to claim. If it were otherwise: 1) there would be no consistency in the relationship between the physical and the psychic from the animal to the plant; 2) the animal would lack its psychological counterpart, even though it finds this counterpart physically in the plant and sees it mirrored through the plant’s physical nature. Both of these outcomes run contrary to rational observation.

<meta>This page continues the argument for the existence of a "plant soul."
