This page solidifies that logical bridge, arguing that the variety of the natural world requires plants to have souls to complete nature’s "plan."</meta>

It would be a different matter if no psychic contrast <term>psychic contrast</term><note>original: *Seelengegensatz*. The author argues that just as there is a physical difference between plants and animals, there must be a corresponding difference in their internal, mental, or soulful experiences.</note> corresponded at all to the contrast we find so decisively expressed between animal and plant on the physical side. In that case, we could not find the one mirrored by the other and would be forced to substitute a contrast between the "psychic" and the "non-psychic" instead. But such a psychic contrast is not only conceivable, it already exists within the animal and human kingdoms themselves—yet here it is not so extreme that one cannot imagine an even greater one. This leads us to consider that this wider contrast is to be found in the opposition between animal and plant. And since we truly find the physical expression of this broader psychic contrast based on general signs of a soul, we may consider the soul itself to have been found through it.

By summarizing these viewpoints, it seems to me that the plant soul is as well-trapped as a bird in a snare or cage. I do not know how it could still escape. Admittedly, this requires a certain logical framework <term>logical framework</term><note>original: *Schlußgefüge*. Literally a "structure of conclusions." The author acknowledges that his proof is a chain of reasoning rather than a single observation.</note>; a bird can be scared off with a simple wave of the hand, but not caught; and my opponents make such a gesture.

To further support this, consider the tendency observable elsewhere in nature to psychically utilize and exhaust the variety of external conditions through a corresponding variety of creatures—by means of a purposeful variation in their organization <term>organization</term><note>original: *Organisation*. In 19th-century biology, this refers to the specific structural arrangement of a living being's parts.</note>, position in life, and way of living. We would find this tendency only half-fulfilled if only the animals, who constantly change their location, were endowed with a soul, and not the plants, which are bound to fixed circles of life. On the other hand, if both are endowed with a soul, those in the animal—

Reduced to a single one of the viewpoints entering into this observation, the argument becomes incomplete.

This refers to the state of being "animated" or "soul-endowed." To be sure, if one mixes everything together unclearly and stops at the dull observation: the plants

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that the plant soul is not a "lesser" animal soul, but a different kind of psychic existence altogether. Having previously discussed the plant's tendency to "grow through" its environment rather than "run through" it, he now introduces a geometric metaphor using conic sections to illustrate the physical and spiritual differences between animals and plants.</meta>

...are, after all, far too different from animals; they do not run, they do not eat, they do not have this or that in which the soul-life <term>soul-life</term><note>original: *Seelenleben*. This refers to the internal psychic or conscious experiences of a being.</note> of animals expresses itself; in short, if one demands to see animals in them once more, then there is no plant soul <term>plant soul</term><note>original: *Pflanzenseele*.</note>. The justification for the plant soul lies, rather, in its difference from the animal soul. For a different soul, however, a different body is also required.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In "Nanna" <note>Referring to the author's 1848 book *Nanna, or on the Soul-Life of Plants*.</note> on page 370, I pointed out that if one set oneself the task—which is quite permissible in itself—of determining to which of the conic sections <term>conic sections</term><note>original: *Kegelschnitte*. These are the curves obtained by intersecting a cone with a plane, specifically the circle, ellipse, parabola, and hyperbola.</note> the animal form and the plant form (with its upward-diverging branches and flowers, and downward-diverging roots) could be reduced, on average and with the greatest possible approximation, one would find the ellipse for the animal form and the hyperbola for the plant form. These figures can be regarded as the simplest possible schemas of the animal and plant forms, just as their mathematical equations differ from one another only by the opposite sign of a principal variable. Along with the difference in form, they simultaneously represent the greater self-containedness of the animal and the greater openness of the plant toward the outside world, the plant's more boundless growth, and the greater independence of its parts from one another. All this, of course, is only approximate, or rather, an exaggeration. For in fact, the actual plant form remains at all times just as finitely closed-off as the animal form, and remains connected in its two halves; conversely, the animal form also has its openings to the outside world and its deep incisions—especially in insects—that almost reach the point of cutting the body through. The schema represents differences as absolute which, in the animal and the plant, occur only relatively.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;These schemas, when taken as symbols, are also very well suited to represent the ellipse as a soul completely closed off from the exterior, and the hyperbola as a soul completely open to the exterior. A soul completely open to the exterior is, of course, unthinkable, but just as little (insofar as we are dealing with souls characterized by finiteness <term>finiteness</term><note>original: *Endlichkeit*. The state of being limited or bounded, as opposed to the infinite nature of the divine.</note>) is a soul completely self-contained. In this respect, the ellipse has the advantage over the...

<summary>Fechner argues that the plant soul must be understood as fundamentally different from the animal soul, using the geometric metaphor of the ellipse (closed) for animals and the hyperbola (open) for plants to illustrate their differing relationships with the world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s geometric metaphor comparing animals to ellipses and plants to hyperbolas. Having established these shapes as mathematical opposites, he now argues that neither shape is superior, and both possess an inherent logic that implies a corresponding soul. He then transitions into two formal logical proofs for the existence of the plant soul: the Argument from Complementarity and the Argument from Gradation.</meta>

has no advantage over the hyperbola. One must remember, however, that in both cases, the absolute opposition must be translated into a relative one in order to be applicable to reality. From this perspective, I would not know why one conic section <term>conic section</term><note>original: *Kegelschnitt*. A curve formed by the intersection of a plane and a cone.</note> would have less claim to representing a soul of a specific character than the other. The hyperbola possesses its axis and its focal points just as well as the ellipse, and it is just as organically connected through an equation.

**Formula of the Argument from Complementarity.** If A stands in a relationship of opposite complementarity with B, and if A simultaneously mirrors and supports a spiritual quality *a*, to which B mirrors the opposite quality *b*, then it is to be assumed that B also supports that quality *b*. Therefore, if A, B, and *a* are given in that relationship of opposition, the existence of *b* (belonging to B) follows from it by logical inference.

### 3. Argument from Gradation.

This argument, like the previous one, relies on the difference between animal and plant, except that it refers more to a difference in degree <term>difference in degree</term><note>original: *Grad-Unterschied*.</note> than to a relationship of opposition; or rather, it relates to a relationship of hierarchy <note>literally "superiority and subordination" (*Ueber- und Unterordnung*).</note> rather than coordination <note>literally "side-by-side ordering" (*Nebenordnung*).</note>. However, it is not contradictory if we conceive of the plant as being simultaneously subordinately complementary and coordinately complementary to the animal, provided that the relationship of subordination occurs from a different viewpoint than that of coordination. In the same way, we see the woman as coordinately complementary to the man, or the left side of a person to the right, from a certain viewpoint, yet subordinately complementary from others <note>Fechner uses these social and biological analogies to show that two things can be "equal but different" in one sense, while part of a "ranked ladder" in another.</note>.

This argument, like the previous one, could be presented in such a way that we infer the spiritual from the physical as its mirror. Yet here, the reverse path shall be taken. First, we justify the requirement for the plant's level of soul <term>level of soul</term><note>original: *Seelenstufe*.</note> within the spiritual realm and...

<summary>Fechner argues that the mathematical integrity of the hyperbola justifies a plant soul just as the ellipse justifies an animal soul. He then introduces the logic of complementarity and gradation to show that plants and animals fulfill different but necessary roles in a spiritual hierarchy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchy of souls, specifically contrasting the plant’s immediate sensory existence with the animal’s proactive one. It addresses potential objections regarding whether a being can possess a "soul" if it lacks higher cognitive mediation.</meta>

...going beyond the sensory presence. The plant, however, instead of stretching its arms toward a future prey, grows, so to speak, only following the stimulus as it strikes it; its feeling and striving follow only the effect of the stimuli, whereas the representation and the desire of the animal run ahead of it.

However, the question arises: is such a stage of the soul <term>soul-stage</term><note>original: *Seelenstufe*. Refers to the specific level of consciousness or complexity an organism possesses in the natural hierarchy.</note>, as is required here in and for the plant, even thinkable? It is meant to be absorbed in pure sensuousness <term>sensuousness</term><note>original: *Sinnlichkeit*. In this philosophical context, it refers to the raw, immediate data of the senses—feelings like warmth or light—without higher thought or memory.</note>, in a flow and change of inner appearances; yet, for a soul to exist, there is a need at least for the unified connection <term>soul-unity</term><note>original: *Seeleneinheit*. The centralizing principle that binds individual sensations into a single "self" or experience.</note> of this flow and change, which is something higher than this flow and change themselves.

Certainly; but surely the plant will not lack that soul-unity which is established with the organic unity in general, and is thereby mirrored and supported. The difference between the plant and the animal will simply be this: that in the plant, the sensory element merges directly into the soul-unity, whereas in the animal, the sensory element is mediated with the soul-unity through higher—and in man, through the highest—combinations <term>combinations</term><note>original: *Combinationen*. The mental processes that link raw sensations into complex thoughts or associations.</note>, through intuitions, concepts, and ideas. And that is what places the animal and human soul higher.

But it may be further asked: can the sensory element really close itself off directly in the soul-unity, and can the soul-unity be directly absorbed in sensuousness? Does it not rather require, everywhere, a mediation with the unity for the connection of the sensory?

But man himself proves the possibility of such a deep closing-off of the soul, of such an immediate absorption of the soul-unity in sensuousness—as we claim permanently for the plant soul—by the fact that he himself occasionally places himself into the state of such absorption...

things should be. Meanwhile, I believe that in a domain where the question of existence cannot be determined by facts as precisely as in other areas, and once one has adopted the fundamental view of a rational, meaningful, and purposeful world order, one must certainly take into account questions and demands posed in the spirit of such an order, even if one cannot fully rely on them; only then must one also allow the answer in this same spirit. And so I answer: precisely because not only **plant-souls**, but also **animal-souls** in their relatively low status and their isolation would be meaningless beings, I believe—as I do for many other reasons—that these entire lower **soul-states** <term>soul-states</term><note>original: *Seelenzustände*. In this Spirit, the mediations and connections between all individual soul-lives are found at a completely different height, and a fluctuation, a rising and falling within that realm, possesses a far greater significance than what is found in the individual **human spirit** <term>human spirit</term><note>original: *Menschengeist*.</note>. However, the justification and detailed explanation of this does not belong here in the study of the **plant-soul**.

The entire structure, the position in life, and the behavior of the **plant** correspond to the viewpoint of our argument. It has no eyes or ears to orient itself in the external world and draw objective representations from it, and no feet to move toward goals distant in space and time—goals which it does not possess. The **cycle of representations** <term>cycle of representations</term><note>original: *Kreislauf der Vorstellungen*. The author refers to the psychological process where past thoughts and memories "circulate" and return to the conscious mind.</note> in **humans** and **animals**, which constantly brings back things from the past, undoubtedly requires a physical circulatory system <note>The author suggests a parallel between the physical circulation of blood/fluids and the mental circulation of thoughts.</note> as its foundation; the **plant** can do without the latter just as it lacks the former.

It develops such an expansion, freedom, variety, and even gradation of these organs that humans and animals—with their narrowly enclosed sensory circles, incapable of expansion—cannot even remotely match it. It is true that the sleeper and the embryo prove that there is something in the middle between the "death" of the inorganic kingdom and the waking life of organic creatures; but that is precisely nothing other than the *possibility* of awakening; and the sleeper and embryo exist specifically to represent that possibility. According to modern philosophy, the hierarchy would be arranged as follows: bodies that are always and completely asleep, those of the inorganic kingdom; bodies that are *once again* always and completely asleep, the plants; bodies that alternately wake and sleep, animals and humans (with animals being in a constant sleep regarding their higher faculties).

But, say the opponents, the plant in fact shows—no matter where or how one looks—no organs or functions that, in animals, relate even to the lowest manifestation of the soul. Referring to the "animal soul" or the capacity for sensation and voluntary movement.</note> life, characterized by sensations and <term>will</term><note>original: *Willkühr*. <meta>This page continues the author's defense of the "plant soul," specifically responding to the argument that plants only possess vegetative functions. This page counters that by comparing the "foundation" (vegetative life) to the "dwelling" (the soul).</meta>

...dwellings on foundations, as well as just as many foundations without dwellings above them? Or why does one attribute to Nature as wisdom what would be called the greatest folly in a human being? The objection itself acknowledges that the <term>vegetative</term><note>The basic biological functions of growth and nutrition.</note> in the animal has only the significance of a substructure for a sensing soul; how is it that in the plant, it stands alongside it without a soul? In truth, it has only that significance. Aside from the fact that everything vegetative in the animal harmonizes toward producing and maintaining the organism as the carrier of a sensing soul, the most important vegetative organs even serve special sensations directly—such as the skin, which simultaneously transpires, absorbs, and feels; or they culminate in such sensations, as the digestive intestinal canal does in the tasting tongue and the tasting palate, or the respiratory organ in the smelling nose. 

Why should the vegetative organs in the plant not be fit for that which they are fit for in the animal? And indeed, in the animal, it is generally the parts of the vegetative organs facing the bright external world that exhibit the capacity for sensation; meanwhile, the parts sunk into the dark depths lack this, or only possess a certain participation in the <term>general feelings</term><note>original: "Gemeingefühlen." In 19th-century psychology, this referred to "coenaesthesia" or the internal sense of one's own body, rather than specific outward senses like sight.</note>. Now, however, the plant—with its root sunk into the dark depths on one hand, and its stem rising freely into the bright external world with leaves spreading freely within it and culminating in the open blossom on the other—shows the same contrast of vegetative parts to which the contrast between sensation and lack of sensation is linked in the animal, only even more strikingly. The root is a true pedestal; the plant, standing independently alongside the animal, is not.

The reply is: but it is the entire plant kingdom, after all, and for that very reason it is no soul-less pedestal, because the soul-filled animal kingdom is built atop it. The plant kingdom must prepare the nutrients for the animal world, which without them could not exist and live.

Indisputably; just as the <term>peasantry</term><note>original: "Bauerstand." The social class of farmers and laborers.</note> exists for the class of scholars and nobles, who otherwise could not exist and live; or like the herbivores for the carnivores, who otherwise could not exist and live. After all, the clover tastes as good to the ox as the ox tastes to the lion, and the ox feels the fear of the lion at least as strongly as the lion feels the hunger for the ox. This counter-argument [which claims a foundation needs no soul of its own] would, along with the plant, also deny a soul to the lion and the ox.

At the same time, it remains true that the plant is physiologically built from the ground up only from organs and processes of <term>vegetative life</term><note>Basic biological functions like growth and nutrition.</note>; its entire activity in relation to the external world—the opening of blossoms, sleeping and waking, scent, the sexual process—is physiologically only the <term>resultants</term><note>original: "Resultanten." No less, however, can one grasp the higher activities of animals and humans physiologically only as the resultants of uniquely arranged chemical or nutritional processes. Even free <term>locomotion</term><note>Self-propelled movement.</note> depends on such processes, and if electricity is required for it, then it must be produced through them. <meta>This page continues the author’s argument that if we only looked at human biology through the lens of physiology, we would see humans as nothing more than "vegetative" machines, just as we currently view plants. For whatever else one sees in the human is precisely that which is not physiologically detectable or traceable—which one is unwilling and unable to see in the plant because one brings only the "eye of physiology" to that task, whereas one employs an "ideal eye" <note>An "ideal eye" refers to a philosophical or empathetic perspective that assumes the presence of a mind or soul.</note> for the animal and the human.

To this, let us add a few extra diagrams and symbols. Schematically, with a slight variation of a model used previously, one could say: The animal is a closed, sensing sac into which parts fold inward (<term>invagination</term>), and these inward foldings branch off into further internal foldings. The plant is a closed, sensing sac from which parts fold outward (<term>evagination</term>), and these outward foldings branch off into further outward foldings. Now, the higher psychological phenomena of humans and animals depend on these internal foldings entering into complex relationships with one another. In the plant, this condition for higher psychological phenomena is absent, and the sensing sac is entirely absorbed into external branchings. These serve only the lower sensory functions gained through interaction with the outside world, yet they possess all the more freedom to unfold.

This schema, like all previous ones, is to be understood only relatively and <note>original: "cum grano salis"</note> **with a grain of salt**. The animal also displays many outward foldings, which are then likewise intended for interaction with the outside world (the sense organs being **receptive**, the limbs **active**); the plant also displays, particularly in **fruiting** <note>original: "Fructification."</note>, some inward foldings, or **involutions**, and I have declared it possible (in my book *Nanna*, page 342), and still consider it possible, that in this—their pu—

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s morphological comparison between animals and plants. The previous page ended by discussing how "higher psychological phenomena" in animals depend on internal biological structures (invaginations) maturing.</meta>

...later stages of life, a trace of higher psychological <term>function</term><note>original: "Function"</note> awakens within it. Yet this remains nothing but a dark conjecture.

We can further <term>schematize</term><note>To represent something in a simplified, abstract form to highlight its structure.</note> animals and plants in their higher forms from another point of view. The animal shows itself developed along three perpendicular directions, which are defined by the orientation of the spinal column and its various processes. The length of the spinal column corresponds to the principal axis; along this main direction, parts of different shapes and meanings are organized one above the other. In the transverse direction—indicated by the orientation of the symmetrical <term>transverse processes</term><note>The bony projections on either side of a vertebra, which help provide points of attachment for muscles and ligaments.</note>—we find bilateral symmetry. In the direction of depth—from the back to the front, or the dorsal to the ventral side—marked by the simple <term>spinous processes</term><note>The bony projection off the back of each vertebra that you can feel when you touch your spine.</note>, a simpler structure is revealed. 

In the plant, the principal direction (with its parts ordered one above the other) stands perpendicular to the principal axis of the animal, yet parallel to the principal axis of the human <note>Fechner is noting that both humans and plants grow vertically, whereas most animals grow horizontally.</note>. For the other two perpendicular directions found in animals or humans, however, the plant possesses the circle; because all directions perpendicular to the vertical principal axis are indifferent to it. Finally, the linear principal axis and the circle merge into a <term>spiral</term>, according to which type the entire plant is famously built. This melting together of the three spatial dimensions within the plant's schema—which are distinctly separated in the animal's schema—can simultaneously symbolize for us the lack of differentiation regarding spatial relationships within the "soul" of the plant.

**Formula of the Argument of Gradation.** If three physical levels of organization A, B, and C (plant, animal, and human organisms) are given, along with the corresponding soul-levels *b* and *c*, and these still leave a soul-level *a* remaining below them—which is mirrored by A just as *b* and *c* are mirrored by B and C—then it can be assumed that soul-level *a* is also supported by organism A. Thus, if A, B, C, *b*, and *c* are given as existing in that specific relationship, then the existence of *a* belonging to A follows by logical conclusion.

<summary>Fechner compares the geometric symmetry of animals and plants, suggesting the "spiral" growth of plants reflects a more unified, less differentiated soul. He uses a logical formula of gradation to argue that if humans and animals have souls corresponding to their biology, plants must as well.</summary>
<keywords>morphology, spinal column, bilateral symmetry, spiral growth, plant soul, logical gradation, organizational levels</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the morphological relationships between animals and plants. Having previously compared their higher forms and structural symmetries, Fechner now transitions to the "intermediate kingdom" where the clear distinctions between the two realms begin to dissolve.</meta>

### 4. Argument of Connection.

While the previous arguments relied on relationships of equality and difference between the distinct forms of the animal and plant kingdoms, this argument relies on the relationships of transition or connection between both kingdoms through an intermediate kingdom, in which the relationships of equality and difference between the two more or less mingle and blur.

It is well known that the animal and plant kingdoms border one another through an intermediate kingdom; for some of its creatures, it has not even been possible to assign a place on one side or the other with certainty, while in others—which are more definitely assigned to this or that kingdom—the characteristics overlap in many ways with those of the other kingdom, crossing, mixing, and merging with them.

The so-called <term>plant-animals</term><note>original: "Pflanzenthiere." Historically known as zoophytes (such as corals, sponges, or sea anemones), these organisms were once thought to be intermediate forms between plants and animals because of their stationary, branched growth.</note>, which still count as part of the animal kingdom and have already been mentioned several times, nevertheless agree with plants in the simplicity of their structure; the lack of a central organ, a closed circulatory system, and (partly) nerves; in their branched form, often even with flower-like main parts; in their fixed position; in the union of both sexes in the same individual (<term>hermaphroditism</term>); in the possibility of multiplying through budding as well as through artificial division; in the alternation of generations *), and even in many particularities of the—

---

> *) For in the sequence of leaves, flowers, and fruits, the plant has an analog to the so-called <term>alternation of generations</term><note>original: "Generationswechsel." A biological cycle where an organism alternates between a sexual and an asexual phase. Fechner is comparing the stages of plant growth—leaves to flowers—to the complex life cycles of lower animals like jellyfish or polyps.</note> found in lower animals; however, in accordance with the general difference between plants and animals noted earlier, the previous developmental stages persist when the later ones appear. <note>Fechner refers to how a plant keeps its leaves while flowering, whereas a "plant-animal" might physically transform from one stage to the next.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the "Argument of Connection," exploring the "intermediate kingdom" where the boundaries between plants and animals blur. Fechner focuses on "plant-animals" (zoophytes), noting how they share reproductive and structural traits with plants, such as budding and branched forms.</summary>

<keywords>intermediate kingdom, plant-animals, zoophytes, hermaphroditism, alternation of generations, developmental stages, morphology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion of the "intermediate kingdom," focusing on the blurred chemical and behavioral boundaries between plants and animals. He references the work of Nathaniel Pringsheim on the reproductive processes of lower organisms to argue for a continuity of "soul-life" across all biological realms.</meta>

chemical constitution; in many ways—some from this perspective, others from that—many creatures agree with plants through a combination of almost all these points. Meanwhile, from the other side, many creatures categorized within the plant kingdom but belonging to the **intermediate kingdom** approach animals. This is seen through their free <term>locomotion</term><note>The ability of an organism to move from one place to another.</note> in their earlier developmental stages, such as **swarm spores** and **zoospores**, and—according to newer investigations—through very striking similarities in the **fertilization process** (<term>Pringsheim</term><note>Nathaniel Pringsheim (1823–1894) was a German botanist who proved that fertilization in plants involves the fusion of two cells, similar to animals.</note>).

As far as the familiar signs of <term>soul-endowment</term><note>original: "Beseelung." This is a central concept for Fechner, referring to the presence of a conscious or "souled" inner experience in nature.</note> in the intermediate kingdom reach, we see only a lower stage, not a decrease in the intensity of **soul-life** indicated there. Rather, these <term>plant-animals</term><note>original: "Pflanzenthiere." A historical term for organisms like corals or polyps that look like plants but behave like animals.</note> still provide signs of sensory perception and sensory drive as decisive and powerful as those of higher animals (cf. p. 32). This is shown through the variety, liveliness, and nature of their movements, their sensitivity to sensory stimuli, and their predatory greed.

Since the drive for locomotion in the transition to the definite plant kingdom is merely transformed into an equally lively **growth drive**, we have no reason—given the otherwise gradual transition of both kingdoms in physical terms—to believe in a sudden break between the two in terms of their <term>psychic relations</term><note>original: "psychischen Beziehungen." Here meaning the internal, mental, or spiritual qualities of the organisms.</note>. That is, we should not assume a point where psychic intensity suddenly ceases or diminishes. This is especially true when we notice that the organization and life-position of creatures begin to rise again from the intermediate kingdom through the plant kingdom in a new direction. Thus, higher plants—shrubs and trees—stand far above the creatures of the intermediate kingdom, even if the overall organization in the plant kingdom, according to its characteristics—

<meta>This page continues the argument for the continuity of "soul-life" across the natural world. On the previous page, the author discussed how "intermediate" creatures (like swarm spores) bridge the gap between animals and plants through shared traits like locomotion and reproduction.</meta>

...marks by which one is accustomed to measuring their height, do not rise as high as in the animal kingdom. In a word, the **intermediate kingdom** presents itself as a bridge between two different realms of soul, rather than a gap between a realm of soul and a realm of the dead.

This argument merely consciously extends the conclusion that leads us, unconsciously and involuntarily, to hold fast to the belief in <term>soul-endowment</term><note>original: "Beseelung." Fechner's core idea that all living things—including plants—possess a conscious inner life or soul.</note> from the human kingdom through the entire animal kingdom. The worm is so dissimilar to the human that we would doubt its soul-endowment were it not for the <term>intermediate links</term><note>Refers to the evolutionary or taxonomic gradations between simple and complex organisms.</note> between human and worm. But now, so many and such gradually changing intermediate links insert themselves between the two that no boundary can be found where one might cut them off. While preserving only the most general signs of soul, the signs of a specific expression of soul-life transform one after another into others, until, for the human, the worm is there. Now, in the <term>plant-animals</term><note>original: "Pflanzenthieren." A historical term for sedentary marine organisms like corals or sea anemones that appear plant-like but are biologically animals.</note>, all the specific signs of animal soul-endowment have finally been exchanged for those of the plant, except for <term>locomotion</term><note>The ability to move from place to place.</note>; finally, this too is exchanged for the <term>growth-drive</term><note>The internal impulse that governs the development and expansion of a plant.</note>, and the complete plant is there.

**Formula of the argument of connection.** If **A** and **B** are connected through a gradual transition, and ***a*** maintains itself undiminished until the point of transition, then ***b*** will not suddenly become nothing; rather, a ***b*** will be connected with the ***a***. Therefore, if **A**, **B**, and ***a*** are given according to their existence in that relationship, the existence of ***b*** belonging to **B** is to be found through inference.

<summary>Fechner argues that just as we accept the "soul" of a worm because it is connected to humans through a series of intermediate animals, we must accept the "soul" of a plant because it is connected to animals through "plant-animal" hybrids. He presents a logical formula suggesting that if physical forms transition gradually, their internal spiritual states must also transition rather than abruptly vanishing.</summary>

### 5. The Argument from Causality.

This argument refers to causal relationships or relations of origin. One reason to assume the <term>soul-endowment</term><note>original: "Beseelung." The philosophical concept that a being possesses an internal, conscious life or "soul."</note> of a being lies—not alone, but in connection with other reasons—in the fact that it is produced by admittedly ensouled beings and is capable of producing such beings. Conversely, a counter-argument can be sought not only in the fact that a thing is a product of the inanimate, but also in the fact that it forms merely a preliminary stage of conscious soul-endowment, such as an **embryo**. 

Now, the circumstance that humans and animals, which are recognized as ensouled, have grown out of nature can itself be asserted, along with other reasons, as a ground for the <term>divine soul-endowment of nature</term><note>original: "Gottbeseelung der Natur." The "panpsychist" view that the natural world is a manifestation of a divine, conscious spirit.</note>. This divinely ensouled nature will then also be capable of producing ensouled plants, so that from this perspective, no difficulty exists. 

However, should plant life behave merely as an <term>embryonic preliminary stage</term><note>The author is addressing the critique that plants are just "unborn" animals or a lower, incomplete version of animal life.</note>, it could only be thought of as an embryonic stage of animal life. But the entire animal kingdom did not arise from the plant kingdom at the beginning; rather, both are of the same date in the <term>history of creation</term><note>original: "Schöpfungsgeschichte." Refers to the chronological development of life on Earth.</note> and follow parallel paths of development from the simplest forms through increasing levels of <term>organization</term><note>The structural complexity of biological systems.</note>. Nor does an individual animal arise from a plant today; instead, both once again follow a parallel course of development from an analogous state through increasing levels of organization.

Insofar as the development of the animal on the physical side not only mirrors but also supports a corresponding development on the <term>psychic side</term><note>original: "psychischer Seite." Referring to the internal, mental, or soul-based aspect of a living thing, rather than the "paranormal."</note>, we will have to assume that the development of the plant, which is parallel to the animal on the physical side, does not

<summary>This page introduces the "Argument from Causality," positing that since nature itself is divinely ensouled, it is logical that plants are too. It rejects the idea that plants are merely an "embryonic" or lower stage of animal life, noting that plants and animals developed as parallel, equally ancient kingdoms.</summary>

<keywords>Causality, soul-endowment, embryo, divine nature, animal kingdom, plant kingdom, history of creation, biological organization, psychic development</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of the author’s "Arguments" for the existence of a soul in plants. It concludes the "Argument from Causality" (begun on the previous page) and introduces the "Argument from Teleology." The focus is on the parallel development of life forms rather than a linear hierarchy.</meta>

...less supports the <term>psychic development</term><note>original: "psychische Entwickelung." In this context, it refers to the internal, mental, or spiritual growth of a being.</note>, as whose expression, mirror, and symbol it can be regarded.

Schematically, one might say: the plant kingdom and the animal kingdom represent two branches—not grown out of one another, but rooted together in the inorganic kingdom, diverging from a common node, that of the <term>intermediate kingdom</term><note>original: "Zwischenreich." Likely referring to organisms that appear to be both plant-like and animal-like, which were a subject of intense 19th-century biological debate.</note>. One branch shoots up higher and toward the opposite side than the other, though the latter is more broadly unfolded, staying closer to the earth and covering it more; the former mirrors and supports the active, higher, more spiritual side of earthly soul-life, while the latter represents the more receptive, sensory side.

### Formula of the Argument from Causality.

Insofar as both the entire kingdoms and the individual beings of the animal (A) and plant (B), instead of having emerged from one another, show a neighboring, parallel path of development—originating together from the earthly kingdom (C)—and since with A there develops a soul (a), one should not see B as merely a preliminary stage of A. Instead, for the parallel development of B with A, one must also assume a parallel development of a soul (b) with the soul (a).

### 6. The Argument from Teleology or Purposiveness.

This argument refers to the relationships of purpose (<term>teleology</term><note>The philosophical study of design, purpose, or "ends" in nature; the idea that things are shaped toward a specific goal.</note>) in which the plant appears analogous to humans and animals. From this point of view, it can be subordinated to the Argument from Analogy or Similarity and has therefore already been referenced as supplementary to that. However, if one focuses on the aspect of <term>purposiveness</term><note>original: "Zweckmäßigkeit." The quality of being suited to a particular function or goal, like a lung being "purposive" for breathing.</note> as the main perspective, it can be presented as a distinct argument, as follows.

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the "Argument from Causality" into "6. The Argument from Teleology or Purposiveness." It builds on the premise that biological structures are not accidental but exist to serve a specific internal purpose: the life of the soul.</meta>

We find through experience, inductively, that in *humans* and *animals* the entire structure, the whole station in life, and the whole life-process is purposefully arranged for the benefit of a *soul*<note>The author uses "Seele" (soul) to refer to the internal, subjective experience or consciousness of a being, rather than strictly in a religious sense.</note>; that all <term>means of organization</term><note>original: "Organisationsmittel." This refers to the biological "tools" or organs that make up a living being.</note> are so combined among themselves and with external conditions that a *soul* can exist therein, develop itself, and become the cause for the emergence of new souls. This holds true regardless of how one interprets the relationship between *body* and *soul* here, or upon what one wishes to make the existence of the purposeful arrangements themselves dependent; in short, experience teaches it. However, insofar as we encounter similar arrangements, institutions, and a similar combination of organizational means among themselves and with external things in *plants*, we may also assume by *analogy* that they exist just as much for the benefit of a *soul*.

In fact, however, *plants*—in what we regard as purposeful arrangements, institutions, and combinations in *humans* and *animals* and see serving a *soul-life*—stand equal to *animals* not only in general, but also according to many specific determinations. These refer to the particular purposes of a conscious *soul-life*, as is especially true of the purposeful positioning, turning, and opening of suitable organs toward external sensory stimuli and the conditions of reproduction. The entire sexual relations in the *human* and *animal kingdoms* factually work together toward the purpose of further propagating such a life at the peak of a conscious *soul-life*; we may assume by *analogy* that the no less carefully made and analogous arrangements in the *plant world* are no less intended to further propagate such a life at the peak of a conscious *soul-life*.

### Formula of the Argument from Teleology.

Insofar as B is analogous to A in those <term>purposive arrangements</term><note>original: "Zweckeinrichtungen." This refers to structures in nature that appear designed for a specific function or goal (teleology).</note> which, in A, stand in relation to the existence of an "a," we may also presuppose an analogous relationship in B of these same arrangements to a "b."

These would be my six positive arguments for the existence of a *plant soul*, and with them the circle of argumentation is closed, so far as I have been able to close it. The entire organization, the whole life, and the entire position of the plant in life—in its relationship to animals, to humans, and to all of external nature—have been included in this argumentation. This covers all general perspectives that might be relevant: those of similarity, difference, interconnection, and so on, as well as the most general conditions of the <term>existence of the soul</term><note>original: "Seeldasein." The author is exploring the conditions under which a conscious or semi-conscious inner life can exist.</note> itself, its contrasts, and its varying levels. Since we can experience the existence of the soul nowhere except within ourselves, the visible must everywhere serve as a sign, a mirror, and a symbol of the invisible. Yet, not content with merely seeing the invisible reflected by the visible, we have sought everywhere for the perspective that can assure us that the invisible is also supported by the visible.

I call this a piece of <term>natural philosophy</term><note>original: "Naturphilosophie." A 19th-century intellectual movement that sought to understand nature as a living, unified whole rather than just a collection of mechanical parts.</note> and, at the same time, a piece of that philosophy which I set in opposition to the average philosophy of today. I oppose it in this: that instead of philosophizing *about* the facts, as modern philosophy does, it philosophizes *from* the facts; it bases its concepts of the factual upon the facts themselves, and through generaliza—<note>The word "Verallgemeinerung" (generalization) is cut off at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>The author concludes his six arguments for the plant soul, presenting a formal logical structure for teleology and defining his method as "philosophizing from facts" rather than abstractly over them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's methodological explanation of how we can know things beyond direct experience. It concludes the section on the "six arguments" for the plant soul by providing a memorable metaphor of a hand and transitions into the logical structure of these arguments.</meta>

...supplementing, linking, and analyzing that which is given to us, in order to reach that which is not given to us but is nonetheless given in the nature of things—surpassing and exceeding our limited experience—and seeking to penetrate even to the most general, the highest, and the final, while constantly regarding the preservation of our higher and practical interests. It is the path of empiricism, but of a <term>rational empiricism</term><note>original: "vernünftige Empirie." This refers to a method of investigation that starts with observable facts (empiricism) but uses logical reasoning to extend those facts to reach conclusions about things we cannot see directly.</note>. The doctrine of the <term>plant soul</term><note>original: "Pflanzenseele." The author, Gustav Theodor Fechner, was a pioneer in psychology who argued that plants, like humans and animals, possess an inner life or consciousness.</note> is but a small example of where this path leads. It will lead us yet further and higher.

If one joins, as one can, the sixth of the preceding arguments to the first, then one might not inappropriately compare the five arguments to which they are hereby reduced to the five fingers of a hand. These fingers grasp their object in a unified way, based on something that lies behind the individual parts and of which they are merely the offshoots. This "something behind" is the view of a <term>divine ensoulment</term><note>original: "Gottbeseelung." The belief that God is present within all parts of the living world, providing a spiritual foundation for all existence.</note> of the entire world. The first argument is the "main finger" <note>likely the thumb</note>; it acts on its own with the greatest emphasis and must assist all the others, yet they in turn assist it. In the third argument, the reasoning reaches its peak; the fourth and fifth are in themselves the weakest fingers—all quite by chance, just as with an actual hand.

The formulas in which I have condensed the arguments show that the conclusions on which I base them are fundamentally very simple. Assuming the empirical facts that the preceding discussion has served to present, these conclusions can be sharply summarized in few words. Without being strictly mathematical, they nevertheless approach the form of mathematical formulas to some extent; they consist, so to speak, of at least three terms of a proportion, which through a certain, only...

If we equate plant and animal to two straight lines, then the first argument (including the sixth) concludes from the partial coincidence of both when they are laid upon one another; the second and third from the difference between the two which remains. Specifically, the second applies insofar as they are laid upon one another such that they project beyond the overlapping part in opposite directions; the third, when they are laid upon one another such that the plant line coincides with the lower part of the animal line, while the latter projects beyond it with the upper part. On the whole, the intention here is directed more toward letting them work through their interlocking and mutual support than through their individual execution, whereas in the earlier writing, the reverse holds true. ## VII.

### The Empirical Principles of Faith.

Belief in the plant soul does not seem to signify much. What does it matter, after all, whether one believes in it or not? People have even reproached me for this and said that I have put too much effort into something that is not worth the trouble.

Now, however, faith in the plant soul is merely a small test case for faith in things that cannot be exactly proven in general; and it is not just a small test, but rather the smallest and the greatest things are connected in this entire question. Just as one can grasp a large pot more easily by a small handle than by its wide belly, I also thought I had found a small handle in the little plant soul. I believed that with it, faith in the greatest things could most easily be lifted onto the great pedestal <note>original: "Untersatz." This refers to a stand or base used to support a heavy vessel.</note>. To be sure, I did not succeed. People find the belly too large and the handle too small, and they continue to cook in the old pot, gripping it by its old handles.

So be it. In any case, because the plant soul is only a minor matter—at least relatively, for fundamentally a Brazilian rainforest <note>Fechner often uses the vast scale of the tropics to contrast the "smallness" of a single plant with the "greatness" of nature's soul.</note> full of souls is no small thing—one could also

This refers to Fechner’s major 1851 work, *Zend-Avesta: Or on the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter*, named after the sacred texts of Zoroastrianism.</note>, the contents of which I cannot reproduce here. But the principles which I consider authoritative in these matters, and which were authoritative in that work, I wish to set forth clearly here and illustrate through several arguments based upon them.

Undisputedly, historical authority and practical need will always remain the primary sources and supports of faith in the highest and ultimate things; and so they should. Yet they have not been able to ward off doubts, nor have they been able to establish a firm form of faith that is clearly and rationally connected with what we know of the nature of things. This is so much the case that one is almost always in doubt as to whether one should sacrifice historically transmitted faith for the sake of reason and science, or vice versa—indeed, one might even believe that a chasm or an <term>antinomy</term> <note>A contradiction between two beliefs or conclusions that are both reasonable; a conflict of laws or principles.</note> between the two is grounded in the very nature of things themselves. These sources or supports of faith have not been able to suffice on their own, as they flowed along unconcerned with a third source, not supporting themselves by a third; for as of yet, no faith stands firm historically and in a way that satisfies all needs; rather, religions still conflict, and within those religions, the various <term>confessions</term> <note>Denominations or specific branches of a religious tradition, such as the various divisions within Christianity.</note>. Thus, those foundations of faith alone cannot suffice. Indeed, that which is destined to stand on three feet cannot stand firmly on two. Rather, it will be necessary to also base faith upon the nature of things, in order to resolve its contradiction with the nature of things,

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument for a "third source" of faith. Having established that historical authority and human needs are insufficient on their own to resolve religious doubt, he now proposes a reconciliation through the "nature of things."</meta>

and thereby simultaneously resolve the contradictions between faith and the needs of humanity, as well as between the various historical forms of faith. For the former—our needs—are themselves rooted in that same universal nature of things, of which the objects of faith are the highest and final expressions; therefore, our needs must base their satisfaction upon that nature. The latter—the historical forms of faith—present themselves merely as different paths from a common starting point toward a common goal, which is to be found in the unanimous satisfaction of the most universal and highest human needs. And so, one may take the following principles into closer consideration. Instead of contradicting the other foundations of faith, these principles rather reinforce them by adding a groundwork based on a return to the nature of things <note>Fechner refers to *Natur der Dinge*, his philosophical approach that looks for spiritual truths within the physical laws and structures of the universe.</note>, and thus intend to contribute to faith growing toward its goal.

Indeed, the concern here is not with tearing down what stands firm and wholesome, but rather with growth upon a broader foundation. There lives one God, and all that exists, exists through God; there exists a life hereafter, which is the punishment, reward, and higher stage of this earthly life; there exists a sacred world order, in the sense of an eventual victory of good over evil. These are the foundations that have already become firm beneath the rubble of a decaying paganism <note>original: "Heidenthum." In this context, Fechner is likely referring to the historical transition from polytheistic traditions to the core tenets of monotheism and moral order.</note> that is being increasingly cleared away. There is nothing more to take away from these and nothing more to add to them, except what reinforces their stability and elevates the structure built upon them. I truly believe, however, that the principles in question here are capable of contributing something further in this regard.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the foundations of faith. The previous page concluded by arguing that religious principles should be grounded in the "nature of things" to provide a firmer basis than mere historical tradition or human need alone.</meta>

These are principles of experience. Everything that is real must prove its reality through its "experiencability" or through its effects within the realm of what can be experienced. The objects of faith, however, aspire and are meant to be the highest, greatest, and most universal realities. How, then, could their existence be removed from the realm of experience?

For a long time, people have spoken of religious experience as a source of faith. And certainly, what is called by that name belongs among its best sources; yet it does not cover the whole of what I mean. Or should everything we experience of those realities, or everything we can conclude from what is experienced, be truly and exclusively contained within the experience of a <term>feeling of dependence<note>Fechner refers to the *Abhängigkeitsgefühl*, a concept famously championed by the theologian Friedrich Schleiermacher, who defined religion as the "feeling of absolute dependence" on God.</note></term>, of a highest and ultimate need, of the satisfaction of feeling oneself in God's keeping, or the hope of a balancing of all earthly imperfections in the <term>afterlife<note>original: "Jenseits," literally "the beyond."</note></term>? In our relationship to our father and mother, we have feelings that are not of the same height, yet are of a related character to those we feel for God; but do we know nothing more of our father and mother than these feelings?

Now, admittedly, experience does not reach God and the life hereafter as immediately as it reaches our father and mother and our daily life; but even if it is far from reaching them immediately, are we therefore unable to achieve anything upon its foundation?

An oak tree can grow from an acorn, yet it cannot grow without the acorn. From the entire sky, the astronomer sees only small discs and tiny points, and yet a whole heaven full of great planets and suns belongs to him. Yet it is only on the basis of what he sees that the unseen things in the heavens become his. In the same way, our entire knowledge of reality everywhere supports itself only upon a relatively small circle of experiences of the real-

...fruitfulness, but it must also rest upon such a foundation. And thus our principle will also be this: to base our knowledge of the highest and final realities—which far exceed our experience—not on that which lies outside of experience, but on that which lies within it.

Of course, this principle has always been applied in a crude, fragmentary, almost embryonic way, even involuntarily by those who reject it. For we all stand under its compulsion. Indeed, there is no religion that does not create God in the image of man, and the <term>afterlife<note>original: "Jenseits," literally "the beyond."</note></term> in the image of <term>this world<note>original: "Diesseits," literally "this side."</note></term>. Man simply cannot do otherwise than to infer that which he does not see from that which he does see; otherwise, his thoughts would remain empty. Yet, forced to infer in this way, he seems forced to infer wrongly. God is not as, and the afterlife is not as, one finds them to be according to such inferences.

Should the principle be rejected for this reason? But what then would fill the thought of God and the afterlife? One must apply it eventually; thus, it is only a matter of choosing whether to do so crudely—so that the character of the principle is lost—or in a rational sense. Everywhere it is a difficult art, learned and practiced by only a few, to correctly infer from the given to the non-given, even within the circle of the most immediate things. 

Would it not then be the task of theological and philosophical systems to develop, teach, and practice this same difficult art—through the refinement of which <term>natural science<note>original: "Naturwissenschaft."</note></term> has been raised above <term>superstition<note>original: "Aberglauben."</note></term> in earthly matters—with respect to the highest and final things? <meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his "Day-View" philosophy. This page completes the opening question regarding the task of the philosopher and addresses the common religious objection that the finite human mind cannot possibly comprehend an infinite God.</meta>

...task to seek the inference from that which lies within experience to that which lies beyond experience? Instead, people seek those inferences from what lies outside, above, and behind experience, and apply them to what is above and even what is within it.

But, one might object, your principle is a fundamental error. What are our means of inference from the given to the non-given? Inductions, <term>analogies<note>Fechner was famous for using analogies—comparing the soul of a plant or the earth to the human soul—to bridge the gap between science and religion.</note></term>, and mathematical deductions. With these, one can indeed infer from the finite to the finite—and even then, one will err often enough—but in no way can one infer the infinite. Yet, in every matter concerning faith in the highest and final things, we are dealing with subjects that bear the character of infinity. One may well—indeed, one must—imagine the infinite through finite images, framing it within the bounds of finiteness to grasp any part of it at all; however, this remains a <term>makeshift solution<note>original: "Nothbehelf." A temporary or emergency measure used because nothing better is available.</note></term>. One must always remain aware that it is only a crutch, and one must use that awareness to correct the conception of the infinite that was formed according to the proportions of the finite. To know that God is incomparable to everything that is finite—that is the only true view of Him, and the only one worthy of God, that we can possess. Not even in mathematics does one consider the finite comparable to the infinite, much less could one do so in these spiritual matters.

One is right; the finite is incomparable to the infinite, yet not in every respect, and it is necessary to look more closely at which respects those are. People are too quick to settle on this idea of incomparability.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal of the objection that the infinite cannot be understood through finite analogies. He moves from the mathematical comparison of lines (started on the previous page) to the theological definition of God as "Spirit," arguing that if the word "Spirit" is to have any meaning at all, the infinite Spirit must share the essential qualities of the finite spirit.</meta>

...line, and what the one makes, the other makes in addition. And so an infinite and infinitely high Spirit—for should God not be a Spirit?—will be incomparable in height and breadth to every finite spirit, and yet quite comparable, indeed quite equal to it in everything that makes a spirit a spirit. Or why call him a Spirit at all?

> “God breathed into man the living breath of life into his nostrils, and so man became a living soul.” <note>Genesis 2:7. Fechner uses this biblical foundation to argue that the human soul is essentially of the same "substance" as God's spirit.</note>

Can the spirit, which is a breath of God's spirit, be completely incomparable to it; and shall we be permitted to say nothing of the wind that applies to the breath? <note>Fechner is playing on the dual meaning of the German word *Geist*, which means "spirit" or "mind," but is etymologically linked to breath and wind.</note>

Not in our principle, but in the objection itself lies a fundamental error, one so deeply rooted that only the same revolution that will uproot the whole current <term>worldview<note>original: "Weltbetrachtung." This refers to the prevailing scientific and religious outlook of the 19th century, which Fechner sought to replace with his "Day-View."</note></term> will uproot it along with it; for this error is itself one of its firmest roots. It is the error of placing the infinite over against the finite, above it, beyond it, outside it, behind it—indeed, even placing a monstrous divide between the two, as if they could not approach each other at all. In truth, they do not lie apart at all; rather, the finite is the **content** of the infinite, and no other relationship between the two is reasonably thinkable than precisely this: that the finite is the content of the infinite. 

Thus, the infinite is also not incomprehensible at all; rather, it is graspable by uncounted "handles" of finitude. It is only *un-encompassable* <note>original: "unumfaßlich." A distinction between being able to touch or understand parts of something (*faßlich*) versus being able to wrap one's mind around the entirety of it (*umfaßlich*).</note>; but it is graspable from as many sides and at as many ends as there are one-sidednesses and finitudes. These finite parts often enough count for the whole—another error, to be sure, but one which does not make the first error the truth.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument against the conventional theological separation of God from the world. He has just finished a mathematical analogy regarding lines to show that the infinite does not exist "outside" the finite, but rather contains it. He now applies this logic to the concepts of Space, Time, and Spirit.</meta>

...find any other relationship of the finite to the infinite than that the one is the content of the other? The straight line was perhaps only a strange exception. Well now, is there perhaps an infinite space beyond finite spaces, an infinite time beyond finite times, an infinite physical world—Nature—beyond finite bodies? There are only infinities which include within themselves the instances of finitude of their own kind.

The God of modern opinion, however, is an infinite Spirit who does not include the fullness of finite spirits, but rather excludes them, holding them externally opposite to himself—much as one finite spirit faces another (for clarity and precision are not to be found here; one contradicts oneself by the second word). And eternal life, according to modern opinion, is not something that includes our finite life as a whole includes the part, but is instead supernaturally torn away from it and led in an unapproachable <term>beyond<note>original: "Jenseits." This refers to the afterlife or a transcendent realm separate from the physical world.</note></term>.

A truly infinite Spirit, on the other hand, includes the lives of all spirits within his own life, and a truly eternal life includes <term>this side<note>original: "Diesseits." This refers to our current, earthly existence.</note></term> and the beyond as merely two stages of the same life that naturally follow upon and out of one another.

I believe this is simply reasonable; but people want no reason in these matters. They prefer "the knife without handle or blade." <note>This is a German idiom (Messer ohne Heft und Klinge) describing something that is promised or discussed but possesses none of its essential parts—an absurdity.</note> They want the infinite Spirit without the characteristics of infinity or of spirit. He is infinite, yet has finite things alongside him; he is a spirit, yet is unchanging and timeless, unlike any other spirit; "he lives and moves in us, and we in him," <note>A reference to Acts 17:28. Fechner frequently uses scripture to support his pantheistic views.</note> yet he does so facing us and we facing him. People throw up their hands in horror at the "impiousness" of thinking that finite spirits are <term>immanent<note>original: "immanent." To be immanent means to exist within or be an inherent part of something else, rather than being separate from it.</note></term> in God. If one...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of the popular theological view that separates God from the finite world. He has just used the metaphor of a "knife without handle or blade" to describe a God who is defined by having no attributes or connection to reality.</meta>

...would rather strike one's forehead with it and ask how such contradictions harmonize. Now, it is certainly no wonder that where the most glaring contradictions coexist so peaceably, there exists an irreconcilability with the view that resolves them. The appearance of godlessness can indeed be resolved, but such contradictions cannot be resolved.

Among the philosophers, it was mainly Kant who asserted an absolute <term>unpredicability<note>The quality of being impossible to describe using predicates or categories of human logic.</note></term> of God through any categories that also find application in the finite. And yet, he was as little able as any other to maintain his assertion in its execution. For to speak of God without a connection to the finite is to speak of nothing or to contradict oneself. In fact, after Kant put forward that assertion in the *Critique of Pure Reason*, supposedly established it, and thereby withdrew all ground from the thought of God, he himself nevertheless steps upon that withdrawn ground in his two later *Critiques*. In his moral and <term>ethicotheological<note>original: "ethikotheologischen." This refers to theology based on moral laws rather than theoretical reasoning.</note></term> proofs, he conceives of God under categories and attributes that are drawn from the observation of finite relations.

One might say: what you intend may be valid for a finite magnitude; but God is at the same time infinitely higher than we are; there, it does not fit. In His infinite height above us, He remains forever completely incomprehensible to us and entirely incomparable to us.

Yet the very same principle applies to height as it does to breadth. An infinitely high tower can no more exist without the lower stories that it encloses than an infinitely large surface can exist without the partial surfaces it encloses. There are as many steps to climb in God as there are handles to grasp Him; only that we never—

...will never be able to ascend to its full height, just as we can never fully encompass Him. The tower is, of course, only an image; but "high" is only a more general image, and if someone is able to present the objection to me without an image, then I will also answer it without an image.

And upon what does the thought of an <term>infinity<note>original: "Unendlichkeit." In Fechner's philosophy, God is the infinite whole that physically and spiritually contains all finite things.</note></term> beyond all finite things rest? Upon experience, upon reason, upon practical interest? Upon all three, based on a misunderstanding; and a right understanding contradicts all three. And what does this misunderstanding hinge upon? It hinges on the despair of being able to grasp God entirely, as if that were the same as being unable to grasp anything of God at all; and on the other misunderstanding, as if the infinite could be limited by the finite within it, whereas the finite things within it only limit one another—just as one segment of an infinite line limits another, but neither limits the infinite line itself.

One error is linked to another. Just as the infinite loses its content within the finite and thereby becomes a hollow void, even an empty word, so too does the finite lose its encompassment, its vessel, its cohesion, and its bond within the infinite; it crumbles, becomes isolated, and is fragmented. Souls fall apart <term>monadically<note>derived from "monad," a term used by philosophers like Leibniz to describe fundamental, isolated units of existence. Fechner's evocative term for individual spirits that have lost their connection to the divine whole.</note></term>. The bodies of these souls—parts of nature—stand in opposition to nature; the individual wants to have their freedom in isolation for themselves. But because one cannot do without a bond after all, it is replaced by a "dialectical concept," "flowing matter," a "substantial infinite," or this or that surrogate for the spirit; otherwise, one cements realities together through <term>behind-realities<note>original: "Hinterwirklichkeiten." <meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s argument that God (the Infinite) is not a distant, empty concept, but rather the sum and container of all finite things. ...which contains the sum of all finite things as its content, it follows of its own accord that everything <term>finite<note>original: "Endliche." In Fechner's system, this refers to anything with boundaries, such as a single object, a person, or a specific moment in time.</note></term> is the content of an <term>Infinite<note>original: "Unendliche." For Fechner, this is the all-encompassing whole, often synonymous with God or the Universe in its totality.</note></term>; it does not exist in isolation, nor does it have the power to exist on its own. While it is certainly something in itself, it remains constantly subordinate to a higher existence and a higher power in terms of its matter, its structure, its force, its law, and its freedom.

Every straight line is a piece of an infinite straight line; every unit of time is a piece of an infinite time; every space is a piece of an infinite space; every body is a piece of an infinite world of bodies; every organism is a member of an infinite organism; every spirit is a fragment of the infinite unity of all spirits; every temporal life is a wave-beat in the eternal life; every individual law is subordinate to a more general law; and all freedom of the individual is a ray from the <term>Sun of Freedom<note>original: "Freiheitssonne." A poetic metaphor suggesting that individual human agency is a participation in the absolute freedom of the Divine.</note></term> of the One—and for that reason, it is no worse and no less free.

Therefore, let us not be misled by the fact that the objects of faith bear the character of infinity, as if we could not approach them with our experience; for, in truth, we are entirely contained within them with our experiences. We need only correctly account for the character of infinity by never confusing the finite with the infinite, but rather grasping the finite merely as a part, a moment, an aspect, a limb, or a handle of the infinite. The thought of the Infinite with which we conclude will not become deeper, more sublime, more valid, fuller, or richer the higher we think of the Infinite as being raised *above* the finite; rather, it grows through how much we deepen, expand, and intensify our <term>knowledge<note>original: "Erkenntniß." <meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s discussion on the relationship between the finite and the infinite. Having established that the finite is a part of the infinite, he now begins to describe his methodology: the "Principle of Induction" or analogy, which allows us to infer the nature of the unseen higher realms by observing patterns in the visible world.</meta>

...exhaust the <term>finite<note>original: "Endliche." Things with boundaries, like objects or individual lives.</note></term> with our experience, yet we can draw from it into the <term>Infinite<note>original: "Unendliche." The boundless whole, which Fechner identifies with the divine.</note></term> without end.

Because, however, our experiences of the content of the Infinite do not reach into the Infinite itself—indeed, they do not even reach very far into the finite—we must seek the <term>Principle<note>original: "Princip." Here, Fechner refers to his specific inductive method of analogy.</note></term> by which we can reach the possible with that which we *can* grasp. Above all, we must seek to reach the meaning and the <term>tendency<note>original: "Tendenz." The underlying direction or purpose toward which the universe is moving.</note></term> of the Whole, so that through our finite feeling, thinking, and acting, we ourselves may contribute something to its fulfillment in this same spirit. In this regard, I propose the following:

It is necessary to start from the largest possible circle of the **empirical** <note>original: "Erfahrungsmäßigen." That which can be known through the senses and scientific observation.</note> within the realm of **existence**, in order to—through **generalization**, **extension**, and the **elevation** <note>original: "Steigerung." A key concept in Fechner's thought where a property becomes more intense or refined as it moves to a higher level of organization.</note> of the viewpoints that emerge here—arrive at a perspective of what holds true beyond this, in the other, broader, and higher realms of existence. These are realms which our experience cannot reach because of their distance, or whose breadth and height exceed and surpass our experience. This must be done with the caution that we undertake this generalization, extension, and elevation beyond the realm of the **experienceable** only in the sense and direction already established within the experienceable itself. Thus, we should only claim and consider valid for those other, higher, broader realms what becomes all the more generalized, extended, and elevated the further and higher we direct our gaze into the realm of experience, while [respecting] the viewpoint of difference that [is created] by the...

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s explanation of his "Principle of Induction." He is arguing that we can understand the nature of God by looking at the human spirit and "scaling it up" to the infinite.</meta>

...to take full account of the greater distance, breadth, and height of the domain that arises.

If an all-governing sense pervades the whole, a connection of being, action, and essence, and of laws through the entire domain of existence—reaching from the most general, highest, and final to the most individual and lowest—and if reality can only prove itself through **experienceability** <note>original: "Erfahrbarkeit." The quality of being able to be perceived or tested through the senses or consciousness.</note> or a connection with something that *is* experienceable, then this principle requires no proof of its validity; for that very connection is just as much in the spirit of the principle as it flows naturally from it.

God is a **spirit**; what a spirit is, we learn from ourselves; but he is an infinite and infinitely high spirit. Very well, even in our spirit and in the kingdom of our spirits there are narrower and wider domains, gradations of height. Let us expand and **elevate** <note>original: "steigern." To increase in intensity, quality, or scale.</note> further in this direction, expand and elevate until it can go no further, so that God remains always a spirit—just as our spirit is, according to all that makes a spirit a spirit—yet rises infinitely far and high above everything upon which the finitude, lowliness, limitation, and narrowness of our spirit depend.

With this, we will not indeed arrive at an unchangeable, timeless God, exalted above all space; but rather at such a one who includes all change, all time, and all space in the same sense as our spirit includes change, time, and space as forms of its thinking, knowing, and its whole possession of things. What we have of things in these forms, those are the things to us; what God has of things in these forms, those are the things, and what we there...

<summary>Fechner argues that God should be understood as an infinite Spirit by expanding and "elevating" the qualities of the human spirit. He rejects a static, abstract God in favor of a divine being who encompasses all time, space, and change.</summary>

If we continue to expand and elevate in this direction, we will find that the broadest and highest spirit is supported by the broadest and most highly developed organism; that is the world itself—not merely the inorganic, but the whole world, with its origin and all the histories and fates of peoples.

God, as spirit, also has a relationship to spirits. To spirits *beside* him? If so, we would not have elevated correctly. The truly one and only God does not merely tolerate no other god; he tolerates no spirit beside himself, for every spirit is itself a little god. Rather, let us look within ourselves and infer from the "little god" the "great god." My eye sees nothing of what my ear hears; my ear hears nothing of what my eye sees; each of these members, with its own sensation, is a being closed off from the other; yet all are collectively open to my whole spirit. It knows simultaneously everything that the eye and ear and all other senses within me know individually, and it reaches even higher with more general relationships above them. With this, the bridge of **representation** and **inference** to him is broken, and all talk of God remains an unintelligible babbling.

**Man** changes his **body** partially from day to day, and completely over the course of years; he gives it back to the **external world** and draws a new one from it. The soul is not merely a **linking** principle of the body, but also a principle that changes the material and form of that **link**. Now they turn again toward the <term>exterior</term>, and the soul perceives the <term>external world</term> once more. Thus, the <term>soul</term> follows every turn of the <term>activities</term> linked through it, or conversely, let us say, they follow every turn of the <term>soul</term>.
