<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s exploration of the "principle of induction" regarding the soul's survival after death. Here, he expands that logic to argue that just as individual organs can be lost without destroying the soul, the loss of the entire body allows the soul to transition into the "greater body" of the universe (God).</meta>

We can destroy any part of our <term>living body</term> <note>original: "Leib." Fechner uses this to mean the animate, ensouled body, as opposed to "Körper," the mere physical matter.</note>, even the brain; if it is not too much at once, the soul does not die; it does not even suffer, provided that the rest of the body still possesses its means of <term>compensation</term> <note>original: "Vertretung." The destruction of the damaged part is even the best means of destroying the damage, provided the means of compensation are in effect. If we expand and elevate ourselves further in this direction, the prospect opens to us that even when our entire body falls prey to destruction, our soul will not die, indeed will not suffer, because the greater body—of which our body is a part, just as our soul is a part of the soul of this [greater] body—will also have means of compensation for its preservation; and that this will be the final means, provided for in the nature of things, to destroy all the damage caused by age and disease.

Let us consider an image in our eye. The <term>physical body</term> <note>original: "Körper." Here referring to the material substances of the eye.</note> provides the matter, fluids, and forces for it; the soul provides the sensation. The image fades, the matter, fluids, and forces scatter back into the body from which they were gathered; yet, in place of the sensation, the memory of the sensation enters; it enters a new, higher realm from which concepts and thoughts are woven, meets therein the memories of all other and earlier images, and helps to weave along with them the higher life itself. <summary>Fechner argues that just as the soul survives the loss of individual body parts through biological compensation, it survives the death of the whole body by transitioning into the "greater body" of the universe. <meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s argument that the soul’s transition at death is analogous to the transition of a memory within the mind or a child leaving the womb. Having established that the soul is part of a "Greater Spirit" (God), Fechner now explains how our life leaves an "echo" in the world that sustains our consciousness after the physical body dissolves.</meta>

of all other and earlier departed humans—that is, their otherworldly souls—will help to weave the higher life of this spirit. And just as the <term>memory-echo</term> <note>original: "Erinnerungsnachklang." Fechner’s term for the way a sensation persists in the mind after the stimulus is gone.</note> of an image within us attaches itself to an <term>after-effect</term> <note>original: "Wirkungsnachklang." This refers to the physical or spiritual trace left behind by an action or event.</note> which the physical image left behind in us after it dissolved, so too the memory-echo of our life attaches to an after-effect that our earthly physical life has left behind in the world.

Whoever now wishes to ponder exactly where in the world—the body of the Great Spirit—the new seat of our soul is to be found, and what its nature is, let them first ponder—and until they have puzzled this out, they will not puzzle out the other—where in the body of the "lesser spirit" (the part belonging to the "greater spirit") the seat of memory is to be found, and what its nature is; yet however long it may be before we puzzle out the one or the other, the question is no longer about the *whether*, it only concerns the *where* and *how*. And even for the *where* and *how*, enough points of reference for imagination and deduction can be found in our principle. (Compare *Zend-Avesta* <note>Fechner is referring to his own major work, "Zend-Avesta: Or on the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter" (1851), rather than the primary Zoroastrian scripture.</note>, Part III.)

Before birth, the child was tightly enclosed in the mother’s womb; with the tearing of all old life-bonds—which seemed like its death—it steps out into a completely new, free realm, which is not severed from the previous one, but rather one that encloses it, and there the child interacts freely with those who have reached the same stage of life. — If we continue to expand and elevate our thinking in this direction, the prospect opens to us that with the tearing of our current life-bonds—again, seemingly our death—we step into an even freer, entirely new realm, which is yet not severed from the previous one, but rather encloses it in a wider circle, and we will interact even more freely with those who have reached the same stage of life.

<summary>Fechner argues that just as memories persist in the mind through "after-effects," the soul persists in the world after death. He uses the analogy of birth to show that death is not an end, but an expansion into a broader, freer existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his philosophical method. On the previous page, he drew an analogy between human memory and the "echo" of a life remaining in the physical world. Here, he addresses the critique that his system is merely a stack of unproven hypotheses, arguing instead that they form a unified, logical structure based on the "principle of induction" from experience.</meta>

Und so können wir von den verschiedensten Gesichts=puncten ausgehen; nichts bedarf es, als der Erweiterung und Steigerung im Sinne unseres Principes, so finden wir uns von dem Diesseits in das Jenseits geführt, nicht in Widerspruch, sondern auf Grund der Thatsachen des Diesseits selbst; nichts bedarf es, als des Verlassens des Principes, um die Seele rettungslos untergehen oder durch Unbegreiflichkeiten und Widersprüche gerettet zu sehen.

And so we can begin from the most varied points of view; nothing is required but the expansion and <term>enhancement</term> <note>original: "Steigerung." This is a central concept for Fechner, meaning the intensification or raising of a physical property to a higher spiritual level.</note> in the sense of our principle. By doing so, we find ourselves led from <term>this world</term> into the <term>beyond</term> <note>original: "Diesseits" and "Jenseits." Literally "this side" and "that side," these are the standard German terms for the earthly life and the afterlife.</note>, not in contradiction, but upon the very foundation of the facts of this world itself. Nothing is required but the abandonment of this principle to see the soul either perish beyond hope or be "saved" only through incomprehensible mysteries and contradictions.

Auf gleichem Fundament erhebt sich auch der Glaube an die höheren Wesenheiten. Dies zeig' ich in einem folgen=den Kapitel. (IX).

On this same foundation also rises the belief in higher beings. I shall demonstrate this in a subsequent chapter (IX).

Man sagt: aber du bauest eine Hypothese auf die andre; deine Hypothese des Jenseits auf die Hypothese, daß die Welt um uns der Leib eines Geistes, und unser Leib ein Theil von seinem Leibe und unser Geist ein Theil von seinem Geiste.

One might say: but you are building one hypothesis upon another; your hypothesis of the afterlife upon the hypothesis that the world around us is the <term>body</term> <note>original: "Leib." Often used by Fechner to distinguish the living, organized body from "Körper," a mere physical object.</note> of a Spirit, and that our body is a part of His body and our spirit a part of His spirit.

So ist es; nur daß die Gesammtheit dieser Hypothe=sen aus einem Principe fließt, was keine Hypothese. Daß sie sich aber wechselseitig unter einander stützen, vermin=dert nicht, sondern vollendet ihren Halt.

It is so; except that the totality of these hypotheses flows from a single principle which is itself no hypothesis. That they mutually support one another does not diminish their stability, but rather completes it.

Und hier tritt eine Betrachtung ein, die eigentlich selbstverständlich wäre, wenn schon sich Niemand danach richtet, weil man sich überhaupt nicht nach dem Principe richtet, daß, wenn es gilt, die Lehre von den allgemein=sten, höchsten, lezten Dingen auf das Princip der Verall=gemeinerung, Erweiterung, Steigerung vom Boden der Erfahrung aus zu stüzen, man nicht einzeln dies und das, sondern Alles im Zusammenhange in der durch die Erfah=rung angewiesenen Richtung zu erweitern und zu steigern hat, soll es einen Zusammenhang der höchsten, lezten Dinge geben, ja ihre Thatsache nur bestehen; weil sie in der That nur im Zusammenhange besteht. Unser jenseitig Leben kann auf solchem Wege nur gefunden werden, wenn

And here a consideration enters which should actually be self-evident—even if no one follows it, because people generally do not follow the principle—that if we are to base the doctrine of the most general, highest, and final things upon the principle of generalization, expansion, and enhancement from the ground of experience, we must not expand and enhance this or that thing individually. Rather, we must expand and enhance *everything* in connection, following the direction indicated by experience. Only then can there be a coherent connection of the highest and final things; indeed, only then can their reality be established, because their reality exists, in fact, only in that connection. Our life in the hereafter can only be found in such a way if

<summary>Fechner argues that his theories on the afterlife and the world-spirit are not random guesses, but a unified system derived from the logical expansion of earthly experience. He asserts that the truth of these "final things" can only be understood when they are viewed as an interconnected whole.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that his philosophical system is not a mere collection of isolated hypotheses, but a unified structure. On the previous page, he argued that one must follow the principle of induction and "enhancement" to understand the relationship between this world and the hereafter. He continues here by linking our individual immortality to the very nature of God’s existence.</meta>

124

we find God and higher beings along the same path, and the ideas of God and higher beings can only be properly formed and developed along this path if we simultaneously develop the idea of our life in the hereafter in connection with them. If the world, the earth around us, is a grave, then at death the soul falls into this grave along with the body; if the world around us is the living body of God, and the earth around us a God-enlivened part of this body—if the lower is contained within the higher, and finally everything, both here and there, is integrated into God—then our death is merely a special instance of <term>metabolism</term> <note>original: "Stoffwechsel," the exchange of physical matter.</note> and a spiritual <term>change of residence</term> <note>original: "Sizwechsel" (Sitzwechsel), meaning a change of seat or position.</note> within the living body of God, a journey out of the narrow cell into the wider house.

From the other side, the life of God and his most sublime creatures would lose its higher content without our life in the hereafter being <term>preserved</term> <note>original: "aufgehobenes." Fechner uses this in a sense similar to Hegel’s "Aufhebung"—where something is both canceled and preserved at a higher level.</note> within it. If there were only humans, animals, and plants with souls in <term>this world</term> <note>original: "diesseits," the earthly life.</note> without the echoes of their existence in a higher life beyond, then for God and the beings between Him and us, it would be the same as it would be for us if we possessed seeing eyes, hearing ears, and feeling hands, yet what they had seen, heard, and touched were not preserved and continued within a higher realm. The vague heaven of which the nations dream is this higher realm within God.

Understood in this way, everything is rationally connected, and everything can endure. But what makes it so difficult for this principle to take root is that everything can only exist within such a connection. For the <term>common consciousness</term> <note>original: "gemeinen Bewusstsein," the everyday or average mindset.</note>, it is already difficult to <term>enhance</term> <note>original: "steigern," to intensify or raise to a higher spiritual level.</note> beyond the ordinary in even one direction, let alone in all directions simultaneously; people's understanding simply comes to a standstill there.

<summary>Fechner argues that human immortality is essential to the "life of God," framing death not as an end, but as a "metabolism" or transition within God's living body. He suggests that without a hereafter where earthly experiences are preserved, God’s own consciousness would be as empty as a human who perceives things but cannot remember or process them.</summary>

<keywords>panentheism, body of God, immortality, metabolism of the soul, afterlife, cosmic consciousness, Gustav Theodor Fechner</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of his "principle of enhancement." On the previous page, he argued that death is a transition within the living body of God. Here, he contends that his philosophical method is not a flight of fancy, but a necessary middle path between the "low" views of materialists and the "abstract" views of dogmatic theologians.</meta>

and yet, understanding is found only in this continuous <term>enhancement</term> <note>original: "Steigerung," a key concept for Fechner referring to the upward progression or "scaling up" from earthly life to higher spiritual stages.</note>. Is our principle therefore hopeless? On the contrary, the fulfillment of its prospects lies furthest away only because they are the widest and the greatest.

In truth, this principle is anything but a fantastical principle or a principle for whimsical fantasies; rather, it is the only thing that can put a bit and bridle on fantasies in this field; indeed, all fantasies in the field of faith are based solely on the fact that one abandons it.

However, it can be abandoned in two ways: first, by establishing views of the highest and ultimate things that do indeed take their valid starting point from experience, but instead of taking it merely as a starting point, they stop there. They attempt to cover the highest, ultimate, and most universal things with that which is valid only in the finite, limited realms of our experience and through crude analogies with it; such are the views of the <term>materialists</term> and the <term>heathens</term> <note>Fechner uses "heathens" here to refer to those who possess a limited, purely nature-bound or polytheistic understanding of the divine.</note>.

Secondly, however, it is abandoned by establishing views of those things that do indeed keep the viewpoint of the most universal, highest, and ultimate validly in sight; but instead of conceiving it as the most universal, highest, and ultimate of what is *experienceable*, they relocate it into the <term>transcendent</term>, the unfathomable, and the incomprehensible. They tear it away from everything—indeed, they place it in fundamental contradiction with everything—upon which imagination, concept, and conclusion can be based; such are the views of most dogmatic theologians and idealistic philosophers.

By the first path, one arrives at low, limited, unworthy views of these things that reach nowhere and conflict with other particular views, falling into crude <term>anthropomorphism</term> <note>Attributing human physical characteristics or limited human emotions to the divine.</note> and <term>fetishism</term>—

<summary>Fechner argues that his "principle of enhancement" is the only way to avoid the dual errors of materialism (which makes God too small and physical) and dogmatic theology (which makes God too abstract and unknowable).</summary>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of two extremes in religious and philosophical thought. The previous page discussed how the "principle of enhancement" is often abandoned; this page concludes that sentence, noting that when we lose this principle, our concepts of the divine either become distorted or disappear entirely. Fechner is positioning his philosophy as a middle path between crude materialism and overly abstract theology.</meta>

...[anthropomorphis]m <note>The word likely concludes "Anthropomorphismus," referring to the practice of attributing human characteristics to God.</note>, or even, they are lost entirely. By this latter path, one arrives at views of the highest and ultimate things that are empty, unclear, incomprehensible, and ungrounded; views that conflict with themselves and the perceivable nature of things, and are unrelated to it—formulated, understood, and posed differently by everyone. Instead of clear ideas and thoughts, only hollow words remain in the end.

In the sense of the first deviation is the crude <term>idolatry</term> <note>original: "Götzenthum"</note> of the pagans, according to whom God is merely a larger or stronger human, or even less than a human—an ibis, a snake, a piece of wood, or a stone—and according to whom life in the <term>hereafter</term> <note>original: "Jenseits"</note> looks exactly like life in <term>this world</term> <note>original: "Diesseits"</note>. No less so, however, is the unbelief of the <term>materialists</term>, who do not believe in God or otherworldly souls because they cannot see them. It is their view that, because they themselves require a brain for their own small circle of thoughts, the "circle of thoughts" belonging to all things is likewise unthinkable without a brain.

Materialism cannot point to a single fact that would block the path of our principle of expansion and <term>enhancement</term> <note>original: "Steigerung," Fechner's concept that the laws of the universe expand and intensify as they move from the physical to the spiritual.</note>; rather, all of its "facts" belong to the starting point of the very path that this principle follows. Instead, materialism simply contradicts it by using a principle that refuses to allow any expansion or enhancement beyond the human level.

In the sense of the second deviation are those views which hold that none of the <term>categories</term> <note>Fechner refers here to the fundamental concepts of understanding, such as time and space.</note> that apply to us, the finite spirits, are applicable to God, the highest Spirit. According to these views, He is unchanging, timeless, and exalted above space, existing in a totally different relationship to the world than any spirit—according to experience—has to the physical world, and in a totally different relationship to other spirits than is known through experience or logic...

<summary>Fechner critiques both materialism, which limits the divine to physical human attributes, and abstract theology, which removes God from all understandable categories. He argues that his "principle of enhancement" provides a necessary logical bridge between the human experience and the divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of philosophical and theological errors. Having previously discussed the "principle of enhancement" (Steigerung), he now addresses the tendency of thinkers to either separate God from nature entirely or to imagine the afterlife as a place of bodiless spirits on distant planets. He presents his principle as a middle path—a "safe passage"—between crude materialism and abstract, incomprehensible theology.</meta>

any spirit has toward spirits; [I mean] those views that allow nature to fall away from God or matter to drop out of spirit, which give souls a bodiless existence in the <term>hereafter</term> <note>original: "Jenseits"</note>, letting them migrate to distant planets or into an indefinite heaven.

All this I count among the fantasies of philosophers and theologians, which cannot persist if our principle persists.

Our principle grants safe passage between both cliffs, while the ship of faith, as it usually goes today, avoids one only to strike the other, and is tossed back and forth between them. Yet people refuse to allow anything that applies to finite man to apply to God—the One who is sublime beyond all perception and thought—and then they fall back into images and metaphors, attributing motives to God that are matters of the most ordinary humanity.

One beauty of the principle lies in its clarity: the clarity of its expression, the clarity of the path it leads, the clarity of the ideas to which it brings us. Where does anything remain misunderstood? God, the hereafter, higher beings; everything appears, perhaps not under common ideas, but certainly under completely clear concepts—or concepts that can be made clear by connecting them to what is common. For the expansion and <term>enhancement</term> <note>original: "Steigerung"; Fechner’s idea that the laws of the physical world are amplified and perfected in the spiritual realm.</note> of the familiar is indeed its whole secret.

Another beauty of the principle is that it makes us independent of the philosophical dispute over the ultimate essence, the origin, and the fundamental principles of things—matters upon which, until now, no clarity or agreement could be reached, undoubtedly because people search for the essence of things *behind* the things themselves. The conclusions drawn according to our principle remain

<meta>This page continues from page 127, where Gustav Theodor Fechner was discussing the "beauties" of his philosophical principle. The previous page ended mid-sentence with "Another beauty of the principle...", which concludes here as "is that it works correctly." This section focuses on the relationship between empirical facts and philosophical principles, emphasizing that spiritual truths must be grounded in both historical consensus and practical benefit to humanity.</meta>

is that it works correctly, however one may interpret the facts in the realm of the relations between soul and body—whether in this sense or that—provided that one always supports their conclusions with the actual facts, just as the conclusions of the astronomer remain correct regardless of where one seeks the essence of matter.

Yet the principle cannot conjure up the truth nor guarantee it on its own. It is a principle of labor, which becomes more fruitful to the extent that it is pursued more steadily, with more insight, and more coherently in the same direction. Individual observation and the individual person can err, will err, and must err—all the more certainly and severely the more they rely on a merely one-sided view of the nature of things, the more recklessly they expand and <term>enhance</term> <note>original: "steigert"; referring to Fechner's "principle of enhancement" where physical laws are projected into the spiritual realm.</note>, and finally, the more they attempt to isolate their principle from historical and practical principles. For in this regard, the thinker stands in a different relationship to his subjects than the <term>natural scientist</term> <note>original: "Naturforscher"</note> does to his. For the scientist, confirmation through experience replaces every other form of control; historical acceptance and practical utility follow his discoveries naturally. But in the highest and ultimate matters, these things are themselves part of the <term>confirmation</term> <note>original: "Bewährung"; the process of proving a theory's worth through its endurance and utility.</note>. That which cannot achieve historical validity does not deserve to achieve it; and where historical agreement is still lacking, fragments of that agreement or a tendency toward it must at least be demonstrable. That which cannot benefit the <term>salvation</term> <note>original: "Heile"; can also mean the "well-being" or "welfare" of humanity.</note> of humanity remains eternally false. Much more could be said regarding this interconnection and mutual support of the three principles; but it would be too much to say here.

<meta>This page transitions from the general defense of Fechner’s philosophical principle to a specific, major objection. While the previous page argued that a principle must be confirmed by its practical and historical utility, Fechner now addresses the "greatest difficulty": how individual human freedom and the existence of evil can be reconciled with a God who contains all beings as parts of Himself.</meta>

### VIII.
## A Difficulty.

One difficulty stands above all others, one that seems impossible to overcome. It makes ears deaf to our teaching and tongues ready to speak against it. No matter how much I have already said in my earlier writing *) to dissolve the appearance of this difficulty, it is as if I had said nothing at all. And so, I raise my tongue once more to preach to deaf ears.

A feeling runs through this book, much like what <term>Cassandra</term> <note>In Greek mythology, Cassandra was a prophetess cursed by Apollo to always speak the truth but never to be believed.</note> felt—that every truth she spoke was cast into the wind. But when the time comes for the Troy of the old worldview to fall, the wind that now blows in vain will help fan the flames.

If we are <term>part-beings</term> <note>original: "Theilwesen." A central term in Fechner's panentheism, suggesting that individual souls are constituent components of the Divine, much like individual thoughts are parts of a mind.</note> of God, how can our <term>independence</term> <note>original: "Selbstständigkeit." Literally "standing-by-oneself."</note> and freedom persist? And must not our sins and imperfections fall upon Him? Is such a relationship between <term>Spirit</term> <note>original: "Geist"; here referring to the universal Divine Spirit.</note> and spirits even possible?

No one should sacrifice their independence and freedom, not even to the Most High; unless it be that they offer it to Him voluntarily. "I would very much like to accept your views," a friend told me; "but I feel that I am something for myself, and not just a part of another." To be "facing God" and to be "free" are held to be one and the same.

Abomination upon abomination happens daily in the world through <term>human will</term> <note>original: "Menschenwillen"</note>; if this will is to be God's will, and—since it exists within the human spirit—is also the human's will, then what becomes of the holy God?

---

*) <note>original: "Zend-Avesta I. 300 ff., 396 ff." This refers to Fechner's 1851 work, "Zend-Avesta: Or on the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter," where he first detailed his system of panpsychism.</note>

<summary>Fechner addresses the central paradox of his philosophy: if humans are parts of God, how can they possess free will and who is responsible for human sin? He compares his struggle to be understood to the prophecies of Cassandra, noting that people fear losing their individuality in the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>panentheism, free will, theodicy, independence, part-beings, human will, Cassandra, Zend-Avesta</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "greatest difficulty" mentioned on the previous page: the apparent conflict between human freedom and being a part of the Divine Spirit. Fechner moves from acknowledging the difficulty to offering a parable to illustrate why being "part" of God actually increases, rather than decreases, human agency.</meta>

It is true; a difficulty lies here—and not merely a simple one, but a twofold difficulty, reinforced by its internal connections. Yet this difficulty resides only in the half-understood and incomplete implications of our teaching, not in those that are fully and completely understood. The primary hurdle is rather that it is difficult to convince anyone to follow these implications to their full and ultimate conclusion. Furthermore, besides not understanding the aim of our teaching, people mostly do not even know what they themselves desire in these matters.

No one who desires and affirms a God would ever assert that humanity is absolutely independent from Him. For what use would a God be otherwise? Now, it is simply a matter of taking that aspect of dependence and independence from God’s will—which is usually viewed as something external—and translating it into something internal. If we do this, the situation for our <term>drive for freedom</term> <note>original: "Freiheitsdrang."</note> does not grow worse, but better.

A master owned a vast estate, upon which lived a great number of <term>serfs</term> <note>original: "Leibeigene." Literally "those whose bodies are owned." Fechner uses this feudal metaphor to describe the traditional view of man's relationship to God as a servant to a master.</note> who were forced to till the soil for him. One day he said: "I wish to set you free. Each of you shall share in the management and the profits of this estate, in proportion to your own insight and effort. I myself shall retain only the supreme oversight." "No," replied the serfs. "As it stands, we submit to your power; but if we were to share ownership of your estate, we would only then become the true <term>vassals</term> <note>original: "Hörige." In feudal law, those who are bound to the land or a lord's service. The serfs' logic is that sharing in the system makes them more "beholden" than simply obeying an external force.</note> of you and your property."

Human spirits are just such foolish serfs. They believe their freedom is better preserved by standing *opposite* God, rather than by being <term>participants</term> <note>original: "Theilhaber."</note> in His spiritual essence. For the "great estate" is the Divine Spirit, and the "Master of the estate" is the Divine Will.

In our own spirit, the will is merely the highest [part]...

<summary>Fechner argues that human independence is not lost by being part of God, but rather transformed from external obedience to internal participation. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument regarding the relationship between human and divine will. Having previously used a metaphor of a master freeing his serfs by making them partners, he now addresses the technical psychological and theological objection: how can two "wills" exist in one space? He argues that just as our own minds contain many spontaneous thoughts and urges that aren't our "will," the Divine Spirit can contain our individual wills within His greater "highest will."</meta>

...not the only <term>self-active</term> <note>original: "selbstthätige." This refers to the capacity for spontaneous, autonomous action or internal drive that arises from within an entity rather than being pushed by an external force.</note> faculty. How many thoughts arise, how many drives proceed without—even against—our will; should there not then be even more room for self-activity under the highest will in the so much higher and wider divine Spirit? Or is God to consist of nothing but the highest will?

People place the highest step of the staircase opposite the others by setting the divine will separately against the creaturely will. For it to truly be a highest step, it must rather have even lower steps of that same staircase beneath it. Human wills beneath the divine will in the divine Spirit are these lower steps.

Admittedly, do not the lower wills of the subjects stand externally to the will of the King, and is God not the highest King? — On the contrary, God is superior to all kings in that he lacks any relationship of <term>coordination</term> <note>original: "Nebenordnung." In legal or philosophical terms, this describes a relationship between equals or separate entities on the same level. Fechner argues God has no equals "beside" Him; everything is "within" Him.</note> to His subjects. But then His rule over them cannot be an external one, like that of a human king, but only an internal one.

The will of the king himself must exercise an internal rule in his own spirit before any external rule; to be a truly royal will, he must surpass the will of the subjects in the breadth, height, and goodness of this internal governance. And the king only becomes similar to God and a preferred servant of God by reflecting the internal rule of the infinite, the whole, Spirit within this internal rule of his own spirit. By conducting external rule through this internal rule, he helps God conduct His internal rule, in that he helps—as much as he is able—to realize the will of the whole Spirit, which is directed toward the best of the whole. Yet in this, he remains always unlike God, in that his rule...

<summary>Fechner argues that human free will exists within the Divine Will like lower steps on a staircase, suggesting that God’s rule is internal rather than the external rule of a human king.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s theological argument regarding the nature of the "will." He is contrasting human kingship with divine sovereignty, arguing that while a human king rules over subjects external to himself, God rules over us from within, as we are parts of His larger being. He is currently addressing the psychological mechanics of how a human "will" can exist inside a divine "will."</meta>

only a finite and partially external one, while God’s is an infinite and entirely internal one.

The King, who is conscious of this position within God and toward God, is simultaneously aware of the highest dignity and the highest duty; the king who acts in the spirit of this position is a true <term>representative of God</term> <note>original: "Stellvertreter Gottes." Historically, this term often referred to the "Divine Right of Kings," but Fechner uses it here to describe a ruler who recognizes their actions as a localized expression of God's internal governing will.</note> on the Earth; kingship rests on a firm foundation in this sense, and only in this sense; yet, for it to stand thus, the king must not stand *beside* God, but *in* God, and God must not be a king only in the sense in which the king is *not* God.

It is said: how can the relationship between God’s will and our will be compared with the relationship between our will and our <term>impulses</term> <note>original: "Triebe." This refers to our basic instincts, drives, or urges—those internal forces that push us to act before we have consciously "willed" to do so.</note>? Is the relationship between one will and another will not generally incomparable to the relationship between a will and its impulses? Is "will" not something unspeakably higher than "impulses"?

Certainly; but does one not admit—indeed, does one not rather assert—that God’s will is likewise something unspeakably higher than our own will? The shared name must not lead us astray, and the difference must not confuse us. And nothing prevents the ascending relationship between them from being comparable, despite the incomparability of our impulses to our will and our will to the divine will; indeed, if one wishes, the very incomparability of every lower thing to its higher counterpart is itself comparable. Thus, whatever our impulse can do against our incomparably higher will, our will can do against God’s incomparably higher will, despite the fact that it is of the same spirit. Through this, our will possesses the <term>freedom</term> <note>original: "Freiheit." Fechner is solving a classic theological puzzle: if we are part of God, how can we be free to sin or err? He argues that just as our own urges can "resist" our better judgment, we can "resist" God's higher will from within Him.</note> that one seeks for it outside of God just as well within Him; for even if the possibility of resisting God’s will is also...

<summary>Fechner argues that human kingship is a reflection of divine rule only when the king acts "within" God rather than as an independent power. He further explains that human free will exists within the divine mind just as biological impulses exist within the human mind—capable of resistance yet part of a higher unity.</summary>

A truly powerful will within us, however, is able to eventually steer and attune everything that inwardly resists it toward its own purposes and goals. For this purpose, education, punishment, and reward exist in both this world and the world beyond. But this is not a cause for fear, but rather a hope and a comfort.

It is said: but one will and another will cannot exist together at all within the same spirit. Rather, "will" is once and for all a supreme thing, and thus a unique thing in the being that possesses it.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the will within a hierarchical spiritual structure. If it were to stand beneath something higher, it would no longer be the highest, and consequently, no longer be the will of *this* person.

However, all these difficulties—which make it impossible for one human will to exist alongside or beneath another human will within the same person—do allow for a human will to exist alongside another human will beneath the *divine* will within the same God. That is to say, they exist beneath a unified peak of all conscious self-activity in God. Indeed, we might not even be permitted to call this divine authority "will" anymore, if we were to include in our definition of the "will" the lowliness, finitude, and limitations that characterize the will within us.

Tasks are divided within God; the care for the individual is left to individuals, and over the governance of individuals stand individuals to lead that governance. The highest will cannot concern itself with individual details except insofar as they affect the whole. But now, to turn to the other side of the question: how is God's holiness compatible with the fact that He contains within Himself our sins and imperfections alongside our spirits?

Just as both sides of the difficulty are connected, so too is the resolution of both.

One need only frame the concept of God's holiness in such a way that not *nothing*, but *everything* proceeds in accordance with it—just as we see things proceed in the world—and at the same time in such a way that we may hope it moves toward good ends. Let the same standard be applied to God's holiness—only expanded and intensified—by which we measure humanity; for we do, conversely, command that humanity be measured against God; yet one requires the other. If this is done, one will no longer find any contradiction with God's holiness. But whoever pushes the concept of God's holiness beyond any comparability with all things human is himself to blame for finding the content of his concept only in the inconceivable <note>original: "Begriffslosen," referring to that which cannot be captured by thought or logic</note>, and for the fact that human holiness itself no longer finds a prototype in the holy God.

Our will, in its freedom to determine itself independently of the highest will—and even in total opposition to it—is, in its relation to that highest will, something <term>involuntary</term> <note>original: "Unwillkührliches," meaning something that occurs without the primary actor's conscious intent or choice</note> for God. <meta>This page continues the discussion of how God’s holiness remains intact despite the presence of human sin within the Divine. Fechner transitions from the concept of the individual will to a larger musical metaphor for the universe.</meta>

...activity, not according to what is involuntary, or indeed, what emerges and moves within a lower region of his spirit in opposition to him. <note>Fechner is arguing that a person’s moral character is defined by their conscious intentions, not by intrusive thoughts or "lower" impulses that they do not choose.</note> It is enough if the will, as far as it is able, directs things toward the Good and steers them toward noble goals. This is the inherent sense of the **world-order**, which is simultaneously the order of the divine spirit.

The connection is quite simple: if our will is not God’s will, but rather something standing far beneath God’s will or even running contrary to it, then our sin is also not God’s sin. For the sin of a being is a matter of that being's own will; otherwise, it would not be considered that being's sin.

To declare God sinful because our sin exists within Him would be the same as declaring a symphony discordant because it contains dissonant notes. <note>original: "Mißklänge," literally "mis-sounds" or discords.</note> It would only be truly discordant if the resolution of those dissonances were not also contained within it. We expect such a resolution from God for all evil through His own will. And we can certainly place a more certain hope in the ultimate healing and reconciliation of all evil if God must resolve it—like a dissonance in the lower regions of His life—into a higher harmony, rather than if it stood against Him as an external discord.

Comparing the course of the world to a symphony is a frequently used and often striking metaphor. It only fails where a finite image is generally unable to capture the infinite. In our musical compositions, we expect the resolution of a dissonant chord or a sequence of dissonant chords to occur in the very next moment. This is due to the narrow frame of **finitude** <note>The state of being limited or having boundaries, as opposed to the infinite.</note> in which the piece is contained and the conclusion set for it within time; — All

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s musical metaphor, transitioning from the idea of a dissonant chord in a symphony to the "disharmonies" of the world that require time to resolve into a higher harmony.</meta>

...must indeed reach its resolution within a short hour—it requires it to be so. But in the <term>course of the world</term> <note>original: "Weltgang," referring to the historical or evolutionary progression of the universe.</note>, disharmonies can span a millennium, only to find their resolution in a second; they can span an entire life, only to find their resolution in a second; and through completions in time, it proceeds toward ever new and higher completions into eternity. Yet how much is also resolved in a short time—the small within the small, the great within the great of space and time.

The resolution of evil is brought about and consists in this: that within the order of things which it opposes, it calls forth counteractions against itself. These grow along with the evil and finally outgrow it, whereby the evil is not merely removed, but <term>sublated</term> <note>original: "aufgehoben." This is a key term in German philosophy meaning to cancel, preserve, and lift up to a higher level all at once.</note> into good, indeed becoming the source of a new good. In this, evil is distinguished from the good: while the good becomes a source of higher good through the supportive cooperation of the general order, evil—turned and transformed through counteractions—becomes the source of new good. Necessity is the mother of invention; overcome sin is the mother of new virtue.

This resolution of evil does not occur blindly; rather, just as evil only has meaning in the realm of the conscious, its resolution also occurs through effects upon consciousness and under the influence of consciousness.

The righteous human will contributes consciously, through the human order dependent upon it and as far as it is able, to the realization of the ultimate resolution of all evil. Whoever believes in God may also believe that the divine will, with its all-encompassing consciousness, through the divine order dependent upon it, as far as it is able—which is the highest degree—

<summary>Fechner explains that while musical dissonances resolve quickly, worldly "disharmonies" may take lifetimes or millennia to find resolution, ultimately transforming evil into a source of new good through conscious counteraction.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the resolution of evil. He argues that the "human order" is a nested part of the "divine order," and that the consequences of one's actions in this life will eventually necessitate a reconciliation with that divine order in the next.</meta>

...the most general and final resolution; and that the right human order itself is a subordinate part of the divine order.

The bad human will resists this order, and yet it cannot resist it in the long run. Whoever believes in an eternal life may also believe that the following life will, in this respect, be only the concluding act of the current one, insofar as the consequences of one's actions and conduct in this life—rebounding upon the wicked in the next life—will finally force them to fall into line with this order.

And indeed, we may believe both the one and the other because we already see the direction toward it in the order all around us and in <term>this life</term> <note>original: "Diesseits," referring to the earthly, material world as opposed to the afterlife.</note>. We need only generalize, expand, and intensify what we see, since we are dealing with the more general, the broader, and the higher aspects of what we see; and since what we see already generalizes, expands, and intensifies itself in this sense the more we direct our gaze toward the general, the broad, and the high. This is the <term>empirical principle of faith</term> <note>original: "Erfahrungs-Princip des Glaubens." Fechner’s philosophical method suggested that we can learn about the spiritual world by observing the patterns of the physical world and projecting them upward.</note>.

To be sure, if we could arbitrarily imagine and wish for a God according to human fashion, we might perhaps wish to imagine Him differently than what is compatible with a look at the world—whether we think of the world as externally dependent on Him or included within Him. We might wish to imagine His entire being as a perfect inner harmony; we might wish to seek the outward expression of this harmony in the <term>course of the world</term> <note>original: "Weltgang."</note> and the state of the world dependent on Him; and, instead of the prospect of a distant resolution of all evil, we might wish all evil to be removed from the world immediately. In vain; God is not as such thoughts and wishes would suggest.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into the problem of evil. Having previously established that the human order is nested within the divine, he now addresses the classic theological dilemma (theodicy): if God is all-good and all-powerful, why does evil exist at all?</meta>

Evil exists; it prowls around like a roaring lion <note>A reference to 1 Peter 5:8: "Be sober, be vigilant; because your adversary the devil, as a roaring lion, walketh about, seeking whom he may devour."</note>, and it does no good to stop one's ears against it or to close one's eyes to it. Now we ask: how—despite God, the Creator of heaven and earth, of man and the human spirit (whether one conceives of creation as something external or internal)—did evil enter the world, along with this monstrous task of first lifting, erasing, and reconciling it once more? Did God create evil by design, did He willingly permit it, or does it lie in an involuntary side of His existence? Only these choices remain.

For the sake of God’s unlimited goodness, we reject the first option; for the sake of God’s unlimited omnipotence, we reject the third. Thus, He has permitted evil and sin. Why? To allow room for the freedom of His creatures; yes, perhaps even for the sake of love, for in order to have creatures He could love, they had to be His equals in freedom. How can one love something that functions like a machine?

With such motives and similar ones, one seeks to make the divine permission of sin comprehensible to humanity. To be sure, as far as education, law, and custom reach among men—insofar as they are considered "right"—they grant man freedom only to do good, and where necessary, they compel people in this direction as much as they can. Is God alone—who cannot or will not sin, and who has or has reserved for Himself only the freedom to will what is good—supposed to have willingly granted man, His own image <note>original: "Ebenbilde," referring to the Imago Dei, the theological concept that humans are created in the likeness of God.</note>, the same freedom for that which His law forbids as for that which it commands? Why did He not also grant him freedom solely within the realm of good? For we see: even then, a kind of freedom would still exist. — But, it is said, that would not [fit] the concept of freedom

<summary>Fechner explores the "theodicy" problem, asking why an all-good and all-powerful God allows evil. He critiques the common argument that evil is necessary for true human freedom, questioning why God did not limit human freedom to choices between different kinds of "good."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of traditional theodicy. He is examining the logical contradictions in the argument that God "permits" evil to preserve human freedom, transitioning into a discussion on whether evil is instead an unavoidable necessity of a finite world.</meta>

...fully fulfill. — So be it, but God would have removed the "bad half" from him if He had only half-fulfilled it. <note>Fechner is responding to the argument that God could have created a being with only the freedom to do good. He argues that in a finite world, the freedom to do good cannot exist without the possibility of its opposite.</note> — But it was not possible; one aspect of a creature’s freedom is essentially bound to the other by the very nature of its finitude. — Well then, one is saying the same thing I am: there is something against which even God’s will itself is powerless. Thus, one must ultimately excuse God by appealing to the very thing that—if it were granted from the start—would have made the entire excuse unnecessary: namely, that evil exists according to an involuntary—or, if you prefer, a <term>metaphysical necessity</term> <note>The philosophical idea that certain limitations or "evils" are logically unavoidable in any created, finite universe, regardless of a Creator's power.</note>.

If it were true that God permitted sin, or "moral evil," for the sake of the freedom of His creatures, then whence and why does the unspeakable <term>physical evil</term> <note>Refers to natural suffering, such as disease, pain, or disasters, that does not result from human choice.</note> exist, which strikes these free creatures without any exercise of their freedom? — People say it is there as a punishment for their sins. — But does even a human being punish someone for the sake of what he himself permits? Does he also have eternal hells for temporary sins? Does he also let children bear the punishment for the guilt of their parents? Does he also decree devastation without distinction over both the righteous and the unrighteous? If one wishes to explain God's actions through human motives, then one should take the highest, noblest, and best of what exists in human nature as the point of reference for God. And if one does *not* wish to explain Him this way—on the grounds that God is exalted above all human motives—then one should not depict Him as loving, punishing, and avenging like a human at all. One certainly should not use His "exaltation above humanity" as a pretext to attribute to Him inhumanities in which a human tyrant would find a welcome model.

And the suffering of animals, who cannot sin—is this also there for the sake of punishing sin, or for what other purpose is it there? — That belongs to

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of traditional theodicy. On the previous page, he argued that explaining evil as a "punishment" or a "permitted" result of freedom leads to logical contradictions. Here, he transitions to his own "principle": that evil is a byproduct of the world's existence within God, which God’s supreme will works to overcome over an infinite timeline.</meta>

...the unfathomable mysteries of God. — Thus, in the end, the entire explanation becomes an unfathomable mystery; it is merely a **will-o'-the-wisp** <note>original: "Irrlicht". A metaphor for a deceptive or misleading logic that leads the seeker into a dead end.</note> that concludes its path by confessing that it has led us around in a darkness from which there is no escape.

After all this, I prefer the other view—both for its simple clarity and its comfort, as a natural consequence of our principle—that the general ground of evil, insofar as it exists in the world, exists independently not of **God**, but of **His will**. Rather, His will has only the tendency to improve it more and more and to turn it into a **means for improvement itself**, not unlike a righteous human will. It differs only in what the concept of the Supreme Being makes of it: that is, that in this respect He possesses an all-surpassing and ultimately all-conquering power. This occurs not in the moment, nor over every individual evil separately; rather, infinite as He is, it is only fulfilled in the infinity of space and time throughout the entire interconnectedness of all that exists within them.

Unquestionably, this means sacrificing something of the **omnipotence**—not of God Himself, for everything that happens occurs only *through God* and *in God* <note>Fechner is a proponent of panentheism, the belief that the universe is part of God, but God also transcends the universe. Therefore, evil can be "in" God's being without being part of God's "will."</note>—but sacrificing something of the omnipotence of His **supreme will**. Yet, it means sacrificing only so much that it becomes possible for us to maintain true trust in His will in every supreme and final instance—though for every lower instance, there are also <term>lower wills</term> <note>Fechner suggests that humans, animals, and other spirits have their own limited wills which are "lower" parts of the total divine being.</note> and lower powers within God. He who allows evil to arise and persist, whether through God’s free will or free permission, so that nothing is withdrawn from His will, sees in that very will something that desires or willingly permits evil. He who [lets] evil arise independently of God’s will within God, be-

<summary>Fechner rejects the idea that God "permits" evil, proposing instead that evil is an unavoidable necessity within the divine being that God’s will constantly works to improve and overcome. He argues that it is better to believe in a limited divine will that is perfectly good than an omnipotent divine will that allows suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Theodicy, divine will, omnipotence, panentheism, problem of evil, infinity, Gustav Theodor Fechner</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that evil is an inherent part of the world-substance, which God’s will constantly works to transform. He transitions from the theoretical necessity of this "principle" to its practical and comforting implications for human progress and faith.</meta>

...leaves this principle standing, then one can consider this Will—like that of a righteous man—as purely counteracting evil. One can rely with full confidence on the fact that, if even the righteous human will is capable of so much—turning evil toward the better within the small circle of what depends upon it (though certainly not succeeding immediately)—then the divine Will, reaching over everything, will eventually be capable of everything in this regard. And, even if it might need an eternity to improve and heal the infinite evil of an infinite world, it will nonetheless find finite means for the improvement and healing of every finite evil of finite beings within the infinite abundance of its finite resources. The improvement and healing of all evil in the world may well be an ideal, but one which reality increasingly approaches by way of finite steps. After all, chaos has already cleared into the splendor of the blue sky, the shining stars, and the blooming earth; Christian ideas have triumphed over crude paganism; the tortures of the court <note>Fechner refers here to the historical abolition of judicial torture in Europe, viewing it as evidence of moral progress.</note> no longer exist; perhaps, in the progress of things, the "tortures of nature" <note>This likely refers to physical suffering, disease, and the perceived "cruelty" of the natural world.</note> will also one day be mitigated or cease altogether. Look at the whole, and progress is indeed visible. And suppose there were none; would one honor God more through the belief that He produced a world from Himself that was incapable of improvement—even though He could have created a better one—than by believing He carries such a world within Himself involuntarily? <note>Fechner argues it is more respectful to view evil as an involuntary, inherent part of God’s nature that He actively struggles to improve, rather than a deliberate creation of an all-powerful being who could have chosen otherwise.</note>

Or suppose that behind every healed and reconciled evil, new evil always emerged from the ground of things, so that God’s work not only never ended but never diminished—I do not say that I have fathomed the ultimate mystery <note>original: *Geheimniß*</note> in this regard—will it [diminish] the comfort of one who is afflicted by evil,

<summary>Fechner argues that while evil is an inherent part of the world, God's divine will acts as a constant force for healing and progress. He cites historical and cosmic advancements—from the ordering of chaos to the abolition of judicial torture—as evidence of this ongoing improvement.</summary>
<keywords>divine will, theodicy, problem of evil, historical progress, Christian ideas, nature's suffering, healing, infinity</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s theological argument regarding the nature of suffering. He moves from the theoretical idea of God’s will to the personal, psychological comfort found in the "Panentheistic" view—that because we exist within God, our pain is His pain, and therefore His own nature compels Him to heal it.</meta>

...lessen the burden, to know that not only his own evil <note>original: "Uebel." In this context, Fechner uses this word to refer to both moral evil and physical suffering/affliction.</note>, but also all the evil that will grow up after his, is moving toward healing and reconciliation just as his own one day will?

If all creatures are in God, then God feels within Himself the suffering of all—moral and physical, by whatever name it may be called, including mine. He would feel it as His own suffering if His goodness and satisfaction did not consist precisely in the connected resolution of everything that He feels as disharmonious in the lower regions of His being <note>Fechner views the universe as a hierarchy of consciousness; "lower regions" refers to the individual experiences of finite beings like humans.</note>, and if His striving directed toward this did not carry the certainty of fulfillment within itself. He cannot lift <note>i.e., remove or alleviate</note> my suffering immediately, nor can He lift it in isolation; but He can lift it one day. He can finally lift it along with other things through the interconnected use of His means, and He **must** finally lift it, for otherwise, it remains His own. There is no firmer and stronger comfort in the worst suffering, and I think of many times in which I have found it. One can hold onto it when nothing else suffices; for it reaches over everything, just as truly as God reaches over everything and our life in the hereafter <term>Jenseits: Literally "the side beyond"; the state of existence after death within the greater divine consciousness.</term> reaches over this life in God's eternity; it reaches beyond death, which, with the beginning of a new life in His eternal life, brings a beginning of new means.

But this comfort is only to be found for those who stand **in** God, not **beside** God; only for those who stand under an omnipotence that can finally lift all evil within the context of the whole, and under a will that directs itself according to the sense of this omnipotence—directing the powers of this omnipotence—not under an omnipotence that could have lifted all evil from the very beginning, immediately and individually, and a will that does not want what it could do with such omnipotence <note>Fechner is critiquing the traditional "theodicy" problem. He argues that God isn't a bystander who *chooses* to allow pain, but a being who *inherently works* to resolve pain that is a necessary part of the world's development.</note>.

God, Evil/Suffering, Omnipotence, Comfort, Reconciliation, Creatures, Being, The Hereafter

<summary>Fechner argues that because all beings exist within God, our suffering is a disharmony within God Himself that He is fundamentally committed to resolving. This provides a unique form of consolation: the certainty that all individual pain will eventually be healed through the divine process of universal reconciliation.</summary>
<keywords>Panentheism, suffering, divine empathy, omnipotence, theodicy, consolation, afterlife, reconciliation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s theological argument regarding the nature of God’s will and the presence of suffering. He transitions from the idea that God shares in our suffering to a redefinition of divine omnipotence, suggesting that God works through time to resolve evil rather than possessing a magic-like power to vanish it instantly. This addresses the "Problem of Evil"—how a good and powerful God can allow suffering.</meta>

According to contemporary common faith, firm trust in God depends solely on the *need* for that trust—a need so great that it overcomes the most glaring contradictions. Yet, many people still fall into despair because of these very contradictions. If we assign to God’s will—in the sense of our **principle** <term>principle: Fechner refers to his "analogy principle," where God is understood as the soul of the universe, just as the human soul relates to the body</term>—nothing more and nothing less than what belongs to a supreme Will presiding over all human will (starting from our observation of the human will), then we will find no more contradictions to overcome. We will be able to build the most comforting outlook upon God’s will and see it become clearer as our insight sharpens. If one tries to attribute more to God’s will than this, contradictions will rise up one after another, and one will find salvation for their trust only by darkening their own vision.

We may indeed say: God can do everything He wills; but He only wills what He *can* do, and there is much He *cannot* do. He can neither desire **evil** <note>German: *Uebel*. In this context, it refers to both moral wrongness and physical suffering.</note>, nor can He remove it all at once; but He *wills* to remove it as far, as quickly, and as completely as He is able. However, achieving this completeness requires an eternity. We search for the **omnipotence** <term>omnipotence: *Allmacht*, the quality of having unlimited power</term> of the divine will in every single spoonful, when it actually resides only in the bowl as a whole <note>Fechner uses this domestic metaphor to argue that divine perfection is a property of the entire universe and the whole of time, rather than something that must be visible in every small, isolated event.</note>.

No one can see God, and so many deny His existence. One of the most common and powerful reasons for this denial is that the world does not appear to be made in a way that corresponds to the simultaneous assumptions of unlimited **goodness** and unlimited **omnipotence** in a presiding God—at least, not after we have been taught to link these two assumptions in our concept of God. If we abandon the assumption of the bottom- <note>The text breaks off here at the word *boden-*, likely intended to be *bodenlosen* (bottomless or unfounded).</note>

10

<summary>Fechner redefines divine omnipotence, arguing that God is not a magician who allows evil, but a supreme Will constrained by His own nature to resolve suffering gradually over eternity. He suggests that the perceived contradictions in faith arise from expecting perfection in every small detail rather than in the totality of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>omnipotence, divine will, problem of evil, suffering, panentheism, Gustav Theodor Fechner, theodicy, faith and reason</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s theological argument regarding the nature of God’s will and the presence of suffering. Having previously suggested that God works through time according to laws rather than by instantaneous magic, he now addresses the philosophical problem of "Free Will" and how it relates to the existence of a Divine Will.</meta>

...fall away from the vague **omnipotence** <term>omnipotence: *Allmacht*; here Fechner argues against the traditional view of God as a magician who can do the logically impossible</term> of God, and the argument against God will fall with it.

Then one will see the **world order** in a way that corresponds to the premise of a good Spirit, who governs the world according to laws directed toward purposes in the sense of **Ideas** <term>Ideas: *Ideen*; in 19th-century German philosophy, these are the guiding archetypes or ultimate spiritual goals of reality</term>. Now, it may be that someone who finds freedom lacking in human conduct will also find it lacking in the administration of the world; yet, just as little as he can deny the existence of the human spirit and will simply because he finds them lacking in "freedom," will he be able to deny the divine spirit and will for that reason. But whoever finds freedom in the individual human being will find a higher freedom in the creation and history of mankind, and will be able to recognize the dependence of the latter on a free will, just as he recognizes the former's dependence. There may also be a third person who finds the ultimate **point of unity** between law and freedom within the will of God Himself. Thus, the question of the existence and governance of a divine spirit and will in the world does not depend at all on the question of His freedom. And we gladly leave this **dark question** <note>The "dark question" refers to the centuries-old debate between free will and determinism, which Fechner views as a distraction from the core reality of the Will's existence.</note>, which mixes its darkness into everything it touches, now that it has been shown that it has no business interfering here.

It is strange, however, that especially those who contest the freedom of the human will—without being able to contest the existence and action of a human will thereby—nevertheless believe that by contesting the freedom of the world, they can contest the existence and governance of a divine will within it.

---

And what else, in the end, is the entire objection we have considered—perhaps the weightiest against...

<summary>Fechner argues that the existence of a Divine Will is not dependent on the philosophical debate over free will; even if the world operates by law, it still manifests a governing Spirit. He critiques skeptics who use determinism to deny God's existence while still acknowledging the existence of the human will.</summary>
<keywords>omnipotence, world order, free will, determinism, divine will, human spirit, laws of nature, theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s exploration of the relationship between God and the world. Having previously discussed the nature of the Divine Will, he now transitions to the core of his "Daylight View": the idea that all individual spirits are nested within a higher, universal Divine Spirit, referencing the biblical concept of panentheism.</meta>

Our entire teaching, [often seen] as an objection against that most beautiful word of **biblical doctrine** <term>biblical doctrine: *Bibellehre*; the foundational teachings of the Christian faith</term>, that we live, move, and have our being in God, and He in us <note>original: "daß wir in Gott leben, weben und sind"; a reference to Acts 17:28. Fechner argues that his philosophy is not an "objection" to the Bible, but its most literal and logical fulfillment</note>; what else is our teaching than the clear interpretation of this word? It is a word that the **literalists** <term>literalists: *Wortgläubigen*; literally "word-believers," those who profess the letter of the scripture without grasping its deeper philosophical or spiritual implications</term> likely carry on their lips, yet not in their heads and hearts; otherwise, they would carry the content of this teaching in their heads and hearts, for it is nothing but the explanation and consequence of this word.

It is a word equally fruitful for feeling, acting, and thinking; equally healing and beautiful, equally simple, clear, and true, if it is taken exactly as it sounds. In contrast, the intellect has strained all its powers to misunderstand it so that its fruits wither and dry up and its clarity is extinguished. Yet it is only the core word of a more general proposition.

If **human spirits** <term>human spirits: *Menschengeister*; the individual consciousness of human beings</term> can live, move, and be in the highest Spirit, then spirits even higher than human spirits can also live, move, and be in the highest Spirit, and the lower can be in the higher and thereby in the highest.

And that this is not merely thinkable, but is actually so, is what we now wish to consider. We shall see how the **heavenly spirits** <term>heavenly spirits: *himmlische Geister*; for Fechner, these are the conscious souls of planets and stars</term> are **mirrored** <term>mirrored: *Spiegeln*; Fechner’s concept that the physical world is the outward manifestation or "mirror" of the inner spiritual reality</term> in their bodies, how the relationships of the heavenly spirits are mirrored in the relationships of their bodies, and how **sustaining** <term>sustaining: *Tragen*; literally "carrying," referring to the way a higher spirit supports and contains the lower spirits within it</term> is connected with this mirroring.

**Vocabulary:** Biblical doctrine, literalists, human spirits, heavenly spirits, mirroring, sustaining.

<summary>Fechner argues that his philosophy is the true realization of the biblical promise that we exist "within" God. He proposes a hierarchy where human spirits are nested within higher celestial spirits, all of which are contained within the Divine Spirit.</summary>
<keywords>panentheism, Acts 17:28, hierarchy of spirits, mirroring, sustaining, body-soul relationship</keywords>

### IX.

## The Soul of the Stars and the World.

> “And there are heavenly bodies, and earthly bodies. But the heavenly have one glory, and the earthly have another.” (1 Cor. 15:40.) <note>Fechner frequently cites the Bible to show that his philosophy of a living, ensouled universe is compatible with Christian scripture.</note>

The view that **heavenly bodies** <term>heavenly bodies: *himmlische Körper*; here referring to planets, stars, and the Earth itself</term> are also inhabited by **heavenly souls** <term>heavenly souls: *himmlische Seelen*; the conscious, spiritual essence Fechner attributes to celestial objects</term> appears so foreign to the world today that one can only wonder how it could have seemed so natural to an earlier world. One will only stop wondering about this once it appears just as natural to a future world again.

All the reasons that one can put forward for this today bounce off and scatter against the rock of the **fossilized view** <term>fossilized view: *versteinerten Ansicht*; Fechner’s term for the rigid, materialistic worldview of his time that viewed nature as dead</term> that the Earth is a mere stone. This fleeting wave, too, will meet such a fate; yet the rock will eventually be undermined and collapse, and a living sea will flow over it.

One cannot make apples ripe by shaking the tree; in the end, they fall into one’s lap of their own accord; one only needs to sit under the tree. Yet I shake it again and again, to see if anything is ripe yet.

The leaves play upon the oak tree and imagine it is merely a solid playground for their games. People today feel the same way about the Earth, and are just as blind as those leaves.

The little dot over the letter 'i' is vain about its position; man upon the Earth is just as vain about...

<summary>Fechner argues that the Earth and other celestial bodies are living, ensouled beings, contrasting his "Daylight View" with the "Night View" of a dead, materialistic universe. He uses metaphors of ripening fruit and leaves on an oak to describe the slow awakening of humanity to this spiritual truth.</summary>
<keywords>celestial souls, panpsychism, Earth soul, living cosmos, materialism, nature philosophy, spiritual evolution</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s metaphor from the previous page, where he compared humanity’s relationship with the Earth to leaves on an oak tree. He transitions into a philosophical justification for his "Daylight View" by quoting the philosopher J.G. Fichte, arguing that humanity is currently in a transitional state of spiritual "blindness" before returning to a higher state of conscious faith.</meta>

...its position, and it is an equally vain dream that they stand above that within which they stand.

The eye of man is a seeing part of a Seeing Being; likewise, man himself is a seeing part of a Seeing Being, yet he does not see it. <note>Fechner suggests a "nested" consciousness: just as our eyes contribute to our vision without realizing they are part of a larger person, humans contribute to a larger Divine consciousness without realizing they are part of a larger Being.</note>

The current <term>worldview: *Weltanschauung*; a comprehensive perspective on the world and human existence</term> has the eye of a flea upon a bull; the flea would only consider the bull to be alive if it hopped like a flea among other fleas. The bull, however, walks slowly with the bulls of the herd, obediently following the shepherd's staff. <note>This is a critique of materialism; because the Earth does not "move" or "behave" like a small biological organism, modern science mistakenly assumes it is dead matter.</note>

The elder <term>Fichte: Johann Gottlieb Fichte (1762–1814), a foundational figure in German Idealism</term> says once:
> “The entire path that humanity takes here below is nothing other than a return to the point upon which it stood at the very beginning, and intends nothing but a return to its origin. However, humanity is meant to walk this path on its own feet; with its own strength, it shall again make itself into what it was without any of its own doing; and for that reason, it had to cease being so.”

(J. G. Fichte, *The Characteristics of the Present Age*, Collected Works Vol. II, p. 12). <note>The original citation lists "J. H. Fichte," referring to the philosopher's son, Immanuel Hermann Fichte, who edited his father's complete works.</note>

With this, Fichte says *why that most natural faith*, with which the world was so to speak born, no longer exists in the maturing world, and at the same time says that it will exist once more in the mature world. Having arisen from the deepest ground of nature, seemingly without ground—revealed if anything ever was—this faith has only flickered out in order to one day rise again with a brighter, clearer, and higher <term>consciousness: *Bewußtsein*; a central term for awareness and self-knowledge in German philosophy</term>, internally worked out by humanity itself. It will be a consciousness of its own grounds, its entire rich content, and its consequences; and it will then persist by virtue of a philosophy and theology which—

<summary>Fechner argues that humanity’s current inability to perceive the Earth as a living being is a temporary stage of development. Quoting Fichte, he suggests that society is moving from an instinctive, original faith toward a higher, self-earned spiritual consciousness.</summary>
<keywords>Fichte, worldview, humanity, consciousness, The Characteristics of the Present Age, philosophy, theology, spiritual evolution</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of a spiritual awakening. The previous page ended with a quote from Fichte about humanity returning to its origins through its own efforts. Here, Fechner describes the early signs of his "Daylight View" taking hold among diverse individuals, contrasting their openness with the rigidity of established scholars.</meta>

...will reach out their hands to one another, newly risen with it, while for now it is held down by the very forces with which they mutually hold each other down. <note>Fechner suggests that modern conflicting ideologies—like extreme materialism and narrow dogmatic theology—act as weights that suppress a more holistic, living view of the universe.</note>

Indeed, not so much that the hope of its new awakening must remain dependent entirely on a distant future. For after a long, deep slumber, it has, in my faith, already opened a bright eye once more; the sun is rising, and other eyes will follow. And when I, impatient to be watching alone, exert myself to wake those sleeping around me, there is movement here and there.

When I survey, count, and weigh it all, I truly have nothing to complain about. On the contrary: 1) A Viennese Police Councilor, 2) a German colonist who emigrated to America, and 3) a West Indian merchant who moved to Paris, have (during accidental visits) personally and unreservedly confessed to me their belief in the doctrine of the **Zend-Avesta** <note>The title of Fechner's 1851 work, meaning "Living Word." He uses it to describe his theory that the stars and the Earth itself possess souls.</note> regarding the **soul of the stars**; only the North American German found the position of man in relation to the Spirit of the Earth to be far too undemocratic, and suggested important **constitutional amendments** to me, which I was not inclined to accept. <note>Fechner is being slightly humorous here; the American immigrant wanted to apply democratic political principles to the hierarchical spiritual structure of the universe.</note>

What prospects for a newborn view that immediately strikes its first roots through remote lands and across seas! And how shameful for philosophers and theologians to see police councilors, colonists, and tradesmen leading the way in insight into these matters!

More than merely jokingly, the previous examples—to which several more could be added—prove that the old **belief in nature**, even in the face of the perspective distorted by philosophy and theology as they still stand today, does not so harshly contradict common sense as to not—
