->“Man is the measure and the measurer of creation.”<-
<note>original: „Der Mensch ist Maß und Messer der Schöpfung.“ This is a variation of the famous ancient Greek maxim by Protagoras, "Man is the measure of all things." Fechner adds "measurer" to emphasize that humanity is the observer through which the universe understands itself.</note>

Just as the <term>ensoulment</term><note>Beseelung: The state of possessing a soul or being animated by spirit. For the ensoulment of the Earth itself, however, the ensoulment of man must again form the starting point and the anchor. Thus, here too, it is the fact of our own ensoulment upon which the entire structure of the conclusion ultimately depends.

Indeed, even recently, some are so unwilling to allow an analogy between the Earth and other celestial bodies that they do not even wish to admit the <term>habitability</term><note>Bewohnbarkeit: The capacity for a planet to support life. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s transition from the specific "ensoulment" of humans and animals to the broader "ensoulment" of the Earth and other celestial bodies. The doctrine of the souls of the <term>celestial bodies</term><note>Gestirne: A general term for stars, planets, and other heavenly bodies.</note> is divided hereafter, much like the doctrine of the plant soul, into the refutation of counter-arguments and the presentation of positive reasons. The former should be seen merely as preparation for the latter. Here, both will be addressed only so far as to bring the main points clearly to light. Those who are not bothered by any particular objection may skip the refutation of objections entirely and focus only on the positive arguments, which remain the most decisive. Those who are troubled by a specific objection may seek it out among the following and spare themselves the effort of wrestling with those they do not find convincing. Besides, all these objections stem from only a few roots; by cutting those, they all fall away.

## The Counter-arguments.

The general counter-arguments discussed in Chapter 4 against any extension of the <term>realm of souls</term> beyond the human and animal worlds also form the core and foundation of all specific arguments regarding the souls of the celestial bodies.

<summary>Fechner argues against the prevailing view that the universe is a "graveyard" of dead matter, proposing instead that celestial bodies possess souls. He outlines his plan to first refute common objections before presenting positive evidence for a living, soulful cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>ensoulment, celestial bodies, plant soul, materialism, graveyard metaphor, realm of souls, counter-arguments, Gustav Theodor Fechner</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the "ensoulment" of the Earth. Having established that the universe should not be viewed as a "graveyard," he now outlines the specific opposition his theory faces from the four major intellectual pillars of his time: common opinion, natural science, materialism, and theology.</meta>

->153<-

It specifically contradicts common opinion to think of a being as dry, and betraying no trace of free will, as the Earth as being <term>ensouled</term><note>Beseelt: Possessing a soul or vital spirit; animated.</note>. Natural science, going deeper, sees the Earth as a purely astronomical, geological, meteorological, and physical being, obeying purely astronomical, geological, meteorological, and physical laws so completely that it finds as little hint of a soul as it does a place for a soul’s activity. <term>Materialism</term><note>A philosophy that maintains that nothing exists but matter and its movements/modifications.</note>, storming heaven while standing on the shoulders of natural science, finds in anatomical and physiological facts the strict counter-proof against a soul of the Earth; for according to it, the soul is merely a product of the nervous system. <term>Idealism</term><note>A philosophy asserting that reality is mentally constructed or otherwise immaterial.</note>, flying like the eagle toward the sun and viewing the Earth with its tail meanwhile, raises ideal difficulties from the viewpoint of the ideas of independence, freedom, individuality, personality, and so forth. <term>Theology</term>, in the officially guaranteed possession of heaven itself, does not find the ensoulment of the stars among the dogmas that secure its possession; it wishes to allow no pagan gods entry into heaven and to erect no partition between us and God.

All these difficulties rise again like the heads of the <term>Hydra</term><note>A multi-headed serpent from Greek mythology that grew two heads back for every one cut off.</note> as often as one cuts them off individually, until eventually the whole Hydra, which desolates the field of the spirit, is struck down—that is the view which lifts God out of nature and leaves it dead beneath Him. However, if this <term>Herculean labor</term><note>A reference to the near-impossible tasks of the hero Hercules; Fechner views the reconciliation of science, spirit, and nature as a monumental challenge.</note> does not succeed at once, we shall meanwhile decorate the room with the severed heads.

The entire difficulty of common opinion and natural science ultimately depends only on...

<summary>Fechner identifies the four major intellectual obstacles—Natural Science, Materialism, Idealism, and Theology—that prevent people from accepting the Earth as a living, ensouled being. He argues that these diverse objections all stem from a single underlying worldview that separates God from a "dead" nature.</summary>

<keywords>ensoulment, Earth soul, Materialism, Idealism, Theology, Natural Science, Hydra, Herculean labor, dualism</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument against the dualism of Earth and life. Having just mentioned the "Hydra-heads" of opposition—common opinion, science, materialism, and theology—he now explains how the Earth is scientifically inseparable from the organisms that live upon it, moving from a critique of those viewpoints into a holistic proof of unity.</meta>

->154<-

...that one views the Earth, instead of what it is—a coherent material system developing the totality of its **organisms** out of itself, linking them within itself, and finding points of connection in them—rather as something external to its organisms, as something standing opposite them. Yet this is not the case: not **astronomically**, for it moves as an inseparable whole with its people, animals, and plants around the sun, and man, like a stone, contributes to its <term>center of gravity</term><note>original: "Schwerpunct"</note> through his weight;—nor **geologically**, for the organic kingdoms have developed in connection with the Earth’s <term>developmental epochs</term><note>original: "Entwickelungsepochen"</note>, and their remains still lie buried within it;—nor **meteorologically**, for the Earth's air is simultaneously the breath of man, its pressure keeps the blood in his veins and the <term>head of the femur</term><note>original: "Schenkelkopf." Fechner is referring to how atmospheric pressure helps hold the ball-and-socket joints of the human body together.</note> in its socket;—nor **physically**, for the law of the <term>conservation of living forces</term><note>original: "Erhaltung der lebendigen Kräfte." In 19th-century physics, "lebendige Kraft" (vis viva) was the precursor to the modern concept of kinetic energy.</note> of the Earth exists only in the connection between man and the Earth;—nor **chemically**, for the whole man is of Earth and shall become Earth again;—nor finally **teleologically**<note>Teleology: the explanation of phenomena by the purpose they serve rather than by postulated causes.</note>, for everything listed here individually, together with what is not listed, is as purposefully fitted and calculated into a whole as everything in our own small body, which itself fits purposefully into the larger whole.

I want to provide an image; it is, of course, only an image. Let us imagine a large round disc that is well and firmly connected within itself. Someone comes with a <term>hollow chisel</term><note>original: "Hohleisen." A tool used for punching out circular shapes.</note> and punches out small round discs, laying them aside in a pile.

How will we [view] the large round disc afterwards

<summary>Fechner argues that Earth is an integrated, unified system rather than an environment external to its inhabitants, citing astronomical, geological, and biological interdependencies. He introduces an analogy of a disc to illustrate how humanity is "punched out" of the Earth's substance but remains part of its design.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument for the "World-Soul" by using a spatial analogy. On the previous page, he argued that the Earth is a coherent physical and biological system. Here, he uses the metaphor of a large disk (the Earth) from which smaller disks (individual organisms) are conceptually "punched out," leading to the false conclusion that the Earth is a dead, "perforated" remnant while only the small pieces possess life or soul.</meta>

...call it? — Perforated. — And the heap of small round disks? — A piecework.

The large, round, whole disk is the Earth, the truly whole, the firmly and intimately coherent earthly realm; the small round disks are the **organisms**, which one knocks out and piles together for themselves as the "organic kingdom," consisting of humans, animals, and plants.

Is the earthly realm not truly perforated if one removes humans, animals, and plants from its union? Can one not point to the holes with one's fingers? And are the organisms then not truly just a heap of pieces? For indeed, just as they contribute to the cohesion of the earthly realm, they are connected from the other side through it; to separate the two means simultaneously to sever the cohesion of each for itself.

One grants a soul to each little disk; but how can the perforated disk still lay claim to a soul? Naturally, it cannot; but who tells us to perforate it? How can the whole disk claim less of a soul than its sub-disks, since it encompasses them in a higher sense than they encompass their own contents?

Of course, a <term>schema</term><note>original: "Schema." A conceptual model or diagram used to illustrate a point.</note>, an image, cannot be accurate in every respect. The previous one represents—in the most general and crude manner, though perhaps for that very reason all the more strikingly—the crude way in which one views and treats the Earth and, in a broader sense, the world. The world, the Earth, is not a uniform disk. Well then, let one distinguish, yet not divide, where there is merely a need to distin— <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("unterscheiden," meaning to distinguish) at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>Fechner uses the analogy of a "perforated disk" to criticize the scientific and philosophical habit of viewing organisms as separate from the Earth, arguing that the planet is a living whole rather than a dead remnant left behind by life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page's analogy of the "perforated disk." Fechner is transitioning from the abstract metaphor of "punching out" organisms from the Earth to a biological and physical description of how all life is an internal "articulation" of the Earth’s own body.</meta>

...uish. How much is there within ourselves to distinguish that is not actually separated.

Have humans, animals, and plants fallen down upon the Earth, such that one can view them as something merely placed upon it externally? Or have humans, animals, and plants fallen off from the Earth, such that one can view them as something separate or separable from it? Or is their existence even conceivable in isolation from it? None of the above. They are a matter of the internal <term>development</term><note>original: "Entwickelung." In this context, it refers to a natural unfolding or evolutionary growth process inherent to the planet.</note> of the Earth; they are constituent parts of an <term>articulation</term><note>original: "Gliederung." This refers to how a whole body is organized into distinct but interconnected members or limbs.</note> that took place within it and was carried out by its own forces. Even today, they possess existence and the capacity for existence only as such.

The earthly system—which has been coherent within itself from the beginning just as it is today, and is one of those material systems of the heavens that are closed in form and float far from one another—has, in the course of its development, articulated itself into large <term>sub-systems</term><note>original: "Theilsysteme."</note>. These are relatively simple parts that support, encompass, and permeate the whole: the soil, the water, and the air. It has further articulated into smaller systems, closed in form and composed of those larger ones—namely, the individual **organisms**. These organisms are, in turn, sub-divided according to the same principle into large, relatively simple sub-systems that support, encompass, and permeate the whole of each organism—bones, skins, nerves, and veins—and even smaller systems, closed in form and composed of those, which are the individual organs. And from the first to the last stage of this articulation, no separation in matter, action, or purpose is visible.

As it is with organization, so it is with life. The great periods of the day and the year, by regulating the life of the entire Earth, thereby regulate...

<summary>Fechner argues that life forms are not external additions to the Earth but are internal "articulations" of the planet itself, organized in a nested hierarchy from planetary systems down to individual organs.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "articulation" of the Earth. Having established that organisms are not separate from the planet but part of its internal structure, Fechner now addresses the rhythmic cycles of life and the distinction between internal and external relationships within a whole.</meta>

...in the most general way, also that of all its organisms in sleep and waking, activity and rest, and are subdivided in each of them again into smaller sub-periods peculiar to it.

Leaves and blossoms of a plant hang *externally* on the stem; flesh and nerves of an animal hang *externally* on the bone; the entire plant kingdom and animal kingdom hang again *externally* on the ground. Here there is an *external* <term>opposition</term><note>original: "Gegenüber." In Fechner’s philosophy, this refers to how a part relates to the whole or to another part; "external opposition" implies two separate things, while "internal opposition" implies parts of one single system.</note>. But leaves, blossoms, and stems are linked materially, functionally, and purposefully within the plant; the whole plant has all these parts only as *internal* counterparts to itself. Flesh, nerves, and bones are linked materially, functionally, and purposefully in the animal; the whole animal has all these parts only as *internal* counterparts to itself. Finally, the animal kingdom, plant kingdom, and the soil are linked in the whole Earth; it, too, has all these parts only as *internal* counterparts to itself—insofar as one can <term>conceptually</term><note>original: "begrifflich."</note> place the parts as such in opposition to every whole as such. This provides the internal opposition.

The fact that man walks with **freedom** over its soil no more places him *externally* over against the Earth than anything else. He stands only over against the ground upon which he walks, but not over against the Earth, into which he enters along with that ground. This is much like that which moves under the influence of freedom within man himself—and how many things move freely in his head!—which stands over against that in which the free movement in man finds its support, yet does not stand over against the man who includes both.

The branch, upon which the leaf and flower sit, is more simply constructed than the leaf and flower; the trunk, upon which the branches sit, is more simply constructed than the branches; the soil, upon which all trunks sit, upon which...

<summary>Fechner argues that while we see plants and animals as "sitting on" the Earth externally, they are actually internal components of the Earth's total system. He uses the analogy of human thought to show that even "free" movement (like walking) occurs within the greater unity of the planet.</summary>

After everything has been admitted or not admitted, people persist in seeing humans and animals as something externally placed upon the Earth, plants as something stuck into it, and, regarding the question of a soul of the Earth, they see only the question of the soul of a globe or a rotating wheel.

At once the ground, core, and error of almost all objections to our conception of the Earth—from whatever side they have come and will continue to come—is in fact this: that one contrasts the Earth, stripped of its organisms, against the organisms themselves. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that the Earth is a higher-order organism. We have a book that bears the name of the world of which it treats, a collection of the most general facts to be found in all the individual teachings of this world—a valuable book. <note>Fechner is almost certainly referring to Alexander von Humboldt’s *Cosmos* (1845–1862), a monumental work that attempted to unify all scientific knowledge of the time.</note> But the world is not a collection. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal to critics of his "panpsychist" view. He is addressing the objection that treating humans as mere parts of the Earth is a logical fallacy. ...simply a <term>begging of the question</term> <note>original: "petitio principii," a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise.</note> in that I count man as a member of the Earth, who is instead "in an entirely different sense than the Earth—which is merely circled by electro-magnetic currents—a thoroughly soulful existence." "Even you, Brutus!" <note>original: "Auch du, Brutus!" After all, he also counts the strings as parts of the **violin**, and does not grasp them as something standing in opposition to it, despite the fact that they are something far more rich in sound than the violin's case, which gives off only raw noise. The strings, to be sure, are merely stretched over the violin externally and can be separated from it at will; the **human organism**, however, has developed along with the developmental path of the **earthly system** and is absolutely inseparable from it. And herein, an idealistic depth—which is generally so difficult to follow—may find a reason to count the strings as parts of the violin more readily than counting humans as parts of the Earth.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal to critics of his "panpsychist" view. He is addressing the objection that treating humans as mere parts of the Earth is a logical fallacy. Another idealistic opponent, my primary adversary concerning the <term>question of the soul</term> <note>original: "Seelenfrage," referring to the debate over which entities (plants, planets, etc.) possess consciousness.</note>, finds that the Earth—much like the plant—lacks that independence from the rest of nature which would enable it to possess an **individual soul**. He declares*)—despite all the effort I have made to prove that the Earth possesses even greater independence and autonomy than man: “But this is not an independence that the Earth has won for itself; that is, it is not a real, energetic independence that overcomes dependency, but rather a dead, static, and abstract one. To me, on the contrary, what my opponent says seems to be the sign of an imperfect, incomplete *view* of the Earth, and a total disregard for what the *Zend-Avesta* regards and has demonstrated as a sign of its higher autonomy. For the entire first origin, the entire further development, and the entire life-process of man as well as all other earthly creatures—all of which has been pointed out—is fundamentally rooted in the self-unfolding of the Earth, has been won by the Earth itself, and remains dependent upon it.

Is that dead or abstract? Does that look like an imperfect, incomplete life-process? My opponent clearly confuses the <term>soil</term> <note>original: "Erdreich." Fechner distinguishes between the mere physical dirt or ground and the Earth as a living planetary organism.</note> with the **Earth**.

From yet another idealistic side, it is objected that since humans and animals—who are undoubtedly ensouled—move freely, this is unquestionably not the case with the Earth; therefore, the most important sign of freedom

And since the idealist has spoken so much of a "spirit of humanity," he should not likewise miss the freedom of its governance. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of the Earth as a living, conscious entity. He is responding to the argument that because the Earth moves in a predictable, mechanical orbit, it must be inanimate. He argues that this regularity is actually a sign of a higher, divine order.</meta>

no less <term>souled</term> <note>original: "beseelte," referring to the possession of a soul or consciousness.</note> than the <term>polyp</term> <note>original: "Polyp." In 19th-century biology, this term often referred to stationary marine organisms like sea anemones or coral, which were seen as the simplest form of animal life.</note> fixed to the rock; rather, she moves as a whole with physical freedom only upon the reins of a great law, which regulates the entire household of heaven in such a way that her own existence remains secure and firm.

The path of creatures is generally arranged according to their circumstances and needs. As simple as all the circumstances of heaven are, so simple is the path of the **heavenly creatures** through the heavens; as unpredictably complex as earthly circumstances are, so unpredictably complex is the path of the **earthly creatures** upon the earth. What, then, would the Earth—the heavenly creature—have had to seek in heaven with any path other than one left physically free? Nothing of all that which the creatures within her seek. Rather, what the Earth requires for herself and for them, she achieves best—indeed, exclusively—with her firm path, which, by regulating seasons and times of day, regulates her entire inner life.

Therefore, ancient philosophers also regarded the inviolable order in which the stars wander—through which they themselves become authoritative for the most general order of spatial and temporal relations, and through which they prevent everything from dissolving into lawlessness—as one of the signs of their high divinity. Who is right now, the **Ancients** or the **Moderns**? Those who drew their concepts of the divine in nature *from* nature, or those who, with their concepts about nature, banish the divine *from* nature?

By the very fact that the Earth moves so completely bound by a firm law of nature, integrated into the general system of the stars, the latter <note>Referring to the "Moderns" or materialist thinkers of Fechner's time.</note> wish to prove that she is un-independent and absorbed into nature, and they prove ever 

<summary>Fechner argues that the Earth's predictable orbital path is not a sign of soul-lessness, but a necessary "firm path" that regulates its internal life and seasons. He contrasts the ancient view of celestial order as divine with the modern view that sees such regularity as merely mechanical and devoid of spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner's philosophical defense of the Earth as a conscious, living being. He is currently refuting arguments from "Idealists" (who focus on abstract concepts) and "Materialists" (who focus only on physical anatomy) who deny the Earth has a soul.</meta>

—only so that they might derive it from a concept, whereas the opposite proves itself directly in the facts of the matter. The Earth, to be sure, is integrated into the general **lawfulness of nature** through its external relationships, just as the human being is in the end; yet, she demonstrates that she is not merely a dependent part of it by the fact that her position and manner of movement in the heavens, the relationships of periodicity, gravity, heat, and light, her system of meteorology, and the entire development—the whole construction and expansion of her inorganic and organic kingdoms—are unique to her, being formed and ordered quite differently than in any other celestial body. For that reason alone, she cannot be absorbed into nature as something less independent than the human being, because the human himself belongs to those factors through which she raises herself individually and independently out of nature.

Enough of the objections of the **Idealists**, who are really so little idealistic. It would be impossible, of course, because it is an infinite task, to foresee and take into account all the objections that might still be raised by such people. It would also be fruitless, because it will always be possible for them to twist some concept into an objection, and they will always feel free to ignore the demonstration of the matter or declare it a <term>begging of the question</term> <note>original: "petitio principii," a logical fallacy where the person assumes the truth of the very thing they are trying to prove.</note>.

The **Materialist**, naturally, finds the nervous system and the central organ of the soul <note>The "central organ" refers to the brain as the supposed seat of consciousness.</note> missing in the Earth, just as he does in the plant. To be sure, she has brains and nervous systems enough, in that she carries our own within her, but these are not themselves linked together by nerve fibers, and the Materialists naturally demand nerve fibers for this purpose. Why? Because they are necessary in humans to connect the nerve cells into ganglia <note>A ganglion is a cluster of nerve cell bodies that acts as a local command center in the body.</note> and to connect the ganglia themselves. On the contrary, however, because the nerve fibers serve to connect the gang—

<summary>Fechner argues that the Earth's unique physical and organic systems prove its independent "individuality" against Idealists and Materialists, specifically countering the claim that the Earth lacks a soul because it lacks a unified nervous system.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal against Materialists. Having previously discussed the brain as the "central organ" of the soul, he now addresses the physical connections between individual beings and the cosmos.</meta>

ganglion cells and nodes in humans; if so, they would not be able to serve a second time to unite the nervous systems of different humans into a higher being, for otherwise one would have nothing more than a **larger** human. But, it is said, something must be there for the sake of connection—and what would it be? Well, can the Materialists first tell me what it is in the nervous system itself that actually connects the ganglion cells? The protein strands <note>original: "Eiweißstränge," referring to nerve fibers</note>, being dead matter, cannot do it—and they do not do it as dead matter.

I think—since at the bottom of it **one** of us knows as much as the **other**—it will be that same ether-vibration <note>The 19th-century scientific theory of "Luminiferous Aether," a medium then thought to carry light and electromagnetic waves through space.</note> which also connects the suns. Partially free, and partially tethered to the vibrations of weighable matter <note>original: "Wägbaren," referring to "ponderable" or physical matter with mass, as opposed to the weightless ether.</note>, it passes back and forth with every glance, every word, every encounter and action between human beings, carrying the stirrings of sensation and thought from one nervous system into another. Why should it suddenly become unfit between nervous systems to mediate and carry in a larger system that which it empirically mediates and carries within the human system itself, and transmits between different human systems? Is a human being any less **ensouled** when he takes off his coat to move more freely? The nerve fibers are merely coats, discarded by the ensouled vibration outside so that it may move more freely. The Materialist loathes almost nothing more than mysticism, yet he is the greatest mystic of all by believing in a mystical force that enables the ether-vibration within the human to be the carrier of conscious sensation, but denies it that ability outside.

Undeniably, of course, the vibrations that run back and forth between human beings would be capable of nothing and

<summary>Fechner argues that consciousness is not trapped within the physical "coat" of the nerves, but is carried by "etheric vibrations" that connect humans to each other and to the larger universe. He critiques Materialists for assuming that consciousness can only exist within biological structures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s rebuttal against Materialists. Having previously discussed the brain as the "central organ" of the soul, he now addresses the physical connections between individual beings and the cosmos. ...would mean nothing without the humans who unite them into a system, just as they themselves are only united into a system by means of these very vibrations; they would not even exist in their current form without humans. But is it any different with the vibrations that may run back and forth between the ganglia <note>original: "Ganglien," clusters of nerve cell bodies</note>? And if a single soul encompasses the plurality of these small centers linked into a system, why should a spiritual rift exist between the plurality of large centers, which are no less linked by intervening vibrations? To be sure, the ganglion cells are fixed in place, whereas humans and animals are mobile; but this does not weaken or loosen the connection, but rather strengthens and multiplies it.

In all of this there lies no proof; but we are not concerned with proofs yet, but rather first with the apparent counter-proofs.

The whole Earth is, in general, a single, self-coherent, ordered system, as much permeated by the Aether <note>A 19th-century scientific concept of an invisible medium that fills all space and carries light waves.</note> as it is immersed in it; and thus all movements of the Aether within it combine into an ordered system, one that merely allows for greater freedom than the systems within ourselves. Organic creatures themselves are merely the most important nodes <note>original: "Knotenpuncte," or junction points in a network</note> of this interconnectedness.

Now, let the Materialists go ahead and declare the church, state, science, art, trade, and industry to be products of matter; but in doing so, they themselves admit that human nervous systems are materially connected by something and toward something that is a carrier or—if they prefer—a producer of higher spiritual relationships than occur within the individual human. How else could even one human understand another? But if one human can [understand] another through the external mediation of language and writing...

He challenges Materialists to explain how social structures and mutual understanding could exist without such a connective medium.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner’s argument for the Earth having a soul. Having previously discussed how human social systems (like the church and state) are unified by spiritual connections, he now addresses the Materialist objection regarding the physical "central organ" or brain of the planet.</meta>

...stand; why should the being that encompasses all humans, with all their languages and writings, into a single whole, not also understand them inwardly?

Materialism <note>A philosophical view that everything, including thought and consciousness, results from material interactions.</note> prides itself on its firm, sure gait upon solid ground; and indeed, it proceeds straight and correctly upon the level ground until it comes to a mountain. But here, it says, the world is at an end.

It is like the oyster which, with one gulp from the sea, firmly closes its shell and says: "This is the sea."

But can the Earth be excused from having a central organ <term>Centralorgan; the biological "headquarters" or seat of consciousness, like a brain</term>, when we otherwise see that a central organ of conscious life is the representative of unity for every higher soul? A brain may be missing in the polyp <note>A simple, stationary aquatic animal, like a coral or sea anemone, used here to represent the lowest rung of biological complexity.</note>; but even the beetle possesses one above the level of the polyp. Fish, bird, dog, and human demonstrate, in sequence, an ever more richly and powerfully developed brain as a foundation for their growing spiritual heights. If the Earth is to possess a spiritual level <note>original: "Geistesstufe"</note> above all these stages, then it must also possess a more powerful, more highly developed central organ than all the others. And yet, where is anything comparable to a brain to be found within the Earth?

Quite easily—if we only permit the fulfillment of the same demand we just made. That is to say, we must not demand the human brain all over again, but rather the expansion, intensification, higher cultivation, and development of that which we see in the human brain. The Earth cannot satisfy a demand that is too small; it can only satisfy a very large one, because only the latter is truly sufficient.

<summary>Fechner critiques Materialism's narrow view of the world and argues that the Earth must possess a "central organ" of consciousness that is far more advanced than the human brain, rather than identical to it.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner’s argument for the Earth having a soul. Having previously discussed how human social systems are unified, he now counters the objection that the Earth lacks a brain-like "central organ" by redefining what a brain actually is: not a point, but a complex space of activity.</meta>

Let us first take a proper look at the brain within the human being himself. In us, too, the brain is not a single point, nor a small space, but rather an extensive region with nerves, vessels, and membranes; it does not occupy the center, but rather the upper space of the human. In humans, compared to other creatures, the hearth of conscious life has simply concentrated more into the specific region of the brain, whereas in the worm or the polyp <note>A simple aquatic animal like a sea anemone, used here to represent the lowest rung of biological complexity.</note>, one part hardly has any advantage over another. 

Now, in the same sense—only in a far greater proportion than that by which a human surpasses the worm beside him—the Earth unspeakably surpasses the human contained within her. This is because all conscious life has contracted itself relatively more narrowly and strictly into the upper space of the Earth. Here is the arena for church, state, science, art, trade, and industry; even the entire sensory life of animals and plants, instead of radiating into the depths through thick nerves as it does in us, is involved within this upper central space <term>Centralraum; here referring to the surface and atmosphere of the Earth where all life and human activity occur</term>. No creature on Earth is centralized to the same degree as the Earth itself. Does one still wish to search for a single midpoint in that space, from which everything radiates and toward which everything flows? Let someone show it to me first in the human brain itself. Indeed, let someone show me even anything that points remotely to such a thing.

Spiritual unity <note>original: "geistige Einheit"</note> is simply not tied to a single point; rather, the spirit is itself that which links the physical multiplicity together.

And if a monadologist <term>Monadolog; a philosopher who believes the soul is an indivisible, point-like entity called a "monad"</term> insists on binding the soul to a single point, why should he be more embarrassed to find a tiny point for it in the Earth than in a human, since even here he only finds it through

It ought to lend gravity to a view that simply ties the knot of this encounter. Solid, spherical angels certainly do not please us; but this distaste is merely our own conception of the matter, educated as we are to see the world only as a **globe**. I was walking outside on a spring morning; the crops were turning green, the birds sang, the dew sparkled, smoke rose from chimneys, and here and there a person appeared. A transfiguring light lay upon everything. It was only a tiny patch of Earth; it was only a small moment of its existence; and yet, as I grasped this with an ever-widening gaze, it seemed to me not only so beautiful, but so true and clear, that the Earth is an angel. The spirit—the only model from which we could form the concept of spirit—which is our own, certainly links its moments of consciousness into a higher, overarching unity of consciousness, not an unconscious unity or one conscious only of individual moments; and it is only insofar as it is such a unity that it is called "spirit," and those moments are called "moments of a spirit." Now, our spirits are undeniably conscious in a higher sense than the individual moments within our spirits; but instead of concluding that the Spirit of which people speak—the one that links our spirits and carries them within itself as moments—must again be conscious in a higher sense than these, its conscious moments (in order for us to speak of it as Spirit and of them as moments of a Spirit), people believe they can entirely dispense with a corresponding consciousness for it, like the one our spirit possesses. They think they have established the most sublime view simply by assigning the name "Spirit" to the unconscious connection of conscious spirits, or to the mutual reflection of their consciousness, or to an idea of their connection known to no one but the individual spirits; and by assigning the name "Life" to the chaining together of earthly, physical activities.

Thus, they allow at most for a soul on the level of a "polyp-soul" for the Earth; or, since their humans, animals, and plants do not actually hang together like the limbs of a polyp, they would rather allow for no soul at all.

But the Earth does not stretch its humans, animals, and plants indifferently toward the heavens, as the polyp does its limbs. Rather, in their mutual interaction, there exists a higher life—one that connects the individuals and reaches beyond them—in a way that does not exist between the limbs of a polyp. The Earth does not merely carry uniform and identical organs like the polyp, but rather a diversity and a gradation of organisms. These are connected to one another no less firmly than the limbs of a polyp, but even more so; for one can separate the limbs of a polyp from each other, but not humans, animals, and plants from one another, because they cannot be separated from the common trunk of the Earth. And finally, the soul of the Earth cannot merely be of the same rank as a polyp-soul, because the height of <term>psychic unity: The level of complexity and integration of a consciousness.</term> depends on what it contains and encompasses within itself. After all, our brain essentially appears merely as a skin tightly folded within the <term>cranial capsule: the protective bony structure of the skull.</term>, and its mass is small compared to the mass of the entire human being. Now, the main difference between the Earth and a human in this regard is only this: that the corresponding organ of the Earth, instead of being enclosed in folds within an Earth-skull, is spread out over it and thus open to the same heavens toward which our brain, within and with its capsule, is merely oriented; and that its entire expansion—consisting of air and sea, fertile soil, forests, fields, cities, villages, roads, rivers, postal services, ships, letters, telegraphs, books, money, humans, animals, and plants—is proportionally much less compared to the mass of the whole Earth than that of the brain is to the whole human. Yet, it is equally the theater of a conscious <term>interaction: the original German "Verkehr" encompasses communication, commerce, and social movement.</term> that gains its foundation and stability only within the context of the whole. For if you take the interior out of the Earth, everything collapses and falls apart, just as if you took all the internal organs out of a human; by doing so, you do not just take away the stabilizing and filling mass, you also remove from the Earth the gravity that unites everything around the center of the Earth, gives direction to plant roots and rivers, and provides footing and stability to cities and the human foot.

...and 15), how comparisons of the Earth with the human can be carried out in the most diverse directions up to certain limits, without contradicting the facts, and yet can never be fully realized. This is because the Earth is not identical to its organisms, but is rather the broader, higher, and unifying whole of those organisms.

The difficulties encountered thus far were merely on the surface; they are swept away with a few light strokes. Nevertheless, I do not imagine that I can thereby wash away the dark stains of deep-seated opinion <note>original: "den Mohren der Meinung zum Weißen machen" - literally "to wash the Moor of opinion white." This is a German idiom meaning to attempt an impossible task or to try and change a fixed, stubborn mindset.</note> or even cause it to shed its old skin entirely. Some objections only present themselves upon deeper investigation and can then only be resolved by going deeper still. Indeed, insurmountable difficulties arise from all sides if one leaves behind the principle of expansion and escalation <term>Steigerung</term> <note>Fechner uses "Steigerung" (escalation or intensification) to describe how qualities found in "lower" organisms are amplified and transformed into higher forms in the Earth-organism.</note>—which allowed us to surmount the first difficulties—and instead attempts to directly transfer the narrow perspectives and measures of human physiology and psychology onto those of the higher being, rather than merely seeing them as the anchor and starting point for expansion and escalation.

In Rome, the Pope rules; in Constantinople, the Sultan; in Tibet, the Dalai Lama. One faith curses the other. Hegel and Herbart <note>Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770–1831) and Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) were contemporary German philosophers whose systems were fundamentally opposed; their followers were often in sharp intellectual conflict.</note>, what contradictions they represent! Everywhere there is war between nations. Could such things coexist in a unified human soul?

Certainly not in a human one. But given that the human soul itself proves that conflicting sensations, feelings, ideas, and aspirations [can exist] in...

And indeed, we recognize the direction toward this. Religion, science, and politics clearly show a tendency toward an increasingly unified and harmonious conception.

We cannot penetrate the region where the consciousness of this unified striving resides, but it can and should suffice for us to perceive the signs of such striving.

...ask: how can it happen in such a great soul? Let us observe how things truly happen in this great soul, and we will thereby learn how it can happen in such great souls. However, we must not let what we can see immediately limit our view of the entire realm of the spirit above us. It completes itself above our earthly realm, just as the interconnected realm of our memories and thoughts exists above the realm of our direct perceptions. ## **The Positive Grounds.**

The stones have been cleared away, and the plowshare <term>plowshare: the cutting blade of a plow; Fechner uses this metaphor to signal that the "ground" has been prepared and the deep analytical work can now begin.</term> can now begin to cut in.

Until now, patience was required; now, sharpness is needed—at least as much as is appropriate for arguments of faith.

However much the soul of the celestial bodies <note>original: "Gestirne" - stars or planets.</note> stands above the plant-soul, both nevertheless fall under the same concept of "soul," and the general reasons for the existence of one and the other are the same. They are merely applied differently to each, and their relative weight in specific instances differs.

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s argument for the "Earth-soul." Following his previous discussion on the structural similarities between plants and higher beings, he now applies the "argument of analogy" to the Earth itself, suggesting that the planet exhibits the same qualities—only on a much grander scale—that we associate with the presence of a soul in humans and animals.</meta>

First of all, every viewpoint put forward in the argument of analogy or similarity regarding the plant can be repeated word-for-word in reference to the Earth. All the general signs of a soul-capacity <note>original: "Seelenanlage" – the latent potential or structural foundation for a soul to exist.</note> based on these points can be found again in the Earth, only in a higher and more rigorous sense.

For is the Earth not also—just like our own body and the bodies of animals and plants—a being bound together as a unity in form and content? Is it not individually self-contained, standing relatively independent against similar (though not identical) systems? Does it not determine and unfold itself from within, under the stimulation and influence of the outside world? Does it not give birth to an inexhaustible variety of effects from its own abundance and creative power <term>creative power: "Schöpferkraft," the inherent ability of a being to generate new forms and actions</term>—effects that are determined by law on one hand, yet unpredictably new on the other? Does it not encompass relationships of coordination, superiority, and subordination? And is it not a being engaged in continuous development through periodic cycles and the rising and falling of activity?

In all these signs, by which a body is able to resemble a soul—signs that our own body possesses along with its soul and loses without it—the Earth stands not below, but above its humans, animals, and plants. It possesses everything, only in an inexpressible expansion and intensification.

Now, all these signs here, as with the plant—and indeed as with the human—may not yet signify a truly awake soul, but rather only the disposition for an awake soul, the essential preliminary and accompanying conditions for being awake <note>original: "Wachsein" – refers to active consciousness or awareness as opposed to a dormant state.</note>. However, the signs of awakening and being awake, through which the argument must first be fulfilled and the symbol of the soul filled, are found in the Earth much more directly than in

<summary>Fechner argues that the Earth possesses all the physical and structural hallmarks of a soul-bearing being—such as unity, self-determination, and creative power—on a scale far exceeding that of humans or plants.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s philosophical argument for the existence of an "Earth-soul." He now transitions from structural similarities to the shared experience of consciousness, arguing that because we are awake within the Earth, the Earth itself must be considered a conscious, waking being.</meta>

...of the plant, in the awakening and waking state <term>waking state: "Wachsein," the state of being conscious and active as opposed to sleeping or latent</term> of all earthly creatures themselves. For, if a person cannot be called sleeping when their eyes and ears are awake, then neither can the Earth be called so, in which thousands upon thousands of eyes and ears are awake, and the entire human and animal commerce <note>original: "Verkehr" – refers here to the sum of all social, biological, and physical interactions and activity.</note> is awake. In the plant, we could only infer its state of being awake through analogy; with the Earth, however, we participate in its waking state and thus also have a share in the direct experience of this waking state itself.

We lack nothing except the direct experience of the enclosure of this entire waking soul-content <note>original: "Seeleninhalt" – the totality of perceptions, thoughts, and feelings that occupy a consciousness at any given time.</note> and soul-life within a corresponding unity—a unity in which we might feel our own soul-content and soul-life to be contained. This experience we cannot have, nor can we demand it, because this containment is above our own; we would have to possess the entire soul of the Earth itself to hold its unified enclosure within us, whereas we only enclose a portion of its content as a smaller circle within its larger circle. On the contrary, within the general signs of the soul-structure <note>original: "Seelenanlage" – the fundamental capacity or inherent design for a soul to exist.</note>, in which the Earth not only agrees with us but surpasses us, the signs of a corresponding unity of the soul are also included. The direct experience of the awakening and waking state of our own souls within hers serves only to complete the conclusion: that there is a soul which is not merely designed for awakening, but is truly awake.

We also see, wherever we turn our gaze toward what is visible, how the Earth surpasses the containment of our own physical system through a higher and purer containment; the visible, however, must serve for us as a sign of the invisible.

In humans, animals, and plants, every part is organized with purposefulness <term>purposefulness: "Zweckmäßigkeit," the philosophical concept of teleology, or the idea that parts of a system are designed to achieve a specific end</term> in itself; yet this purposefulness con—

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s argument for the Earth as a living, unified organism. On the preceding page, he discussed how humans participate in the Earth's "waking state." ...exists only insofar as it enters purposefully, along with other parts, into the larger, purposefully linked whole of the entire creature, which has integrated <note>original: "geschöpft" — a wordplay on the German word for "creature" (Geschöpf), suggesting the Earth has both brought forth and contained these parts within itself.</note> all parts and systems within its own unified context. The entire kingdoms of humans, animals, and plants, however, are themselves merely systems, purposefully ordered insofar as they enter—together with each other and with the systems of soil, water, and air—into the larger, purposefully linked whole of the Earth, which has integrated all creatures and systems within itself in a unified context.

The creatures of the Earth stand only in external functional relationships <note>original: "Zweckbeziehungen" — relationships defined by a specific purpose or function within a larger design.</note> to one another; for the Earth, however, all these functional relationships are internal and self-contained within her.

Individual earthly creatures pass away; individual earthly creations pass away, one after another. Their specific purpose does not make them permanent. Only the Earth persists as the eternal source of all these creatures and creations, and the constant change—the destruction of one for the sake of another—belongs to the richness of the life enclosed within her.

In humans and animals, there is a cycle of matter <term>cycle of matter: "Kreislauf der Stoffe," referring to metabolism or the recycling of nutrients and physical elements through a body.</term> that branches off into smaller loops, which in turn branch into even smaller ones; but all these cycles of matter in humans and animals are themselves merely branched loops of the larger, general cycle of matter between the organic and inorganic kingdoms of the Earth. Within the Earth, however, all substances circulate purely for themselves; she gives nothing away and receives nothing, except for the light and the warmth of the heavens. She alone—not the human being—is a self-contained being; she revolves purely around herself and within herself, and encompasses all the cycles of humans and animals within her own. Even her self-containment is only relative; yet at this level of beings...

<summary>Fechner argues that individual organisms are merely subsystems within the Earth, which functions as the only truly self-contained, eternal source of life and matter. <meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s philosophical argument for the Earth as a living, conscious entity. He moves from discussing the physical containment of humans within the Earth to the Earth’s own place in the cosmos, introducing his "argument of completion" and "argument of gradation" to suggest that celestial bodies are the living inhabitants of the ether, serving as an intermediate spiritual step between humanity and God.</meta>

the Earth stands unspeakably above the human being, whom she encloses.

A person’s hand is mobile upon their arm; the arm is even more freely mobile upon the torso; the whole person is even more freely mobile upon the Earth; and yet, the person remains just as firmly—or rather, even more firmly—bound to her, and adapted to her with the soles of their feet.

Only the whole comprised of human and Earth is no longer bound to anything else; it floats quite freely in the heavens, and only an immaterial law <note>The "immaterial law" refers to gravity and orbital mechanics, which Fechner views as a divine or spiritual ordering principle rather than just a blind physical force.</note> binds the Earth's course—and is permitted to bind it, because all freedom exists within her.

How much more have I detailed in the same vein in my earlier writings!

As an argument of completion <note>original: "Argument der Ergänzung" — a logical principle suggesting that nature does not leave gaps; if a medium exists, it must have inhabitants.</note>, one can assert that since all elements have their creatures adapted to life within them, the ether <term>ether: "Aether," historically considered a refined substance filling the upper regions of space beyond the atmosphere.</term> would have none if they were not the stars <note>original: "Gestirne" — here meaning celestial bodies in general, including planets and suns.</note>, which, in fact, are entirely adapted to life therein. (For more on this, see *Zend-Avesta* <note>Fechner’s major work, "Zend-Avesta: Or on the Things of Heaven and the Hereafter" (1851), where he outlines his panpsychist worldview.</note> Vol. I, p. 234 ff.)

The argument of gradation <note>original: "Argument der Abstufung" — the idea of a "Great Chain of Being" where every level of complexity has a corresponding level of consciousness.</note> can, depending on its application, reveal the soul of the stars as an intermediate stage between us and God, or as a continuation of a hierarchical structure within us, or finally, through a comparison of the internal and external scales of creatures.

1) Physically, the Earth forms an individual intermediate stage between the human body and the world, insofar as she partially includes the human body, is partially included by the world, and stands in opposition to other celestial bodies even more individually than one person does to another. Now, insofar as the upper and lower of these physical stages also support the spiritual stage which they reflect, it must be concluded that the physical intermediate stage—

That the Earth reflects an individual level of spirit <term>level of spirit: "Geistesstufe," referring to a specific rank or degree in a hierarchy of consciousness.</term> is to be carried over from the *argument from similarity* into this present one; in this way, it serves to reinforce the former. This conclusion is met by the need humans have felt from time immemorial to assume individual **intermediate beings** <note>original: "Mittelwesen" — beings such as angels or planetary spirits that bridge the gap between the mundane and the divine.</note> between man and God, and thereby to populate the heavens.

In short: if the spiritual stages *a* and *c* are simultaneously reflected and carried by the physical stages *A* and *C*, then we will have to assume that the physical intermediate stage *B*, which reflects a spiritual *b*, also carries it.

In this turn of the argument, a God of the world is already presupposed—and how many reasons do we have indeed to already presuppose such a Being!—the **Spirit of the Earth** enters here merely to mediate the immense void that would otherwise remain between the human spirit and the divine spirit. If one wishes, one can position him as high above the Earth as God appears above the world—

2) The various sensory organs in humans—eye, ear, nose, tongue, hand—stand individually in relation to one another, both in terms of their physical structure and their mode of sensation; the various humans, animals, and plants stand individually in relation to one another, both in terms of their physical structure and their mode of spiritual life (which includes the sensory); the various world-bodies <term>world-bodies: "Weltkörper," a 19th-century term for planets or celestial objects.</term> stand individually in relation to one another in terms of their physical structure, and since they physically encompass their humans, animals, and plants—just as these in turn encompass their eyes, ears, etc.—and since humans, animals, and plants encompass their sensory spheres <term>sensory spheres: "Sinnessphären," the subjective range of an individual's perception and experience.</term> not only physically but also spiritually within a higher and broader sphere, the same is to be presupposed of the celestial bodies regarding the spheres they enclose, which in turn encompass these sensory spheres.

<meta>This page continues the logical proof for a planetary consciousness. What is it that distinguishes humans and the higher animals from the creatures alongside them, yet lower in rank, in terms of their organization <term>organization: "Organisation," the structural arrangement and functional complexity of a living system.</term>? 

It is the greater number of diverse organs; the more advanced articulation and sub-articulation; the closed circulation <term>circulation: "Kreislauf," referring to the movement of fluids like blood, but also the broader cycles of life-sustaining processes.</term> with more twists, loops, and sub-loops; the more complex periodicity <term>periodicity: "Periodicität," the rhythmic patterns of life, such as sleep-wake cycles or digestive rhythms.</term> with various types of periods and sub-periods; and the more decisive centralization <term>centralization: "Centralisation," the way an organism is governed by a central core or system rather than being a collection of independent parts.</term>, not in a single point, but in a region of central importance (cf. p. 167).

In connection with the development and intensification of these physical signs, we simultaneously see the spiritual development and height increase. 

The Earth, however, incomparably surpasses all earthly creatures in all these signs, insofar as these creatures themselves contribute to the multitude of diverse organs and graduated articulation, just as they contribute—through their circulations and periods—to the entanglements and complexities of its life. Furthermore, the Earth’s surface <note>original: "Oberraum," literally the 'upper space' or the outer realm of the planet where life exists.</note>, as the theater of all organic life and human activity, represents a far more concentrated and, in a higher sense, significant hearth of a grander and freer activity than the brain does for a human being.

In short: if we have two ascending physical ladders <term>ladders: "Stufenleitern," often translated as "scales of being," referring to a hierarchical progression from simple to complex.</term> **A, B, C** (plant, animal, human) and **A', B', C'** (eye, human, Earth), and if the first not only reflects but also carries a spiritual ladder **a, b, c**, while the second corresponds with the first in the manner of reflecting spiritual height, then we may conclude that it also corresponds in the manner of carrying it, thus a

As an argument for this interconnectedness <term>interconnectedness: "Zusammenhang," the way parts are woven together into a functional whole.</term>, one can assert that the means by which people are linked into humanity and by which spiritual relationships are established between them—such as sound, light, roads, means of transport, and so on—are all physical in nature. These would actually serve to create a divide between spirits <term>spirits: "Geister," used here to mean individual minds or centers of consciousness.</term> rather than forge a bond, if they could not be understood as components of a being that is ensouled as a whole.

Thus, even if we cannot directly observe the higher unity above our own spirits, we can nonetheless observe a connection that presupposes this unity.

Furthermore, all the experiential relationships that have prompted philosophers to speak of a "spirit of humanity"—sparing us the need to speak of them again—can be applied even more forcefully to a **spirit of the Earth**. This spirit links the minds of all humans with those of all other earthly creatures into a single unity. Humans do not exist in isolation, but only through the interconnectedness of the Earth, which encompasses all other creatures simultaneously. Beneath the level of human spirits within this higher Spirit, there is a need for even lower levels, which we can find in the souls of animals and plants; for our own spirit is not merely an unconscious sum of uniform, conscious mo— <note>original: "Mo-"; likely "Monaden" (monads), referring to the philosophical concept of fundamental, individual units of consciousness.</note>

   From yet another point of view, it can be asserted that there would be no connection between the soul-realms of the various stars if the stars did not have souls above the individual souls of their creatures, to whom we might attribute such communication. Now, while the requirement for such a higher connection is not a proof of its existence, the fulfillment of this requirement makes the result of the proofs themselves more satisfying.
   The argument of *Causality* rests on the fact that, so far as experience extends, the ensouled <note>Ensoutled: "Beseeltes," referring to anything possessing a soul or consciousness.</note> cannot arise from the unsouled; yet we, as ensouled beings along with all ensouled animals, must view ourselves as products of the earthly system. One can then pose the dual possibility that the Earth first woke up with the emergence of organic creatures, while previously lying in embryonic slumber, or that the emergence of organic creatures itself already falls within the internal *productions* of its waking life. It is not necessary to decide on this point if the only concern is whether the Earth is currently in a state of waking ensoulment. (Detailed explanation of this argument in *Zend-Avesta* Vol. I, pp. 226 ff.)

on a grander scale, purposeful considerations for the preservation and further development of the Earth and the entire earthly household have been taken, just as harmoniously as those made for the preservation and development of our own bodies and their internal households in relation to the existence of our souls. To these theoretical arguments for the belief in the spirits of the stars, one could further add—following the connection of the beautiful and the good with the true—the viewpoints of the beauty and wholesomeness of this belief.

In what a different light the whole Earth now appears to us, awakened to true vitality! In what a different light the heavens appear, filled with hosts of angels <note>The author interprets planets and stars as "angels" or higher spiritual beings, rather than just physical rocks in space.</note> instead of a dead game of spheres! How much more lofty and rich is God now, who carries within Himself level upon level of spirits! How different is our relationship to God and our neighbors, since we find in the spirit above us—the Angel of the Earth—a common mediator to God and a spiritual bond between us. Would we not gladly believe everything as it appears according to this faith? Let us now cast a look back at our **starting point** and our progress until now.

The human body reflects the most essential properties of a soul;—it would be unnecessary to repeat once more what has already been repeated in this regard.

The animal body reflects them in the same way.

The plant body reflects them in the same way.

The Earth reflects them in the same way.

All celestial bodies reflect them in the same way.

Otherwise, we know of no bodies in the world that reflect the most essential properties of a soul in this same way.

The human body simultaneously carries the soul that it reflects.

From this, we believe that the animal body no less carries the soul it reflects.

Following this, we believe it in a consistent sequence and progression for the entire class of <term>soul-reflecting</term> <note>original: "seelenspiegelnd." This is a central concept for Fechner: the idea that physical organization "mirrors" or acts as a container for spiritual consciousness.</note> bodies.

Within the scope of this conclusion, it is naturally implied that the animal has a lower soul and the plant a still lower soul alongside the human one; that the Earth has a higher soul above the human one; and finally, that the soul of the Earth holds those of humans, animals, and plants not merely beneath itself, but also **within** itself.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of the contemporary state of Christianity. He argues that the religion has strayed from its core purpose and must be purged of elements that contradict reason, science, and human progress to achieve its destined universal unity.</meta>

...but count everything of which nothing is to be found in Christ's own teaching, everything which does not make humanity better, happier, or wiser, which contradicts itself and the nature of things and of man, which darkens the spirit, disturbs science, and mixes a gloomy essence into life—in short, everything that resists the possibility of an eventual unification of all, instead of promoting it, and thereby resists the basic idea of Christianity—count all that among what must fall, so that the teaching of Christ may stand.

Christianity is already faltering in its spread across the earth; it gains new territory more by killing peoples than by converting them. How many Jews, Muslims, Hindus, or heathens are there who are counted as converts each year? And how divided is Christianity within itself! Instead of Christians who are united in Christ's cause, there are only Protestants, Catholics, Greeks <note>Refers to the Eastern Orthodox Church.</note>, and sectarians, who quarrel in Christ’s name. Is there any prospect, here or there, that things will change? Anyone who is not blinded says no. Christianity has advanced as far as it has through what is truly "of Christ" within it; but it cannot permeate the world so long as it is not entirely permeated by that same spirit, which requires the removal of much that fills it now. Until Christianity converts itself—converts back to what it was destined to be according to its core idea—until it takes the most universal, highest, and final account of the needs of humanity, it will not be able to convert the world nor become united within itself.

The very fact that it is unable to do this today, and—if it continues as it exists—will predictably never be able to do so, proves that it does not yet account for the needs of humanity. (Proof from the <term>historical principle</term> <note>The "historical principle" refers to evaluating the truth or validity of an institution based on its practical success and development throughout history.</note>.) Not in the abstract idea, but rather in the idea as it has been realized in faith and life and continues to realize itself.

The interpretation of the most weighty words of Christian doctrine itself—which are currently believed only according to their literal wording—according to their truth; the explanation of the <term>highest good</term> <note>The "highest good" or *summum bonum* refers to the ultimate end or purpose of human life and morality.</note> and the unfolding of its content brought into connection with this; and the tracing back of Christianity to Christ, to the Holy Spirit—or shall we say, the <term>spirit of salvation</term> <note>original: "Geist des Heils." And if, along with this internal purification, development, and deepening, the external form of Christianity also entirely changes compared to today...

Instead of deviating from the principle of <term>empirical inference</term> <note>original: "Erfahrungsschluss"; the logical process of drawing conclusions from observation and experience.</note>, this is merely the extension and escalation of this principle beyond the realm of **knowledge** into that of **faith**. Whoever scorns this extension and escalation scorns either faith itself or the union of knowledge with faith.

## X.
### Fundamental View.

> ->“But down there it is terrible,<-
> ->And let man not tempt the gods”<-
> <note>original: "„Da unten aber ist's fürchterlich, / Und der Mensch versuche die Götter nicht“"; these lines are from Friedrich Schiller’s famous 1797 ballad *Der Taucher* (The Diver), where a page boy leaps into a whirlpool to retrieve a golden goblet.</note>

one feels tempted to cry out to anyone who wants to immerse themselves in the depths of a <term>fundamental view</term> <note>original: "Grundansicht"; a foundational philosophical theory regarding the ultimate nature of reality.</note> of things in order to grasp their ultimate essence. One plunges out of life into the dark abyss and does not emerge back into life again. And for that reason, I have considered it best to plunge into the depths after the <term>cup</term> <note>A reference back to Schiller's poem; Fechner suggests he has lived through his ideas before attempting to find their metaphysical bottom.</note> only after I have completely emptied it.

For a fundamental view of things can indeed be inferred, but nothing can be inferred from it.

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s transition into his "Fundamental View" (Grundansicht). Following the previous page's poetic warning from Schiller about "diving" into the depths of ultimate reality, Fechner now begins to define the starting point of his philosophy: the undeniable fact of consciousness.</meta>

Indeed, this statement, like most made in haste, is only half true; however, it is all too easy to make it entirely true. Rather than explaining exactly how far it is true and how far it is not, I will provide a point of reference for judging it. Every <term>fundamental philosophical view</term> <note>original: "philosophische Grundansicht"; a foundational theory regarding the ultimate nature of reality.</note> provides such a point of reference.

One can derive everything from nothing, and then everything one derives is nothing. One can derive everything from the simple, but the unity of the whole remains unproven. One can derive everything from <term>matter</term>; one must only forget that matter is first derived from the sensations of the <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "Geist"; in German, this term refers to both the "mind" and the "spirit" or "intellect."</note>. One can derive everything from the spirit, one must only not forget to begin with a Spirit of the All <note>Fechner refers here to a universal or divine consciousness.</note>, and only then rise from one's own spirit toward it.

---

According to experience, we possess of that which exists only that which falls within our <term>consciousness</term> <note>original: "Bewußtsein"</note>—only our perceiving, feeling, thinking, and willing.

Consciousness is a <term>fact</term> <note>original: "Thatsache"</note>, its unity is a fact, its content is a fact, the relationship between its unity and its content is a fact; all of this can be demonstrated within us, and as it can be demonstrated, so it is; outside of consciousness, behind consciousness, nothing can be demonstrated.

To take consciousness in its unity and with its content exactly as it is constitutes the purest, most immediate <term>experience</term> <note>original: "Erfahrung"</note> we can have of that which exists. Consciousness is a being that knows what it is, and is exactly as it knows itself to be. Beyond this, however, there is no being of which the same can be said. Here

<summary>Fechner establishes consciousness as the only immediate and undeniable fact of existence, arguing that matter and spirit must be understood through the lens of our own internal experience rather than as abstract derivations.</summary>
<keywords>consciousness, spirit, matter, unity, empirical experience, fundamental view, Gustav Theodor Fechner, existence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s exploration of consciousness as the foundation of reality. The previous page ended mid-sentence, arguing that nothing can be demonstrated outside of consciousness; this page completes that thought by asserting the identity of being and knowing within experience.</meta>

...an empirical identity of <term>Being</term> <note>original: "Sein"</note> and <term>Knowing</term> <note>original: "Wissen"</note> takes place.

According to direct experience, there is absolutely nothing except consciousness, the unity of consciousness, the content of consciousness, and abstractions drawn from that which belongs to or is within consciousness—obtained through consciousness, which in turn fall back into consciousness. As far as direct experience reaches, it is impossible to escape the circle of consciousness. Experience itself is a matter of consciousness. To insist on experience without insisting on this fundamental experience is like knocking on a sheet of metal suspended in mid-air <note>The German phrase "auf ein Blech in der Luft pochen" suggests making a hollow, groundless claim or a futile gesture; essentially, "knocking on thin air."</note>.

Yet man has reasons to believe in something lying beyond that part of existence which falls into his consciousness—to believe in an <term>external world</term> <note>original: "Außenwelt"</note>—and to assume that, in addition to the internal, subjective reasons that have shaped the content of his consciousness, there are also external objective reasons that have contributed to it. No one can stop at the idea that only their own consciousness of what exists is what exists.

<margin>{ For consciousness, in becoming aware of its content, also becomes aware through immediate feeling that not all determinations of this content arise from the pre-existing content; the practical point of view compels man to believe in an external world in order to direct his actions toward it; to this is added the historical consensus of all ages and peoples.</margin>

Therefore, despite the fact that we possess of the whole of existence only that which falls into our consciousness, the question is not whether we must assume the existence of something beyond our consciousness; rather, it is *what* it is that we must assume beyond it.

**Identity, Being, Knowing, Experience, Consciousness, Unity of consciousness, Content of consciousness, Existence, External world, Point of view**

<summary>Fechner argues that while we are "locked" within the circle of our own consciousness, human intuition and practical necessity compel us to believe in an external world. He defines the central philosophical problem not as whether an external world exists, but as what the nature of that world is.</summary>
<keywords>consciousness, epistemology, external world, unity of consciousness, empirical identity, German idealism, subjectivity vs objectivity</keywords>
