<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 200. While the previous page established that we cannot logically step outside the "circle of consciousness," this page introduces a choice between two fundamental ways of interpreting what lies beyond our individual experience.</meta>

There are two possibilities, and fundamentally considered, only two possibilities open: two paths of thought, one of which leads into <note>Hades, the Greek underworld, used here to symbolize a realm of shadows and unknowable darkness.</note> Hades, the other into the light. One should probably make it clear to oneself that only these two paths exist, and, instead of wavering unclearly between them or walking half of each, decide between them and follow the path consistently to its final consequences, after having cast a clear eye upon each.

The first thought is this: that besides what we carry of things within our consciousness—besides our sensation, feeling, poetry, thinking, and willing—there exists not only outside of our own consciousness, but outside of consciousness in general, a dark, unknowable <term>Thing-in-itself</term> <note>original: "Ding an sich"; a core concept in Kantian philosophy referring to an object as it exists independently of human observation.</note> or a multiplicity of such things. These things either generate within consciousness that which it possesses of things, or, through their <term>interaction</term> <note>original: "Wechselwirkung"; the reciprocal action or mutual influence between entities.</note>, they generate consciousness itself; yet they remain something entirely different from everything consciousness possesses of them.

Mostly, this dark thing—or these dark things—is imagined as something solid behind changeability, as something simple behind the <term>manifold</term> <note>original: "Mannichfaltigkeit"; the complex variety or "many-ness" of sensory data.</note> of appearances, and is itself darkly conceived. The soul itself is said to be an unchangeable, simple being in its own right, which stays hidden behind the changing appearances of consciousness, never recognizing itself according to its own essence. Rather, even the brightest consciousness is merely the brightest glow upon an eternally enigmatic <term>soul-essence</term> <note>original: "Seelenwesen"</note> that is dark to itself.

That is the one thought.

The other thought is this: that besides our consciousness there is yet other consciousness; that above all <term>individual consciousness</term> <note>original: "Einzelbewußtsein"</note> there is a further and higher consciousness and a further and higher content of consciousness; a con—

<summary>Fechner presents a fundamental choice between two worldviews: one where reality is an unknowable "thing-in-itself" hidden behind appearances, and another where reality consists of a hierarchy of interconnected consciousness.</summary>
<keywords>consciousness, thing-in-itself, soul-essence, manifold, individual consciousness, Hades, German idealism, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical comparison between two worldviews: the "dark" path of the unknowable "thing-in-itself" and the "bright" path of an all-encompassing consciousness. The previous page introduced the first path as leading into a realm of shadows; here, the author explores the second path, which leads toward a higher unity and divinity.</meta>

...consciousness, which, in those aspects where it exceeds and reaches beyond our individual consciousness, represents the external world that determines our consciousness. It links all individual consciousness through commonalities and relationships of effect, of which the highest unity is the final junction.

The first thought leads away from all experience—and everything that is imaginable or verifiable through experience—into the darkness. This is because we have, in truth, neither an experience, nor a concept based on experience, nor even the prospect of clarification through experience regarding the <term>thing-in-itself</term> <note>original: "Dinge an sich"; a reference to Immanuel Kant’s idea of objects existing independently of our perception.</note> (or things-in-themselves) hidden behind consciousness. We cannot know its effect on consciousness, nor its power to generate consciousness. 

The second thought leads from the light of common experience only into a higher light, insofar as our own naturally clear consciousness—with its inherent qualities, relationships, and laws—provides us with a basis for thinking about other, more general, broader, higher, and brighter consciousnesses, and the means to draw conclusions about them.

In any case, the world believes in God above all individual consciousness and has historical, theoretical, and practical reasons for this belief. However, every <term>dark thing</term> <note>A shorthand for the "thing-in-itself" mentioned previously.</note> becomes <term>superfluous</term> <note>original: "müßig"; meaning idle, redundant, or unnecessary in a logical sense.</note> if one truly believes in God. Indeed, true faith in God is precisely that which makes the dark thing redundant. It does so by viewing what one might consider an effect of dark things "behind" us and God as instead an effect of the God "above" us reaching down into us. It finds transparent in God what would remain opaque in the dark thing behind Him. Conversely, true faith in the dark thing—or the dark things behind consciousness—makes God redundant or turns God Himself into a dark thing; for the dark thing is then...

<meta>This page continues the critique of the "night-view" of philosophy, which relies on unknowable "things-in-themselves." The author has just argued that the world's belief in God provides a brighter alternative to these "dark things," which he considers redundant if one accepts an all-encompassing divine consciousness.</meta>

...the ground and creator of that which appears. However, if one wants to combine a divine consciousness existing above phenomena with the dark things hidden behind phenomena, one ends up with two halves and no whole.

It is strange that modern <term>Idealism</term> <note>Idealism is a philosophical school of thought that emphasizes the mind, spirit, or ideas as the fundamental foundation of reality, rather than physical matter.</note> is surrendering exactly what its greatest advantage was—the lack of a need for "dark things" behind appearances—and instead now seeks all "depth" within those very dark things.

The Concept before thinking, the Nothing, Being, the Absolute, the Essence—which are supposedly hidden behind phenomena as their foundation, only to eventually elevate themselves into nature and conscious spirit—what are these but dark things? The <term>Monads</term> <note>original: "Monaden"; a term famously used by the philosopher Leibniz to describe the fundamental, indivisible "souls" or units that make up the universe.</note>, which others still seek behind the units of consciousness, what are they but dark things? But even the matter of the <term>Materialists</term> <note>Materialism is the belief that nothing exists except matter and its movements; here, the author argues that "matter" is just as much of an abstraction as the "absolute."</note> behind the appearance of things, what else is it but a dark thing? None of these dark things have made the inherent clarity of consciousness any clearer or explained it to us; instead, each one, as dark as it is itself, has only served to obscure it.

Therefore, I cast these dark things aside and, without listing every intermediate step of the argument, put forward the following propositions and viewpoints:

There exists a most general and highest unity of consciousness: the divine. There are units of consciousness subordinate to it and, in their shared subordination, coordinated with one another; among these, human consciousnesses belong. There also exist units of consciousness that are superior to us, yet still subordinate to God.

The unity of the highest consciousness transcends all lower units of consciousness and establishes a connection, a bond between them, of which it is itself aware,

<meta>This page continues the author’s rejection of "dark things" or "things-in-themselves" that supposedly exist outside of consciousness. He argues that everything that exists is part of a nested hierarchy of consciousness, ultimately held together by a supreme, divine mind.</meta>

...since being contained within consciousness serves as the very bond for what is contained. In contrast, lower units of consciousness are not directly aware of being included within the higher or supreme consciousness; instead, each unit is only aware of what it personally comprehends.

Before, outside, above, behind, or after the most universal and supreme consciousness—and the contents it holds, which include our own consciousness—nothing can be found. One may speak of things that fall neither within our own consciousness, nor another's, nor a higher or supreme one; but such talk would be nothing but empty words. Behind every present and individual consciousness, there exists only an infinite possibility for future consciousness and the reality of other consciousnesses. Yet even this possibility does not exist outside of consciousness itself. It is merely an <term>abstraction</term> <note>An abstraction is a general concept formed by pulling out specific examples and pulling out common traits, like the idea of "redness" apart from a red apple.</note> derived from the facts of consciousness; it holds meaning only for consciousness and would be nothing apart from it. All the "darkness" that people project onto "the thing" behind consciousness is really just the darkness within consciousness itself—the lack of awareness regarding things that are not part of its current focus, but belong to a future or different consciousness.

An "external world" exists only for finite consciousness, not for the infinite consciousness. This outer world is not something foreign to consciousness; it is simply the broader and higher extension of what every individual consciousness already possesses within itself. In turn, individual consciousness contributes to the richness and the various levels of this broader and higher reality.

Experience shows that within consciousness there is a constant flow, a shifting, and an eternal change of everything that appears there. However, the world does not dissolve into a chaotic, unstable tangle and bustle of <term>appearances</term> <note>In philosophy, "appearances" or "phenomena" are things as we perceive them through our senses, rather than how they might exist independently.</note>, because the supreme, unchanging unity of consciousness [acts as] the bond...

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the "laws" of existence. That which is conceptual in nature, which can be consciously abstracted from the content of consciousness, proves by the very fact that it can be abstracted that it is a matter of consciousness itself.

How will this person behave tomorrow? Ask how he behaves today, yesterday, and the day before, and how he has behaved since his birth. See if you can discover a law in the actions of man. Whatever you can guess about a person, you will guess according to his law, or you will guess nothing at all. <note>The author suggests that human behavior isn't random but follows patterns or "laws" of character that become visible over time.</note>

But of course, no law will suffice to guess everything about a person, and even less to guess everything about God. "Law" is a great word; but "freedom" is also a great word. And while law governs everything, it leaves an indeterminate residue as freedom—a freedom that does not run contrary to the laws, but rather goes beyond all laws. It exceeds the laws in the same sense as the infinite exceeds all that is finite.

Whoever knows the laws of the connection and the course of appearances knows everything that the wisest person can know about the reasons for what happens; only, all the laws to which he can attain knowledge will not exhaust the reasons for what happens, and besides reasons, one must also consider purposes. <note>Here, the author distinguishes between "causes" (how things happen) and "purposes" or "teleology" (why things happen or what they are meant to achieve).</note>

Whoever has an answer to that, let him speak. But whether he has an answer or not, so

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of the nature of "essence" (Wesen) and its relationship to consciousness. the laws of things remain a fact, unaffected by the uncertainty regarding the essence <note>The German word used here is "Wesen," which can mean both the "essence" or "nature" of a thing and a "being" or "entity" itself.</note> of this fact.

Just as there is nothing solid in the world except for that which exists unchangeably within the content of consciousness, there is no essence of things except for that which unchangeably conditions itself within it.

Whatever is connected unchangeably or according to law in such a way that one can only have the one in proportion to having or being able to have the other, is a matter of the same essence; and this connection is the firm core, the essential part of the essence.

In this sense, we rightly call God—the highest consciousness—the highest being; because within the unity of His consciousness, everything that exists in terms of the appearances of things (and thus also of things themselves, which are only connections of appearances) is linked through mutual conditioning.

However, since God is the highest being over all things, we no longer require any obscure "beings" <note>The author is likely critiquing the philosophical idea of "things-in-themselves"—hidden realities that exist behind what we can actually perceive.</note> behind the appearances of things.

Regarding what we call soul or spirit, nothing can be empirically demonstrated and nothing exists except for a connection and a flowing-apart of appearances <note>In this context, "appearances" refers to the phenomena or mental experiences we perceive.</note> linked by a unity of consciousness. I call these "self-appearances," insofar as each unity of consciousness possesses them only for itself in relation to others. Yet, while it possesses them only for itself relative to others, it contributes these—as a subordinate part of the highest unity of consciousness—to the fullness of that highest unity’s own self-appearances.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that "essence" is not a hidden substance, but rather the lawful connection of appearances. Having previously defined the soul as a unity of consciousness, the author now challenges the need for metaphysical constructs like "Monads" to explain the mind or the physical body.</meta>

The connection and the succession of soul-appearances or self-appearances are governed by psychological laws. Of that which we call the **body**, nothing further can be empirically demonstrated, and nothing exists, but a connection and a flowing-apart of appearances. This connection exists collectively for various souls or units of consciousness simultaneously (insofar as different people can see or feel the same body at once), by reaching between them in such a way that the reality and possibility of given appearances which a body offers to one soul simultaneously establishes related realities and possibilities for other souls. This is how the **objective, external existence of the body** is characterized for us. The constant quantity of matter abstracted from this connection of appearances is merely the expression of a constant possibility for the return of external appearances of a given quantitative definition. The connection and flow of these so-called external appearances are governed by the laws of nature. What help can "obscure things" <note>The author uses "obscure things" (dunkle Dinge) to refer to "things-in-themselves"—theoretical entities that philosophers claim exist behind what we can actually perceive through our senses.</note> provide in this regard?

In general, nothing more of the objective existence of the entire corporeal world can be empirically demonstrated, and nothing can be inferred, other than that collective connection of appearances—governed by empirical laws—which exists for more than one unit of consciousness at once; thus, based on experience, we have no reason to regard anything further as existing within the corporeal world.

<meta>This page continues the argument against materialist reductions of consciousness. The previous page discussed how matter is a "thought-based fixed point" for phenomena; this page moves toward a "double-aspect" theory where body and soul are two sides of the same essential reality.</meta>

Insofar as one can grasp the connection of the spiritual or **self-phenomena** on its own, just as one does for the physical external phenomena, and both connections have their particular **point of view** and their particular **laws**, one can also speak separately of an **essence of the spirit** and an **essence of the body**. However, insofar as both connections are inextricably linked according to a relationship of **mutual conditionality** and **requirement**, one can also ascribe to them a **common essence**, and call the **body** the **outward-appearing side**, and the **soul** the **self-appearing side** of this essence.

With this, one does not step outside the circle of **experience** or the permitted **freedom of definitions**.

The **identically shared essence** of the **body** and the **soul** is precisely nothing other than the **inextricable mutual conditionality** <note>original: "solidarische Wechselbedingtheit"; this suggests that the soul and body are so interdependent that one cannot exist or be defined without the other.</note> of the **self-phenomena** of the **soul** and

<meta>This page concludes the argument that body and soul are two sides of one essence. It introduces the term "Psychophysics" to describe the scientific study of their relationship and posits that every mental event has a physical correlate that interacts with the wider world, linking individual experience to a universal consciousness.</meta>

...of the external appearances of the body. One cannot have the one without the other; one cannot show or find something hidden behind them.

Accordingly, the ultimate ground for the connection between **body** and **soul** is none other than the ultimate ground for the connection that body and soul each possess for themselves. Each, on its own, represents a context of appearance that is possible and real only within a highest, universal **unity of consciousness**; the same applies to the connection between these two contexts.

Little has been done yet regarding the knowledge of the laws by which body and soul are connected; seeking them out will be more useful than the dispute between **idealism** and **materialism**. I call the study of this **psychophysics** <note>The author, likely Gustav Fechner, coined this term to describe the scientific study of the relationship between stimulus (physical) and sensation (mental).</note>.

The most general law is this: nothing can exist, arise, or pass away in the mind without something simultaneously existing, arising, or passing away in the body, which extends its effects and consequences into the surrounding environment and the future of the physical world. It can be expressed briefly: everything spiritual has its carrier or expression in something physical and, through this, has its further effects and consequences within the physical realm.

For although every unity of consciousness subordinate to the highest one has its own context of appearance which it shares with no other, every context of <term>self-phenomena</term> <note>original: "Selbsterscheinungen"; the internal, subjective experiences of an individual.</note> nevertheless stands in such a relationship with the general context of appearance that not even the smallest thing can happen in a soul without intervening in the general context of appearance, which exists for all souls and between all souls within and through the **highest soul** <note>In this philosophical framework, the "highest soul" refers to a universal or divine consciousness that encompasses all individual experiences.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of "psychophysics," a term coined by the author to describe the scientific relationship between the physical and the mental. Building on the previous page's assertion that every mental event has a physical carrier, the text now addresses how we can study the "internal" physical processes of the soul that are hidden from direct view.</meta>

The immediate intervention of the soul into the physical world—and thus its immediate physical expression—cannot be observed directly, as the interior of every body is hidden by its exterior. However, we can observe the **context of appearances** <note>original: "Erscheinungszusammenhang"; the interconnected web of physical phenomena we perceive through our senses.</note> that depends indirectly upon it. By applying the general laws of the physical world of appearances through mental representation and logical deduction, we can complete the picture of what would be observed if we could see inside the body, removing all obstacles.

Therefore, from the outwardly perceivable, indirect expression of the **mind** in the physical world (the subject of **external psychophysics**), we draw conclusions in **internal psychophysics** <note>While external psychophysics measures how external stimuli (like light or sound) affect the mind, internal psychophysics attempts to map the relationship between the mind and the internal nervous system.</note> about the immediate expression hidden within the body. This expression must still be conceived in the form of something outwardly perceivable to preserve the laws of how the physical follows from the physical, and to supplement the incompleteness of the observable context of appearances through the context of our mental representations.

Another fundamental law is that the **mental** possesses a character of relative unity or simplicity compared to the **physical**, which is viewed as its expression. This is because not only is every **unity of consciousness** <note>original: "Bewußtseinseinheit"; the idea that our various thoughts and sensations are experienced as a single, coherent "I."</note> essentially bound to a whole context and flow of physical events, but even the simplest **sensation**—as a single determination of consciousness—is directly tied to such a context. Conversely, the physical arrangement and sequence linked to a unity of consciousness cannot exist without it. (Compare Chapter 37 of the *Elements of Psychophysics*).

This can be represented by the expression that the **mind** is the **connecting principle** of physical arrangement and sequence. However, this is intended to denote nothing more than that factual relationship established by law.

<summary>The author explains that while we cannot see the soul's direct effect on the body's interior, we can use the laws of "internal psychophysics" to logically deduce these connections. He posits the mind as a unifying principle that gives coherence to the complex, flowing arrangements of the physical body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into how we understand things we cannot directly see. The concept of **reality** <term>Wirklichkeit; from the root "to work" or "to act"</term> or **actuality** <term>Realität</term> is broader or narrower depending on linguistic usage and definition. The simplest, most fleeting, and most futile **appearance** <term>Erscheinung; that which manifests to our senses</term>—even a deceptive subjective illusion itself—has its own reality. However, there is a reality of a higher kind than anything that manifests as a single appearance. This is that which holds individual appearances together, or that which must be conceived as the starting point, the anchor, or the conclusion of an idea or an inference. We use these concepts to link appearances in **thought** <term>Denken</term> and to deduce the unknown from the known, even if the connection itself never appears on its own; indeed, it may only be graspable as an **abstraction** <term>Abstractum</term>.

For appearances do not take place at all in isolation from a conscious and thinking being, but precisely only within such a being. Therefore, whatever can be abstracted from the connection of appearances in thought—or whatever is necessary for that connection in thought—is also to be regarded as something that truly connects and is thus "real" in a higher sense. 

Such an **abstraction** <term>Abstraction</term> is already familiar to common, everyday thinking. Such a necessity already binds common **consciousness** <term>Bewußtsein</term> regarding the most immediate circle of appearances and leads to the universally common assumption of "things" <note>The author is arguing that we never actually "see" an object like a chair in its entirety; we see colors and shapes and our mind "abstracts" the reality of the chair to make sense of the data.</note>. A related necessity binds "higher" or scientific thought regarding the entire circle of appearances, leading to a view of the highest and final things: the most general **principles** <term>Principien</term> and the ultimate elements of the world.

Realities of the first kind—those which already exceed the reality resting in immediate experience, yet are still quite familiar to common consciousness—are both the **soul** <term>Seele</term> and the **body** <term>Körper</term>. Instead of a whole soul or a whole body, there appear to us only individual be- <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with "beings" or "beings' activities" on the next page.</note>

<summary>The author defines reality not just as what we see (appearances), but as the necessary mental connections (abstractions) like "soul," "body," and "laws" that allow us to make sense of those appearances. He argues that these abstract concepts are "real" because they are the glue that holds our conscious experience together.</summary>

In the unity of divine consciousness, all appearances finally merge and conclude, and according to the character of infinity it bears, an **unpredictability** <term>Unberechenbarkeit</term> of events—which we conceive as **freedom**—is grounded therein; whereas the simple atom, though as such incapable of appearing <note>meaning an individual atom is too small or fundamental to be "seen" as an object</note>, is abstracted from the totality of physical appearances as the **concluding point** <term>Schlußpunct</term> of their connection, and represents the ultimate starting point for all exact calculation of what is calculable in the individual details of the world.

We arrive at the concept of the **first unity** by taking a unified connection—the kind we find within our own finite minds for specific areas of experience—and generalizing, expanding, and intensifying it to encompass the entire field of phenomena. The proof of the **reality of atoms** lies in the mathematical necessity of using them. Today’s **idealists** <note>In this context, "idealists" refers to philosophers who believe that reality is fundamentally mental or spiritual and often rejected the "hard" materiality of atoms</note> want nothing to do with atoms because they do not know how to incorporate them into the spirit. They view atoms either as something external to the spirit or as something that interrupts its flow. In truth, however, the spirit—through the contemplation and analysis of its own internal experience—recognizes atoms as fixed but necessary ultimate points of departure and stability. They provide the coherence for that sphere of appearance in which individual spirits must encounter one another.

This is the pure **fact** <term>Factum; a demonstrated reality</term>, and the pure understanding of this fact is the **idealist truth of atoms**. This truth does not contradict physical science, even though physics—by its very nature—cannot present the matter in this way. Rather, it is idealism that brings this truth from the "mine-shaft" of physics out into the light of day in this form—

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 220, completing the definition of a specific "idealistic" worldview. The text then moves to reconcile this view with the definitions of materialism, dualism, and identity theory, showing how one philosophical system can encompass elements of all three.</meta>

-ine consciousness rests, only that it does not hold matter or nature—in the sense of ordinary idealism—to be a later product of the spirit, or one-sidedly dependent upon it, but rather considers nature to be an immanent <term>immanent; inherent or existing within, rather than coming from an outside source</term> condition of its existence. The connection of phenomena <note>original: "Erscheinungen"</note> which represents nature to us belongs itself to the essential structure and to the foundation of the most universal, highest Spirit.

This view is entirely *materialistic*, in that it permits the possibility of no human thought without a brain and a movement within this brain; and in this, it is even "super-materialistic," as it permits no divine thought without a physical world and without movements in this world—except that it does, after all, permit a God and divine thoughts.

This view is entirely *dualistic*, in that body and soul are, according to it, two sides of existence that cannot be reduced to one another at all; they are fundamentally different and yet related to each other. However, their relationship, instead of being merely external, is mediated through the unity of the divine consciousness, in which ultimately everything physical and spiritual appears and, through this, exists.

This view is entirely an *identity view* <term>Identitätsansicht; a philosophical theory, famously associated with Baruch Spinoza and later Gustav Fechner, suggesting that the "mental" and the "physical" are just two different ways of looking at the same single reality</term>, in that it considers both—body and soul—to be merely two different ways for the same being to appear: one to be gained from an internal standpoint, the other from an external standpoint. The only difference is that it sees the essence which underlies both modes of appearance as nothing other than the inseparable, reciprocal conditionality of both modes, and sees the ultimate condition of this inseparability in the unity of divine consciousness.

<summary>The author explains a philosophical "Identity View" that reconciles idealism, materialism, and dualism by arguing that the physical and spiritual are two inseparable aspects of a single divine consciousness.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the argument from the previous section, where the author established how his "identity view" bridges materialism, dualism, and idealism. He now pivots to contrast this view with "monadology," defining the fundamental disagreement as one between a unified consciousness and a fragmented one.</meta>

It sharply contradicts only *monadology* <note>A philosophical system, notably from Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, suggesting the world is made of "monads"—indivisible, fundamental units of reality. Here, the author critiques later versions by Herbart and Lotze.</note>; with this, it is a *struggle of life and death*. The question is: must every individual consciousness merge into one, or must the one dissolve into the most minute parts? That is the ultimate choice.

The *anteater* either devours the ants or is devoured by them. The former is the *living*, the latter the *dead*. I bet on the *living*. <note>The author uses this metaphor to argue that a single, unified consciousness (the anteater) is a "living" totality, whereas a world split into infinite tiny "monads" (the ants) represents a kind of spiritual death or fragmentation.</note>

My polemic <note>original: "Polemik"; a strong verbal or written attack on someone's opinions or principles.</note> against *Herbart's monadology* is detailed in a treatise in *Fichte’s Journal 1853*, and against *Lotze’s* in the 37th chapter of my *Psychophysics*; these form the foundation for what is said here occasionally and briefly against the *monadological direction*.

However, in this our view contradicts all *current philosophy*: it rejects all regression behind experience and all *transcendence* <term>transcendence; knowledge or reality that lies beyond or above the limits of physical, empirical experience</term> above *experience*. In contrast, it *demands* the highest generalization, the most unified connection, the final analysis of experience, and the most far-reaching conclusion based on experience.

And what has been gained so far with that regression, that *transcendence*? It was a futile struggle to *get into* the circle of the empirical—from whose cultivation alone valuable fruits grow—or a jumping-in from behind or above, without a principle for moving forward.

<summary>The author (Gustav Fechner) rejects "monadology" in favor of a unified consciousness and argues that philosophy should be based strictly on empirical experience rather than metaphysical speculation beyond the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>monadology, Johann Friedrich Herbart, Hermann Lotze, empirical experience, transcendence, Psychophysics, unified consciousness, anteater metaphor</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the author's critique of "monadology" to the positive definition of his own worldview, often called the "Daylight View." He begins by defining the relationship between God and the universe as a totality rather than a collection of fragments.</meta>

## XI.
### Worldview.

God is the All or the Spirit of the All, depending on how one wishes to conceive of it.

The difference in this conception concerns only the word, not the essence of the matter. Just as one can reflect on the word "human" as the entire unified connection of body and spirit, or merely on the latter as the most essential part of this whole, so it is with the word God. (Compare *Zend-Avesta* <note>original: "Zend-Avesta"; Fechner’s 1851 work where he argues that the entire universe is conscious. The title is borrowed from Persian scripture but the content is his own philosophy.</note> Vol. I. p. 327 ff.)

To the physical **hierarchical structure** of the world corresponds a **spiritual hierarchical structure**, which is mirrored and supported by the physical.

What is meant by "mirroring" and "supporting" becomes clear from the discussions on page 48 ff. Related to this is the "functional principle" in the *Elements of Psychophysics* <term>Psychophysics; a field of study founded by Fechner investigating the mathematical relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensations</term> Part II, p. 380.

The hierarchical structure of the world is essentially of such a kind that every higher stage encompasses a plurality of coordinated members of a lower stage, both physically and spiritually; the consciousness of these lower members—which is collectively enclosed within the higher—is closed off from one another and supported by a more or less closed-off physical form. Yet the higher stage can be regarded, neither physically nor spiritually, as a mere sum of these individually conscious members; rather, it connects them through a physical and conscious content that reaches beyond them.

As a schema of the physical and, at the same time, as a symbol of the spiritual hierarchical structure (compare p. 64), one can use either the image of a large circle that encloses a plurality of smaller circles, which in turn enclose even smaller circles, or

<vocab>God, All, Spirit, worldview, conception, human, body, spirit, Zend-Avesta, hierarchical structure of the world, functional principle, Elements of Psychophysics, psychophysics, consciousness, physical form, schema, symbol, circles</vocab>

<summary>Fechner defines God as the totality of the universe and explains his "Daylight View," where the world is a hierarchy of consciousness. In this system, higher levels of existence (like God or the Earth) encompass the consciousness of lower levels (like humans) without being a mere sum of their parts.</summary>
<keywords>God, Panpsychism, Psychophysics, Consciousness, Hierarchical Structure, Gustav Theodor Fechner, Zend-Avesta, Mind-Body Relationship</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues a technical discussion regarding the hierarchical structures of the soul and nature, moving from a wave-based metaphor to a circular one. The author, likely Gustav Theodor Fechner, is explaining how individual elements (like nerves) are nested within larger systems (like organs, people, and planets).</meta>

...of a large **wave crest**, which carries a plurality of smaller wave crests, each of which in turn carries even smaller wave crests. According to the drawing, the latter is essentially the same scheme in <term>elevation<note>The "Aufriss" or vertical view.</note></term> as the first is in the <term>ground plan<note>The "Grundriss" or horizontal layout.</note></term>. In my <term>Psychophysics<note>Referring to *Elemente der Psychophysik* (1860), a foundational text by Gustav Theodor Fechner exploring the relationship between the physical and the mental.</note></term>, use was made of the wave scheme; the circle scheme is more popular.

If, according to this, a circle **A** encloses the <term>juxtaposed<note>Placed side-by-side.</note></term> circles **a, b, c....**, it is nevertheless not filled by them; therefore, it is not to be viewed as the mere sum of them, but rather provides all of them with a content that links them all. Now, the subdivision of the various side-by-side members **a, b, c....** can go to different lengths, and in some, it may even be absent; from this arises an analogous <term>hierarchy<note>A "Stufenreihe," or series of steps/levels.</note></term> of members that are both side-by-side and nested. Those in which the subdivision goes further than in others are to be considered as standing higher. (Compare p. 185 f.)

Starting from the human level and ascending by naming only one member of each internal level at a time, one can set up the internal ladder thus: **distinctly sensing nerve fiber, eye, human, Earth, mutually enclosing world systems, World.** Yet it may appear doubtful to what extent the world systems—which enclose the Earth and are themselves enclosed by the World—still reflect self-contained **spiritual individualities** above the Earth but below the divine unity; consequently, it is doubtful whether they still "carry" such individualities and should therefore be listed as distinct levels. This obscure question, which initially lacks pressing interest, has therefore been set aside in all the preceding text.

The sensory circle of the eye is not entirely filled by the circles of the distinctly sensing retinal fibers; rather, the entire retina is embedded in other membranes and contains, besides the nerves, cellular tissue and vessels. The circle of the human is not entirely filled by the sensory circles of the eyes, ears, and so forth; the circle of the Earth is not entirely filled by the circles of humans, animals, and plants; the world-circle is not entirely filled by those of the celestial bodies; for between them, finally, there is still the **ether**.

On the external hierarchy, the eye, ear, and organ of touch stand higher than the organs of smell and taste, because the former still contain a subdivision into distinctly sensing nerve fibers...

<summary>The author uses wave and circle metaphors to describe a hierarchical "ladder" of existence, ranging from individual nerve fibers to the entire universe (the World), while debating whether cosmic systems possess their own spiritual individuality.</summary>
<keywords>psychophysics, hierarchy, sensory organs, nerve fibers, spiritual individualities, ether, cosmology, retinal fibers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchical classification of living and celestial beings based on their internal complexity. The author, Gustav Theodor Fechner, is using a geometric scheme of nested circles to explain how higher-order consciousness (like that of the Earth or the Sun) emerges from the relationships between lower-order parts (like humans or plants).</meta>

<margin>*) That this also applies with great probability to the ear is shown in my *Psychophysics*, Vol. II, p. 286 ff. <note>original: "Psychophysik"</note></margin>
...contain,*) while the latter do not; humans and animals stand higher than plants because the former contain individually specialized sense organs, which in turn contain <term>discretely sensing nerves<note>Nerves that transmit distinct, individual sensations rather than a vague, undifferentiated feeling.</note></term>, whereas plants do not. Among the celestial bodies, there may also be those in which the internal subdivision goes further or less far than on Earth, and which accordingly stand higher or lower in the sense of the external <term>hierarchy<note>A "Stufenleiter," or a ladder of progression from simple to complex forms of life.</note></term>. Perhaps there are celestial bodies (asteroids?) which, although on the same internal level as the Earth, stand in a similar external relationship to it as the plant and the <term>polyp<note>A simple aquatic organism, such as a sea anemone, used here as an example of a primitive animal form.</note></term> stand to the human, while the Sun may stand higher on the external ladder than the Earth—if indeed the entire solar system, in the sense of the concept developed in <term>*Zend-Avesta*<note>Fechner’s 1851 work *Zend-Avesta: Or on the things of heaven and the hereafter*, which argued for the soul-life of all nature.</note></term> Vol. II, p. 241 ff., is not to be regarded as a single creature. However, this belongs to those obscure questions that one can set aside for now.

In a further development of the scheme, one could imagine that each of the circles **a, b, c....** generated wave-like circles around itself, which filled the entire circle **A** without a definite boundary (and even with reflection back from the perimeter), but because they originate from different points, they would only intersect rather than coincide. This is the schematic-symbolic concept which I take as the basis for the memories that develop from our perceptions, as well as for the <term>spheres of life in the beyond<note>"Jenseitige Lebenskreise"—referring to Fechner's theory that human existence continues after death as a spiritual ripple or memory within the mind of the Earth or God.</note></term>, which develop from our lives in this world. Just as the circles of memory only enter consciousness when the circles of perception have faded, so it is with the spheres of life in the beyond. For the full elaboration of this, see Part 3 of *Zend-Avesta*.

Between the members of a given level, there exist both material and spiritual relationships, which link together as a unity in the higher level and find their conscious conclusion on the spiritual side. In these relationships—not in the content of the lower levels alone—the most significant content of every higher level is to be found.

<summary>Fechner argues that humans and animals are "higher" than plants due to their specialized nervous systems and suggests that celestial bodies like the Sun may also be living, conscious entities. He introduces a wave-based metaphor to explain how memories and the afterlife function as overlapping "circles" within a larger universal consciousness.</summary>
<keywords>hierarchy of being, celestial bodies, solar system, Zend-Avesta, spheres of life, memory circles, perception, spiritual unity</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument that individual consciousness is nested within higher levels of existence. He previously discussed the "external ladder" of creatures; here, he explores how relationships between parts create higher-order mental states, eventually leading to the consciousness of the Earth and God.</meta>

Thus, for the sense of sight, it is not the sensations of the individual <term>retinal fibers<note>The fine nerve endings in the eye that respond to light.</note></term> that are most significant, but rather the relationships of form and color that arise from the connections between these sensations and find their fulfillment in the overall perception of the eye. Likewise for the human being, it is not the <term>spheres of sensation<note>The specific range of sensory experiences (sight, sound, touch) belonging to individual organs.</note></term> of the individual senses that matter most, but rather the concepts and ideas that arise from their relationships and find their fulfillment in the consciousness of the whole person. So too for the Earth: it is not the spheres of thought and sense of individual humans, animals, and plants that are paramount, but a higher realm of spiritual relationships—found in church, state, science, art, and life—which consciously exists within the unified spirit of the Earth and finds its conscious fulfillment there. And so, in turn, there will be relationships between the spirits of the celestial bodies, which, as the most significant part of the world, consciously exist in God and find their conscious fulfillment in Him.

The misunderstanding of this principle has led a more recent direction in philosophy toward the <term>deification of man<note>original: "Menschenvergötterung." Fechner likely refers to Hegelian or humanist philosophies that view human reason as the ultimate peak of the universe, rather than a part of a larger divine or planetary consciousness.</note></term>, according to which the pinnacle of creation is to be found in the consciousness of individual humans, or in the conscious ideas of individual philosophers regarding an unconscious connection within humanity; this is held instead of recognizing that, above the consciousness of individual humans, higher conscious relationships reach across into a universal consciousness.

The relationships between the individual members of a level can, for their part, differ in height, insofar as there are relationships between relationships in the spiritual realm and undeniably corresponding ones in the physical realm, by which the spiritual is mirrored and supported. To this is linked a third gradation of creatures. Thus man—even if standing at the same level as animals on the internal and external <term>hierarchy<note>original: "Stufenleiter," literally "step-ladder." A common 18th- and 19th-century concept of a "Great Chain of Being" from minerals to God.</note></term>—stands higher than the animal in this third respect, just as the animal stands above the plant; this is because in man, a higher realm of conscious relationships in concepts and ideas rises above the simple conscious relationships found in the soul of the animal. Yet the animal still stands higher than the plant, in which all determinations of consciousness merge directly into the <term>unity of the soul<note>The idea that a plant’s "experience" is a single, undifferentiated state rather than a complex web of distinct thoughts and sensations.</note></term>. (Cf. p. 80 ff.)

Not all levels of the internal ladder that are capable of consciousness are always conscious; rather, in the lower levels, a state of unconsciousness, of sleep, alternates—

<summary>Fechner argues that just as individual nerve sensations form a human thought, individual human thoughts form the higher consciousness of the Earth and, ultimately, God. He critiques philosophies that place human consciousness at the very top of the hierarchy, arguing instead for a nested series of ever-higher spiritual unities.</summary>
<keywords>consciousness, retinal fibers, deification of man, hierarchy of being, spirit of the Earth, God, unity of the soul, sleep</keywords>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the hierarchy of consciousness. The previous page ended with a critique of philosophies that view human reason as the ultimate peak of the universe. This page picks up mid-sentence to explain how higher-order consciousness (like that of the Earth or God) remains awake even when its individual parts are "asleep."</meta>

...with one of consciousness and waking; meanwhile, in higher beings, a constant state of waking is based on the fact that, while a certain portion of the members at a lower level sleep, another portion is awake, or that the higher, overarching relationships between them enter into consciousness.

The state of unconsciousness, which separates the periods of waking consciousness of a member at a given level from one another, and which separates the spheres of consciousness of different members, depends physically on the same cause: that the physical activities supporting the mental ones fall below a certain magnitude—a <term>threshold<note>In Fechner's psychophysics, the "threshold" (Schwelle) is the specific level of physical energy required for a stimulus to be perceived by the mind.</note></term>—between those periods or spheres of conscious life; it is only above this threshold that consciousness begins to shine.

This principle is discussed and developed in Chapter 45 of my *Psychophysics*.

The relationship between the soul and the body can be expressed through two formulas. One can say that the soul is the <term>self-appearance<note>original: "Selbsterscheinung." This is a central idea in Fechner's thought: the "inside" view of a being (soul) versus the "outside" view of the same being (body).</note></term> of the same being that appears externally as a body, and that the soul is the connecting principle of the body—of its arrangement and its succession. Both formulas are linked by the fact that in the self-appearance, what spreads out as a manifold variety in the external appearance is pulled together into a unified and simplified form. (For further elaboration, see Chapters 1, 37, and 45 of the *Psychophysics*.)

---

The infinite does not lie beyond finiteness; rather, the <term>finite</term> is the content of the infinite. Experiences within the circle of the finite provide the foundation for an ever-expanding and increasingly certain, yet never exhausted, conclusion regarding the infinite.

<summary>Fechner explains that consciousness exists above a physical 'threshold' and argues that the soul is simply the internal, subjective experience of the physical body. He concludes by stating that the infinite is composed of finite experiences rather than existing apart from them.</summary>

<keywords>consciousness, threshold, psychophysics, soul and body, self-appearance, infinity, finiteness, Gustav Theodor Fechner</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page concludes the philosophical argument regarding the nature of consciousness as the primary reality. While the previous page discussed the physical "threshold" of consciousness and the relationship between the soul and the body, this section argues that all physical concepts—like atoms and laws—are actually abstractions derived from conscious experience. It moves toward a final summary of the author's worldview.</meta>

The most general, certain, and secure experience we can have is that everything that is not consciousness—such as space, time, matter, the atom, or law—is only abstracted within consciousness or from what appears in consciousness. And a highest and final consciousness will therefore be the total encompassing whole, and its unity will be the final knot and the highest peak of all that exists.

Of that which we assume to exist outside or behind consciousness and the contents of consciousness, nothing is immediately known through experience; rather, everything "outside" and "behind" is a hypothesis, which we can only establish if it is necessary.

But if we want to correctly grasp that empirical fact—that one knows and possesses nothing of existence except the consciousness of it—in the sense of the <term>principle of expansion and enhancement<note>Fechner’s "Erweiterungs- und Steigerungsprincip" suggests that the qualities of individual consciousness are expanded and intensified as one moves up the hierarchy toward the universal consciousness of God.</note></term>, then we can do without that hypothesis. We can discard all the dark foundations and grounds of existence outside of consciousness, and trace everything back to the relationships, connections, determinations, laws, realities, and possibilities of consciousness and its content.

XII.
## Final View.

<term>Furthermore, I am of the opinion that Carthage must be destroyed</term><note>original: "Ceterum censeo, Carthaginem esse delendam." This is a famous Roman political slogan by Cato the Elder. Fechner uses it here as a rhetorical device to signal his opposition to a specific school of thought.</note>. By "Carthage," however, I mean that <term>philosophy</term> which places itself above things without having ascended from their foundation to their peak.

->Leipzig, Printed by A. Edelmann.<-

<summary>This page concludes the work by asserting that consciousness is the only certain reality, while physical concepts like atoms and matter are merely abstractions. Fechner rejects philosophies that theorize from the "top down" without an empirical basis in the hierarchy of existence.</summary>
<keywords>consciousness, abstraction, empirical reality, Carthage metaphor, philosophy of nature, Gustav Theodor Fechner, unity of being</keywords>

### XII.

->**Self-praise.**<-

*Furthermore, I am of the opinion that Carthage must be destroyed.* <note>original: "Ceterum censeo, Carthaginem esse delendam." This phrase was famously used by the Roman statesman Cato the Elder to conclude every speech, regardless of the topic, to insist on the total destruction of Rome's rival. Here, the author uses it as a metaphor for overcoming an obstacle.</note> By the Carthage that is to be destroyed, [I mean] that darkness which <unclear>confronts our lips</unclear> here; continue still, as with all such things, to take whatever care is required to be taken.
